\chapter*{Public Derivative Revision Record}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Public Derivative Revision Record}
This derived public revision is based on the 311-page coverage-recovery manuscript dated 3 October 2026. It preserves the scientific findings and their stated limitations while omitting private operational identifiers, account-wide inventories, access correspondence, monetary account details, signature images and student registration data. Private provider exports and original recovery archives are not included. This derivative is not a new institutional certification and does not carry the historical certificate. Historical manuscripts and evidence remain unchanged.

Six original files resolved five named primary-file gaps in the source audit. The public text retains the scientific recovery conclusions and the E14 denominator correction without disclosing the private source inventory. Appendix I explains the publication boundary; the public availability manifest describes what is included and withheld.

The frozen C1 endpoint is 11/64 (17.19\%; Wilson 95\% interval 9.88--28.21\%). Reference errors, material ambiguities and unit mismatches limit its mathematical interpretation. A separate conservative review corroborates three numerical recovery witnesses on the unchanged 64-question denominator. The two counts must be read together; neither is evidence of training improvement.

New material appears in Sections 1.5, 4.6, 7.5, 9.3 and B.13 and Appendix G. Historical V2 results in Section 7.4 remain separate and unpooled. Appendix H is the expanded evidence map. The included case ledger retains classifications, hashes and disagreements. Source descriptions distinguish bundled editable content from evidence retained elsewhere. No frozen source, reference label, selection or primary score was changed. Appendix I reports bounded scientific coverage methods, public-source verification and the remaining limits without private provider inventories. Appendix A corrects historical registry scope and the P11 evidence-tier wording; the reported experimental values are unchanged.

\chapter*{Declaration}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Declaration}
\thispagestyle{plain}

I declare that this project report is my own work. Where the work of others has
been used, it is cited and acknowledged. The matter embodied in this report has
not been submitted, in part or in full, to this or any other university or
institution for the award of any other degree or diploma.

The multi-framework benchmark infrastructure, the baseline framework
integrations, and the initial GRPO/PPO/DPO experimentation were produced by the
Semester-3 project group, of which I was a member, and are inherited here as
the shared foundation. The research direction, the experimental expansion, the
statistical analyses, the P1--P8 internal reports, the reporting and registry
artefacts, the adaptive group-size controller, the applied fraud study, and the
E1--E14 evaluation campaign reported in this document are my
individual contribution, carried out under the guidance of my project guide.

Quantitative results are tied to named evidence. Appendix B distinguishes
recovered primary campaign files, inspectable artefacts and the remaining
limits on underlying payload verification. Incomplete, blocked and negative
results are reported rather than omitted. The boundary between inherited and
individual work is stated precisely in \texttt{platform\_hybrid/sem 4
work/PROVENANCE.md}.

\chapter*{Declaration on the Use of AI Tools}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Declaration on the Use of AI Tools}
\thispagestyle{plain}

The following AI tools were used in this work, as recorded in the project
repository.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Coding and writing assistants.} Anthropic Claude models and
OpenAI Codex were used to write and revise code, analysis scripts and
documents, including drafts of this report. Commits they contributed carry a
\texttt{Co-Authored-By} trailer in the git history.
\item \textbf{Frontier-model synthesis.} ChatGPT Pro Extended and Gemini Deep
Think produced proposed derivations and reasoning recorded in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/frontier\_synthesis\_*.tex}, which those
documents mark as not being experiments. Where this report uses such a result,
it states its own derivation (Chapter 6 \S6.3.3).
\item \textbf{Independent verification.} An LLM verification pass (Kimi,
6 July 2026) reviewed two single-seed results and flagged both as
\textsc{suspect}; both were then re-tested and reported as nulls (Chapter 6
\S6.4.3).
\item \textbf{Evaluation triage.} The Jev (TypeSafe) judgment harness was used
in the E1--E14 ledger only for classification, faithfulness and value
ordering, never for arithmetic (Chapter 4 \S4.5).
\item \textbf{Review.} The pre-defence self-review of 27 September 2026 and the
revision of this report that followed it were AI-assisted.
\item \textbf{Submission preparation.} An AI coding assistant helped revise
reporting language, harden build and test scripts, check stored arithmetic,
and inspect the final PDF. Gemini 3.1 Pro reviewed the build and verification
code through the \texttt{agy} command-line tool. This preparation ran no new
training or benchmark evaluations and changed no stored experimental results.
\end{itemize}

No AI assistant generated experimental data: every reported measurement comes
from an executed run or evaluation recorded in the repository, and simulated
values are labelled as simulations. The research direction,
the experimental design, the choice of claims and their final wording are the
author's responsibility.

\chapter*{Abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Abstract}
\thispagestyle{plain}

This report studies group-relative reinforcement-learning post-training of
large language models under one methodological commitment: a comparative
claim is made only when the whole training stack is held fixed except the
factor under test. It makes four contributions.

\textbf{(1) A recomputable signal-starvation diagnostic and a loss-form
audit.} The Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF) is logged per step and recomputed
from stored reward tensors, with a closed-form Bernoulli null. Reading the
runners' code distinguishes custom REINFORCE group-mean loops from clipped
surrogates, nonzero-KL configuration records and unknown managed settings.

\textbf{(2) Stack-conditioned reporting.} A minimum reporting standard and a
machine-readable stack registry with a \texttt{stackdiff} tool that flags
label-flip risk between two runs from their records alone.

\textbf{(3) Calibrated negative results.} No reliable scaling law is recovered
from reward curves across twelve fitted anchors. A same-stack PPO/GRPO
contrast (0.992 against 0.990, $p = 0.374$, five seeds) and a group-size sweep
find no detectable difference on saturated, in-distribution arithmetic draws;
neither establishes equivalence or an optimum. On an unsaturated GSM8K rerun, GRPO exceeds an
untuned-critic PPO by 5.0 points (paired-$t$ 95\% CI $[1.5, 8.5]$;
$p=0.016$, exact sign-flip $p=0.0625$ at five seeds), a suggestive
configuration-specific result; $G = 2$ and $G = 8$ show no detectable
difference, without establishing equivalence. ZVF has no detectable
association with held-out accuracy in this sample
($\rho = 0.27$, 95\% CI $[-0.37, 0.88]$). An adaptive group-size controller
derived from ZVF reports zero rescue under a plug-in counterfactual model;
92\% of its escalations target observed all-correct groups, without proving
latent certainty or impossibility of recovery with fresh rollouts.

\textbf{(4) An evaluation-governance protocol.} One lightly trained actor
(Qwen3.6-35B-A3B with a LoRA adapter, 40 training steps) is evaluated on
fourteen suites outside its training data under native graders at pinned revisions, with
receipts, a no-pooling rule and a per-lane serving audit that traced a
zero score to a serving defect. Complete scopes include VerilogEval
($129/312 = 41.35\%$), LAB-Bench ($0.2288$) and AgentDojo benign utility
($88/97$); six replacement scopes completed on 27 September 2026
(Chapter~8, Table~8.C); seven original contracts remain externally blocked. A
paired trained-versus-base rerun on 5--151 identical items per lane finds no
significant difference in any lane (inconclusive at those sizes). No
claim of campaign-level improvement over the base model is made.


\textbf{C1 diagnostic extension.} Benchmark reference errors, ambiguous wording and equivalent units limit the interpretation of a new frozen-policy study on public GSM8K train. The first 64 of 87 eligible questions from 4,096 screened questions received 24 fresh responses after eight valid, uncapped parser-zero responses. The frozen endpoint is 11/64 (17.19\%; Wilson 95\% interval 9.88--28.21\%). Assistant-assisted reconciliation corroborates three numerical witnesses on the same denominator, while preserving convention-dependent and ambiguous cases and one wrong-reference match. This is conditional diagnostic evidence, not a population estimate, reasoning validation or training gain. A separate, undeployed parser candidate preserves 2,025 previously valid parse objects and admits four unambiguous extractions in a predefined future subset of 2,048 outputs. It does not alter C1's frozen parser-v2 score.

\vspace{0.25in}
\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} GRPO, RLVR, zero-variance fraction, signal
starvation, stack-conditioned results, benchmark evaluation, reproducibility,
evaluation governance, attribution.

\part{Foundations}


\chapter{Introduction}\label{introduction}

\section{Background: critic-free RL post-training and the group-relative
estimator}\label{background-critic-free-rl-post-training-and-the-group-relative-estimator}

Post-training is now the stage at which a base language model is turned
into something that reliably follows instructions and solves problems.
For several years the dominant recipe was reinforcement learning from
human feedback (RLHF), in which a learned reward model is trained on
human preference comparisons and then used as the reward signal for a
policy-optimisation loop. RLHF works, but it introduces a second learned
model whose errors the policy can exploit, and it makes reward
construction expensive and hard to audit. The alternative that has taken
hold more recently is reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards
(RLVR): instead of a learned reward model, the reward comes from a
programmatic checker --- exact-match against a reference answer for
grade-school mathematics, unit tests for code, a formal or semi-formal
validator for structured output. The checker is deterministic,
transparent, and free to run. What it gives up in generality it gains in
measurability: every reward is a fact about a generated string rather
than a prediction from a model (Figure~\ref{fig:grpoloop}).


\fig{fig_grpo_loop}
{The GRPO training loop}
{The GRPO training loop. A prompt is sampled $G$ times, each completion is scored by a reward function, advantages are formed relative to the group, and the policy is updated. When all $G$ rewards coincide the group-relative advantage collapses to zero and no reward-relative gradient flows, although the rollouts are still paid for. Number line: the Phase-1 P2 batch ZVF of 0.72--0.77 (four methods, 40 steps, 16 prompts $\times$ 8 samples, GSM8K, seed 0, Qwen3.5-4B) and the group-size sweep on Qwen2.5-0.5B two-digit addition over three seeds, where mean ZVF falls from 0.838 at $G=2$ to 0.631 at $G=16$ (a fall of 0.207).}
{fig:grpoloop}



The workhorse optimiser for both settings has been proximal policy
optimisation (PPO), which optimises a clipped surrogate objective
against a value function critic. The critic is a second network, usually
of comparable size to the policy, trained to predict the expected return
of the current state; its predictions supply the baseline that the
advantage estimator subtracts. This works, but it is expensive and
fragile. The critic consumes memory that could hold policy parameters,
it has its own learning rate and its own failure modes, and its errors
propagate directly into the policy gradient as biased advantages.

Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) removes the critic entirely.
Rather than learn a value function, GRPO samples a group of \(G\)
completions for the same prompt, scores all of them with the verifier,
and derives the advantage of each completion from the group's own reward
statistics. Concretely, for a group with rewards \(r_1, \dots, r_G\),
the advantage assigned to completion \(i\) is a within-group
standardisation,

\[A_i = \frac{r_i - \mu_g}{\sigma_g + \varepsilon}, \qquad \mu_g = \frac{1}{G}\sum_j r_j, \qquad \sigma_g^2 = \frac{1}{G}\sum_j (r_j - \mu_g)^2,\]

which in the original GRPO formulation is then used in a clipped,
KL-regularised policy-gradient update. Several inspected custom runners
omit KL regularisation; the custom-loss Tinker reference loop is
REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline and has no ratio or clipping. This
does not describe every run: TRL configuration records include a
reference policy and nonzero KL, while managed or unrecoverable settings
remain unknown. Table 4.2 gives row-wise loss evidence; the campaign
actor's original execution source is not recoverable (Appendix D). The
empirical baseline is the group mean; there is no value network to
train, no value loss to weight, and no critic to keep in sync with the
policy. The trade is a much simpler optimiser for a much smaller
effective batch, since \(G\) samples of the same prompt now carry one
learning signal rather than \(G\) independent ones.

That trade has a sharp consequence, and it is the observation this
project is organised around. Because the advantage is a
\emph{within-group} standardisation, its numerator is a deviation from
the group mean and its denominator is the group's own spread. When every
completion in a group receives the same verifier outcome --- all
correct, or all incorrect --- the group contributes nothing:
\(\sigma_g = 0\), every numerator is zero, and every advantage \(A_i\)
is exactly zero. The group has been fully sampled and fully scored, at
full token cost, and the reward-relative part of the update is
identically zero. In an objective with a KL penalty, that penalty would
still pull the policy toward the reference; for verified zero-KL
group-relative policy surrogates there is no such contribution, although
auxiliary/value losses and optimiser state can still update parameters.
Exact ties imply zero reward-relative advantage; the tolerance-based
diagnostic in §4.3 also counts near-ties and does not assert exactly
zero gradient for them. We call the fraction of a training batch that
falls into this state the \textbf{Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF)}, and it
is the single measurement that most of this report's diagnostics are
built around.

ZVF is not a rare edge case, but its size depends on the regime. The
Phase-1 main configuration (Qwen3.5-4B, GSM8K, 40 steps, \(G = 8\))
records a ZVF of approximately 0.72--0.77, meaning that roughly three
quarters of that rollout batch contributes no reward-relative gradient.
Other measured runs span 0.16 (Qwen3-8B on held-out GSM8K at \(G = 8\))
to 0.84 (Qwen2.5-0.5B arithmetic at \(G = 2\)), so no single value is
typical; the measured values are collected in the canonical-numbers
table (Table 1.1). The fraction responds to the one knob that most
directly controls it: on the Qwen2.5-0.5B arithmetic sweep, mean ZVF
falls from 0.838 at \(G = 2\) to 0.631 at \(G = 16\), a decline of 0.207
across that range. Larger groups buy more contrast, with diminishing
returns that saturate well before the group is small enough to be cheap.

There is a second and less comfortable observation in the background,
and it is about attribution rather than mechanism. ``GRPO'' is not a
single algorithm. It is a label that covers implementations in Tinker,
TRL, SkyRL, verl, OpenRLHF and a growing number of research forks, each
of which chooses its own answer parser, sampling temperature, top-\(p\),
KL coefficient, clip range, LoRA target set, precision and evaluation
harness. Two runs that both say ``GRPO'' in their titles may differ in a
dozen ways that plausibly move the outcome, and the reported headline
number does not distinguish between them. The project's own
stack-sensitivity probe makes this concrete: a nominally matched
configuration (Qwen3-8B, seed 42, \(G = 8\), learning rate
\(1\times10^{-5}\), GSM8K-500, 30 steps, visible hyperparameters held
equal, although the managed Tinker run used the Qwen3-8B-Base checkpoint
and TRL the instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B) was run across four framework
paths, two of which completed and two of which are dry-run placeholders,
with results recorded in
\longtok{experiments/results/framework\_comparison.json}. Four separate
marginal one-way ANOVAs over 42 experiments give \(\eta^2 = 0.546\) for
library or framework, \(\eta^2 = 0.558\) for training algorithm,
\(\eta^2 = 0.471\) for model family and \(\eta^2 = 0.322\) for model
scale --- with the important caveat, stated by the source itself, that
these factors are confounded with one another and with back-end, reward
implementation, hardware and task. Because each value is a marginal
per-factor \(\eta^2\) on a collinear corpus, the four sum to 1.897 and
are not a variance decomposition; none estimates a factor's independent
contribution (Chapter 6 §6.1; source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/\_shared\_methods.tex). The framework
factor is nonetheless not a nuisance parameter to be wished away:
marginally, outcomes vary with it about as strongly as with the
algorithm label.

\section{A Motivating Example}\label{a-motivating-example}

Consider a single training step with a group size of \(G = 8\) on an
easy GSM8K prompt that the current policy already solves. All eight
sampled completions produce the correct final answer, so each receives
reward \(r_i = 1\). The group mean is \(\mu_g = 1\) and the group
standard deviation is \(\sigma_g = 0\), so every advantage \(A_i = 0\).
The compute spent on that group is real --- eight full forward-decode
passes at whatever temperature the sampler is configured with --- and
the group supplies no task-learning signal. It contributes zero
reward-relative surrogate gradient in a verified group-centred
implementation; KL and auxiliary losses, and optimiser state, remain
separate. Now imagine a batch of 16 such prompts in which 12 are already
solved and 4 are not: \(12/16 = 0.75\) of the batch is ``dark'' compute.
What the Semester 4 work adds to this picture is that 0.75 is not a
worst case: it is close to the 0.72--0.77 measured on the Phase-1 main
configuration. It is not a universal value either, since the measured
runs range from 0.16 to 0.84 depending on model, task and \(G\) (Table
1.1), so any claim that GRPO is learning efficiently has to be read
against the ZVF of its own regime (Figure~\ref{fig:confounding}).


\fig{fig_confounding}
{The confounding problem this work addresses}
{The confounding problem this work addresses. A single reported reward figure sits on top of an unstated stack; without fixing and reporting that stack the number is not attributable to the method under test. Top right: of the four frameworks in the nominal configuration only Tinker and TRL were run; veRL and OpenRLHF were not installed in the sandbox and have no measurement. The $17\times$ gap between the two measured stacks is an under-specification exhibit, not a clean back-end-only effect, because the managed stack also pinned a different base checkpoint (Qwen3-8B-Base vs.\ Qwen3-8B). Bottom left: one-way $\eta^2$ shares over 42 reported experiments; they are confounded with one another and with back-end, reward implementation, hardware and task, so they do not sum to one. Bottom right: the PPO-versus-GRPO rows of the Phase-1 table, one run per arm, shown without inferential statistics; the Qwen arms did not share a back-end (GRPO on the managed API, PPO on a Modal H100 cluster), so no directional Qwen algorithm claim is made.}
{fig:confounding}



Now suppose, having noticed this, that one wants to answer the obvious
follow-up question: is the group-relative estimator actually a better
choice than a learned critic? The natural experiment is a matched
comparison --- same model, same training prompts, same verifier, same
step budget, same evaluation, with PPO and GRPO as the only difference.
The project's first attempt at that contrast is recorded in a historical
internal report under the label ``Qwen3-8B'', with a Welch \(t\)-test of
\(p = 0.7605\) computed over the final ten steps of a single run per
arm. Those ten steps are autocorrelated observations of one run, not ten
independent runs, and the project's later methods note re-examined the
row and marks it ``provenance conflict; not estimable''. No effect size,
confidence interval or power figure is therefore quoted for it here, and
the Welch value is carried only as a provenance record (Chapter 6
§6.2.3). The project's own rule is that single-seed Tinker experiments
have ``zero statistical power'' and are ``treated as descriptive only
and are not subject to significance testing''.

Two further observations from the same historical source make the
example sharper rather than cleaner. First, the same table reports the
PPO-versus-GRPO contrast on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct in the opposite
direction; it is also one run per arm, so again no inferential statistic
is quoted, but the direction alone shows that the apparent answer is not
stable across model families. Second, the Qwen arms were not executed on
a common back-end --- the GRPO arm ran through Tinker-managed
infrastructure and the PPO arm on a Modal H100 cluster --- and the
source states this explicitly and declines to draw the algorithmic
conclusion it invites: ``We therefore treat the PPO/GRPO rows as
stack-conditioned evidence, not as an algorithm diagnostic baseline'',
and ``We therefore withhold a directional Qwen algorithm claim''. The
same section records an aggregation gap that illustrates why such care
is warranted: the Qwen PPO last-10 GSM8K figure is 22.5\% in the primary
results ledger but 35.0\% in the statistical summary, and the source
names the ledger value as primary and treats the summary value as
evidence of an aggregation defect rather than as a second measurement.

The genuinely same-stack evidence in the repository is also a null, and
its scope has to be stated with it. The same-stack PPO/GRPO run
(Qwen2.5-0.5B, 40 steps, five seeds, paired by seed but not by
evaluation item) is a two-digit addition task on which both arms are at
ceiling: evaluation accuracy is 0.990 for GRPO and 0.992 for PPO, the
paired difference is −0.002 with p = 0.374, and the 200 evaluation
prompts are fresh random draws from the same generator used in training
rather than a disjoint split; the two arms of a seed share only 3--7 of
their 200 evaluation items, and PPO had drawn about 40\% of its
evaluation items during training against about 7\% for GRPO (§6.2.3;
sources: platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/samestack\_\allowbreak{}ppo\_\allowbreak{}grpo.json;
platform\_hybrid/experiments/modal/modal\_\allowbreak{}samestack\_\allowbreak{}ppo\_\allowbreak{}grpo.py). At
that ceiling a null cannot separate ``no effect'' from ``no headroom'',
so the result is that no difference was detectable on a saturated task,
not that the estimators are equivalent; a paired TOST at a ±0.005 margin
gives p = 0.10 (Table 4.2 gives the exact loss form of both arms). Rerun
on the unsaturated Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT configuration with a disjoint
test split, the contrast is suggestive and baseline-specific: GRPO gains
+4.5 points while a minimal untuned-critic PPO shows no detectable gain,
a post-training gap of +0.050 {[}+0.015, +0.085{]} over five paired
seeds (paired-t p = 0.016; exact sign-flip p = 0.0625) (§6.5.5). The
variant audit (not to be confused with campaign lane E1) takes four
GRPO-family variants (DAPO, GSPO, Dr.~GRPO, AERO) plus the GRPO
reference, runs five arms across eight paired seeds for 40 units at a
frozen stack fingerprint, and evaluates on 500 held-out GSM8K examples
per unit. Every one of the four contrasts is inconclusive. The figures
below are paired-\(t\) 95\% intervals and paired-\(t\) \(p\)-values over
the eight seeds, recomputed from
\longtok{zvf-program/audit/results/full/*.json}: DAPO \(+0.00100\), CI
\([-0.00632, +0.00832]\), \(p = 0.756\); GSPO \(+0.00500\), CI
\([-0.00357, +0.01357]\), \(p = 0.210\); Dr.~GRPO \(-0.00200\), CI
\([-0.01272, +0.00872]\), \(p = 0.673\); AERO \(-0.00075\), CI
\([-0.01029, +0.00879]\), \(p = 0.858\). These paired-\(t\) intervals
replace the audit's own intervals (corrected; see Table E.1). After
Benjamini--Hochberg correction every adjusted \(p\) is at least 0.84.
The pre-audit aggregator's verdict that the differences ``disappear'' is
itself declared invalid, because the locked exact noncentral
paired-\(t\) minimum detectable effect of \(0.010115893\) exceeds the
\(0.010\) margin the study was designed around. The audit's permitted
wording is deliberately narrow: across eight paired seeds no arm
produced a BH-significant held-out difference from GRPO, and none met
the locked exact-power equivalence rule --- which is not the same as
saying the methods are the same, nor that they fail to improve. That
audit runs on Qwen3-8B GSM8K with 500 evaluation examples per unit
(Appendix A, P11).

Taken together, these two halves of the example are the whole motivation
for what follows. The mechanism half shows that a large fraction of the
compute in a GRPO run is not doing what a casual reading of the loss
curve suggests it is doing. The attribution half shows that the most
natural comparative question one would ask about that mechanism --- is
this estimator better than the alternative? --- cannot be answered from
the label ``GRPO'' and ``PPO'' alone, and that the answer on the
project's own evidence is a non-significant contrast that is either
back-end-confounded or measured at a performance ceiling, rather than a
ranking. Neither observation is a defect of the GRPO estimator; both are
defects of what gets measured and what gets reported.

\section{Problem Statement}\label{problem-statement}

The project is organised around four problems, in decreasing order of
how often the literature acknowledges them.

\textbf{Attribution is missing.} Comparative claims about RL
post-training are routinely made across frameworks that differ in
parser, sampler, KL coefficient, clip range, LoRA targets, precision and
evaluator. A reported gain from ``GRPO'' over ``PPO'' is, on most
published evidence, a gain from one complete stack over another complete
stack, with no way to say which component produced it. The project's own
marginal per-factor \(\eta^2\) values put the framework factor at a
magnitude comparable to the algorithm factor, although they are not a
partition of the variance, and its same-stack PPO/GRPO contrast, run on
a saturated 0.5B addition task (evaluation accuracy 0.990 GRPO, 0.992
PPO), returns a paired difference of −0.002 with \(p = 0.374\). The
problem is not that these numbers are surprising; it is that no paper in
this area is obliged to report the information that would let a reader
separate them.

\textbf{Signal starvation is invisible.} Standard training logs report
reward, loss, gradient norm, entropy and completion length. None of
these surfaces the fraction of a batch whose group advantage is exactly
zero. A run can show a rising reward curve and a falling ZVF, or a
rising reward curve with a ZVF pinned near 0.8, and the two look
identical in a loss plot. The fraction of sampling budget buying no
reward-relative gradient is, in the project's own measurements, the
largest measured source of zero-advantage sampling on this fixed setup,
and it is not part of any standard telemetry.

\textbf{There is no reporting or provenance standard.} Even where
authors are willing to describe their stack, there is no
machine-readable form in which to do it, no validator that checks
whether a claim's recorded configuration is internally consistent, and
no accepted minimum set of fields that a stack-conditioned result must
carry. Consequently, results cannot be pooled, cannot be compared across
papers, and cannot be audited after the fact.

\textbf{Evaluation results are rarely reported with their terminal
states.} Benchmark suites in the agentic and reasoning evaluation space
are frequently multi-scope (a public split versus a private original
contract, a benign-utility scope versus an adversarial scope),
quota-limited, or gated behind private payloads. Reports tend either to
omit such suites or to collapse them into a single number, which
conceals how much of the suite was actually graded. The project treats
this as a first-class concern rather than a presentational one: a result
that is genuinely partial should be recorded as partial, with its
coverage denominator and its reopen condition, and a lane that cannot be
run at all should be recorded as externally blocked rather than quietly
dropped.

\section{Objectives}\label{objectives}

The project pursues eight objectives. They are stated as they were
pursued, not retrofitted to the results.

\textbf{O1 --- Build a single harness that can dispatch the same
experiment to multiple back-ends.} Provide one control surface over
Tinker, TRL, SkyRL, verl and OpenRLHF paths so that a configuration can
be held fixed while the framework varies. The harness is the
deliverable; it is not a completed multi-framework benchmark. Only
Tinker and TRL produced completed runs, and those two used different
base checkpoints (Qwen3-8B-Base and Qwen3-8B Instruct), so no framework
comparison is claimed (Chapter 5 §5.2).

\textbf{O2 --- Instrument the training loop so that signal starvation is
directly observable.} Emit per-step telemetry carrying ZVF, gradient
utilisation, policy entropy, completion length and KL divergence, and
persist the raw per-group reward tensors so that any derived statistic
can be deterministically recomputed after the fact rather than trusted
from a log line.

\textbf{O3 --- Measure the zero-variance regime on real reward tensors
and test the levers that might move it.} Characterise ZVF empirically on
genuine GRPO reward tensors, and investigate re-baselining and
curriculum interventions as ways of converting dark compute into
contrast.

\textbf{O4 --- Establish and hold a scientific protocol.} Multi-seed
runs, matched baselines, held-out evaluation with explicit denominators,
deterministic recomputation, declared (not pre-registered) effect-size
and power accounting, and reporting of negatives and nulls --- including
the cases where the answer is that the study was underpowered to answer.

\textbf{O5 --- Scope the most original direction in the field.} A
literature scan for white space, which in this project identified stack
provenance and reporting standards as the unclaimed ground and produced
the minimum reporting standard (P5) and the machine-readable stack
registry (P6).

\textbf{O6 --- Turn the diagnostic into an intervention.} Derive an
adaptive group-size controller from the ZVF measurement and evaluate it
as a training-time policy rather than a post-hoc observation (P7).

\textbf{O7 --- Build and exercise an evaluation-governance protocol on
one trained actor.} Run a single trained checkpoint against fourteen
agentic and reasoning suites that were not part of its training data,
each with its own native evaluator at a pinned revision, under receipt,
budget and decontamination controls, and record every lane's terminal
state. The contribution is the governance protocol and the receipt-bound
record it produces. The campaign is not evidence about GRPO: the actor's
training run was short (40 steps, Chapter 8 §8.1.1), and the only
trained-versus-base comparison is the small-sample Table 8.B.

\textbf{O8 --- Report under a deliberate asymmetry.} Where the evidence
supports a structural or descriptive claim, state it; where it does not,
withhold it. No cross-suite aggregate, no pooling of original-contract
and replacement-scope numbers, and no claim of improvement over the base
model in the E1--E14 campaign. Its paired trained-versus-base comparison
(Chapter 8, Table 8.B) is inconclusive in every lane. The controlled
studies in Chapter 6 separately report pre-to-post gains and the M1b
GRPO--PPO contrast, limited to the tested stack and an untuned critic;
five seeds give an exact sign-flip p-value of 0.0625 despite the paired
t-test p-value of 0.016.

The objectives are what the project set out to build. The results
chapters answer four research questions that the objectives make
testable. Each is mapped to the table that answers it, and §9.3 gives a
one-line answer to each.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{RQ1.} Is the Zero-Variance Fraction recomputable from stored
  reward tensors, and does it carry information about training outcome?
  Answered by the ZVF rows of Table 1.1 and §6.3.
\item
  \textbf{RQ2.} With the training stack held fixed, do algorithm labels
  (PPO vs GRPO, GRPO vs Dr.~GRPO) or group size separate on evaluation
  accuracy? Addressed by the same-stack rows of Table 1.1, the loss-form
  table in §4.3, and §6.2.3, §6.4 and §6.5. The experiments reported
  here can bound this question but not answer it: the PPO/GRPO,
  group-size and arithmetic GRPO/Dr.~GRPO contrasts were all run on a
  saturated task at evaluation accuracy near 0.99, and the two
  unsaturated GRPO/Dr.~GRPO comparisons (§1.2, §6.5.4) are inconclusive
  and establish no equivalence (§9.3). A same-stack rerun on an
  unsaturated task (§6.5.5) suggests a GRPO advantage over a minimal
  untuned-critic PPO, sensitive to the test used, while the G = 2 versus
  G = 8 contrast is inconclusive.
\item
  \textbf{RQ3.} Can a machine-readable stack manifest flag label-flip
  risk between two runs from their records alone? Answered by §7.2
  (registry health and \texttt{stackdiff}).
\item
  \textbf{RQ4.} What does a governance-first evaluation of one trained
  actor yield, and what can it not establish? Answered by Table C.3 and
  Tables 8.A and 8.B.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Contributions.} Measured against these objectives, the thesis
makes four contributions. Everything else in it is supporting evidence
for one of them.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{A recomputable signal-starvation diagnostic and a loss-form
  audit.} ZVF is logged per step and recomputed from stored reward
  tensors against a closed-form Bernoulli null (§4.3, §6.3). Reading the
  runner code distinguishes custom REINFORCE group-mean loops from
  clipped surrogates, nonzero-KL configuration records and unknown
  managed settings (Table 4.2).
\item
  \textbf{Stack-conditioned reporting.} A minimum reporting standard
  (P5) and a machine-readable stack registry with \texttt{stackdiff},
  which flags label-flip risk between two runs from their records alone
  (Chapter 7).
\item
  \textbf{Calibrated negative results.} No reliable scaling law is
  recovered from reward curves; the same-stack PPO/GRPO and group-size
  contrasts are nulls on a saturated task, and on an unsaturated rerun
  only the PPO/GRPO gap appears, from an untuned critic; ZVF has no
  detectable association with held-out accuracy in the measured sample;
  and the ZVF-derived controller's zero-rescue counterfactual is imposed
  by a plug-in model fitted to observed all-equal groups, not a test of
  fresh-rollout recoverability. Each is reported with the power
  limitation that bounds it (Chapters 6--7).
\item
  \textbf{An evaluation-governance protocol.} Native graders at pinned
  revisions, per-lane receipts, a no-pooling rule, a per-lane serving
  audit and a paired same-engine trained-versus-base design, exercised
  on one lightly trained actor across fourteen suites outside its
  training data (Chapter 8). The contribution is the protocol and the
  record it produces, not the actor's scores.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Scope and Non-Goals}\label{scope-and-non-goals}

The C1 extension dated 3 October 2026 adds a frozen-policy diagnostic
whose chief limitation is the reference itself. Exact-match zeros can
reflect an incorrect key, ambiguous wording or equivalent units as well
as a wrong response. Among the first 64 eligible questions from a fixed
4,096-question screen, the frozen score records 11 fresh-response
events; assistant-assisted review corroborates three numerical witnesses
on the same denominator. This extension separates scoring from semantic
support and does not imply reasoning improvement, useful RL gradients or
training gain. Section 7.5 and Appendix G retain the full accounting,
including disagreements. Historical V2 remains separate.

The scope of this report is measurement and diagnostics, not leaderboard
performance. The controlled studies and the campaign actor use
small-to-mid open models --- the Qwen2.5, Qwen3, Qwen3.5 and Qwen3.6
families, from roughly 0.5B parameters up to the 35B sparse
mixture-of-experts configuration used for the campaign actor. The P1
cross-scale roster is wider, 0.6B to 1T total parameters, but its
frontier-scale anchors are single-seed runs through a managed API and
are read descriptively (Chapter 6 §6.1; source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_abstract.tex). Verifiable rewards
come from mathematics and code checkers, principally GSM8K exact-match
and HumanEval-style unit tests. Held-out evaluation uses suites' own
native evaluators at pinned revisions.

Several things are explicitly out of scope. This is not an attempt to
set a state-of-the-art result on any benchmark, and no such claim is
made anywhere in the report. It is not a study of frontier reasoning
models. The evaluation sets used in the controlled comparisons are
small, and their sizes differ by study: 200 fresh prompts for the 0.5B
arithmetic runs, 200 per seed for the 1.5B GSM8K chain-of-thought runs,
500 for the variant audit, and only 8 to 20 in several Phase-1 openings
(the layer-freeze result rests on n = 8). Table 1.1 gives the n behind
each headline number. Claims about novel algorithms are not made: the
length-weighting intervention (P4) and the adaptive controller (P7) were
designed during the work and are reported as designed and simulated
systems with their evaluation limits attached, not as demonstrated
improvements.

The report states negative, null and withdrawn results alongside
positive ones; this is its one general statement about reporting
practice, and later chapters report the results without repeating it.
Three evidentiary rules apply throughout and are worth stating here
because they constrain what the later chapters are allowed to say.
First, every number in this report is traceable to a repository file
that was actually read; where a figure could not be sourced, the finding
is described qualitatively and the figure is attributed to its source
rather than reconstructed. Second, original-contract and
replacement-scope benchmark results are never pooled and never averaged
across suites --- the two are different measurements of different things
under different licences, and treating them as one column would
manufacture a number that no suite reported. Third, no improvement over
a baseline or over a base model is claimed unless the source states it
with a test; where the source reports a difference without a test
supporting it, or reports an inconclusive test, that is what is written
here.

\section{Contribution of the
Candidate}\label{contribution-of-the-candidate}

This report covers work that spans two semesters and two very different
modes of authorship, and the distinction matters for how the
contribution should be assessed. Semester 3 was a group project (Group
6). The multi-framework repository, the baseline integrations, the
initial Tinker, SkyRL and GRPO experiments, the literature foundation,
and the early benchmark, evaluation and reproducibility scaffolding were
produced by that group, of which the candidate was a member. Semester 4
is the candidate's individual continuation, and the new research
direction, the experiment expansion, the analysis, the report writing
and the P1--P8 internal reports are the candidate's solo work (Figure~\ref{fig:contribution}).


\fig{fig_contribution_map}
{Boundary between the inherited Semester-3 group infrastructure and the individual Semester-4 contribution reported in this document}
{Boundary between the inherited Semester-3 group infrastructure and the individual Semester-4 contribution reported in this document. Both columns are drawn from the repository's own provenance record (the Semester-3 tag and boundary commit, the post-boundary commit and file counts, the solo-authorship commit, and the Semester-4 README with its paper-to-evidence map); where the record does not settle a claim, the claim is left out rather than inferred.}
{fig:contribution}



The boundary is recorded precisely rather than loosely. The inherited
state is frozen at the Git tag \texttt{capstone-final-2026-04-25},
corresponding to boundary commit
\longtok{21a99ef766543a1ca86bfbad188445d3596ea73c} of 23 April 2026. At
the time the separation was documented, 584 commits had been made after
that boundary, touching 5,050 changed files --- a figure the provenance
record explicitly declines to present as a measure of academic
contribution, since it counts mechanical and generated changes alongside
substantive ones. The transition to explicit solo authorship is marked
by commit \longtok{282e6b4f0f158de11b3f5bb4656e2d871f68f5c0} of 7 July
2026.

What was added after that boundary is the substance of this report. It
includes the eight unpublished internal research reports: P1, a
cross-library and cross-scale study of GRPO behaviour; P2, the
Zero-Variance Fraction as a descriptive diagnostic; P3, group size,
contrast density and the relationship to DPO; P4, length bias and
held-out generalisation; P5, the minimum reporting standard for
stack-conditioned results; P6, a machine-readable GRPO stack registry;
P7, an adaptive group-size controller derived from the signal-starvation
diagnostic; and P8, a side study comparing a language model against
gradient-boosted trees in credit-card fraud detection. It includes the
post-capstone experimental, audit, synthesis and report iterations ---
206 recorded analysis iterations --- the Berkeley-course-derived
research audits and prototypes, the expanded statistical analyses, and
the new GRPO diagnostics and controllers. And it includes the E1--E14
benchmark campaign, in which a single Tinker-trained actor (base
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at commit \texttt{995ad96e…}, with the
\texttt{campaign-adapter} adapter at commit
\texttt{withheld-adapter-revision…}, in bfloat16) was evaluated against
fourteen suites under receipt, budget and decontamination governance.
Its results are reported per suite in Chapter 8 and never pooled; Table
C.3 is the single source for each lane's scope and terminal state.

Two further contributions deserve naming because they are contributions
of restraint rather than of result. The first is that the campaign's
negative findings were kept rather than discarded. A later
BankerToolBench report records 100 base-model trials with mean reward
0.0. The underlying trajectories remain unavailable, and the earlier
recovered finish ledger predates that rerun. The bridge's missing stop
sequences provide a plausible failure mechanism, but the available
comparison does not identify its contribution to the full aggregate
zero. The finding is a source-limited, confounded diagnostic rather than
a controlled model-capability estimate (Chapter 8 §8.5). The second is
the discipline applied to the project's own claims: the same-stack audit
that invalidated the pre-audit aggregator's ``disappears'' verdict did
so on the strength of the study's own locked minimum detectable effect,
and the permitted wording it left in place is narrower than the finding
it refuted.

The report does not reallocate the shared runtime code. The inherited
repository remains where it was; the Semester 4 internal reports cite
their canonical LaTeX sources and evidence directories rather than
duplicating them, and the distinction recorded here is a distinction
about academic contribution, not about file ownership.

\section{Organisation of the Report}\label{organisation-of-the-report}

The report is organised in four parts.

\textbf{Part I --- Foundations} contains the present chapter, followed
by Chapter 2, the literature survey, which situates the work against the
RLHF and RLVR literature, the critic-free line of estimators, the
group-size and advantage-estimation literature, and the reproducibility
and reporting literature on which the provenance argument depends; and
Chapter 3, the system requirements specification, which states the
functional and non-functional requirements the harness, the telemetry
layer and the campaign infrastructure were built to satisfy.

\textbf{Part II --- Design and Implementation} contains Chapter 4, the
proposed methodology, which sets out the measurement design, the
statistical protocol, the evidence rules and the study-to-evidence
mapping; and Chapter 5, the implementation details, covering the
multi-framework harness, the per-step telemetry and raw tensor
persistence, the campaign launcher and its receipt and budget
governance, and the registry and validator components.

\textbf{Part III --- Results} contains three chapters. Chapter 6 reports
the core GRPO studies --- the cross-library and cross-scale scaling
work, the ZVF diagnostic, the group-size and contrast-density sweep, and
the length-bias and held-out generalisation study, corresponding to P1
through P4. Chapter 7 reports the reporting, registry and controller
work, corresponding to P5 through P7, including the minimum reporting
standard, the machine-readable stack registry, and the simulated
behaviour of the adaptive group-size controller. Chapter 8 reports the
E1--E14 benchmark campaign: the actor, the governance model, the
strictly completed scopes, the original-contract scores, the incomplete
and blocked lanes, the verification results and the campaign's terminal
states.

\textbf{Part IV --- Synthesis} contains Chapter 9, which draws the
cross-study synthesis, states the threats to validity, gives the
conclusions the evidence will bear, and sets out future work and the
reopen conditions under which the blocked lanes could be completed.

Nine appendices follow. Appendix A gives the historical run registry and
its identity reconciliation. Appendix B documents reproducibility and
provenance, including the inherited-versus-individual boundary and the
environment records needed to reproduce the reported results. Appendix C
gives the notation, symbols and abbreviations used throughout, and its
Table C.3 is the single record of each E1--E14 lane's scope, status and
receipt. Appendix D holds the lane-by-lane campaign detail moved out of
Chapter 8. Appendix E lists every correction applied to an internal
source document. Appendix F reports the applied fraud-detection side
study (P8), which uses synthetic data and supervised fine-tuning and so
sits outside the RL contribution. Appendix G records the C1 case ledger,
reference classifications and source bindings. Appendix H is the
evidence map for the preceding scientific chapters and appendices,
arranged section by section. Appendix I records the cross-service
experiment and checkpoint coverage audit, including exact inventory
boundaries and unresolved evidence gaps.
