\chapter{Literature Survey}\label{literature-survey}

This chapter surveys the prior work on which the project is built and
the gap it occupies. It revises the survey of the Phase-1 report,
retaining that survey's seven-part structure and updating each part with
the material the Semester 4 work added: the P1--P8 internal reports and
the E1--E14 evaluation campaign. Material on RLHF, PPO, DPO, GRPO,
DeepSeek-R1, parameter-efficient fine-tuning and evaluation benchmarks
also draws on the Semester-3 Group 6 capstone literature survey
\citep{group6capstone2026survey}, a group work of six authors of whom
the present author was one; where this chapter reuses that survey's
findings they are attributed to it here. Two rules of construction carry
over from Phase 1. The first concerns proportion. The contribution of
this project is overwhelmingly a contribution of measurement and
reporting practice rather than of a new training algorithm, so the
literature that establishes measurement practice receives more room here
than the literature that establishes methods. The second concerns
evidence. A figure is quoted only where a repository source actually
states it; where a source states a finding without giving a number, the
finding is described qualitatively and the chapter says so. Where the
project's own documents state the same quantity differently, the
divergence is reported rather than silently reconciled, and Section 2.5
records one such divergence in full.

\section{From RLHF and PPO to Group-Relative
Methods}\label{from-rlhf-and-ppo-to-group-relative-methods}

Policy-gradient post-training of language models begins with the RLHF
pipeline. Christiano et al. \citep{christiano2017deeprlhf} established
the pattern of reward-model training on human pairwise preferences
followed by reinforcement learning against that reward model, on Atari
and MuJoCo tasks starting from a randomly initialised policy; the
supervised fine-tuning stage that precedes both in the language-model
pipeline was added in later work
\citep{stiennon2020summarize, ouyang2022instructgpt}. Ziegler et al.
\citep{ziegler2019finetuning} adapted the pattern to language,
collecting approximately 60,000 comparisons and, following Jaques et al.
\citep{jaques2017sequencetutor, jaques2019wayoffpolicy}, adopting the
per-token KL penalty against a frozen reference policy that has been
standard ever since. Stiennon et al. \citep{stiennon2020summarize}
scaled the recipe to a 6.7B summarisation model with 64,832 comparisons,
demonstrated transfer to an out-of-distribution summarisation set
(CNN/DailyMail), and produced early empirical evidence of reward
over-optimisation: continued optimisation against a frozen reward model
eventually degrades the true objective. Ouyang et al.
\citep{ouyang2022instructgpt} then built InstructGPT, whose three-stage
pipeline used roughly 40 contractors to produce about 13,000 supervised
fine-tuning prompts, about 33,000 reward-model prompts, and about 31,000
PPO prompts. Two details of that work matter for this project. First,
the reward model was 6B rather than 175B because the larger reward model
proved unstable during training --- a reminder that the reward model is
itself a trained component with its own failure modes. Second, the
preference data were collected as rankings of K ∈ \{4, \ldots, 9\}
responses, which yields C(K, 2) pairwise comparisons from a single
annotation batch; this is precisely the within-prompt comparative
structure that group-relative methods would later exploit directly.
InstructGPT's headline results were that a 1.3B InstructGPT was
preferred to the 175B GPT-3 base model, that the 175B InstructGPT was
preferred to GPT-3 in 85\% of evaluations, that hallucination on a
closed-domain task fell from 41\% to 21\%, that toxicity was reduced by
roughly 25\%, and that TruthfulQA performance roughly doubled;
inter-annotator agreement was approximately 73\%. The same source
records four limitations --- annotation cost, reward-model
over-optimisation, the difficulty of aligning to user intent rather than
to annotator preference, and the residual capability costs of alignment (Figure~\ref{fig:lineage}).


\fig{fig_lineage}
{Algorithmic lineage from RLHF through PPO to GRPO, with DPO as an orthogonal branch}
{Algorithmic lineage from RLHF through PPO to GRPO, with DPO as an orthogonal branch. Each transition removes a component or substitutes a cheaper estimator; GRPO removes the critic and replaces it with a group-relative baseline. DPO (dashed frame) removes the on-policy loop and the reward model rather than simplifying the critic, trading online exploration for stability. Dates are first publication or first use at scale. GRPO variants (DAPO, Dr.~GRPO and others) are discussed in the text and not drawn.}
{fig:lineage}



The optimiser for that pipeline was PPO \citep{schulman2017ppo}. PPO's
clipping mechanism, with ε ≈ 0.2, restricts each update's policy ratio,
and generalised advantage estimation \citep{schulman2016gae} with its
standard defaults (λ = 0.95, γ = 0.99) provides the advantage estimate.
Recasting language generation as an RL problem makes the state the
prompt plus generated prefix, the action the next token, the policy the
language model, and the reward sparse and terminal. The practical
consequence is a four-model architecture --- policy, reference, reward,
and value --- whose mixed-precision memory footprint is roughly four
times that of supervised fine-tuning alone; PPO-ptx mitigates capability
regression by mixing pretraining gradients into the update. Zheng et
al.'s ``Secrets of RLHF'' study \citep{zheng2023secretsrlhf} found that
policy constraints, not algorithmic choice, are the single most critical
factor in whether RLHF succeeds: without them, training exhibits reward
hacking, length explosion, and catastrophic pattern collapse. That
finding is the intellectual ancestor of the zero-variance analysis in
this project, because it locates the failure mode in the constraint
structure rather than in the objective.

Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), introduced with DeepSeekMath
\citep{shao2024deepseekmath}, removes the learned value network
altogether. For each prompt it samples a group of G completions, scores
them with a reward function, and standardises each completion's reward
against the group mean and standard deviation; the resulting z-score
serves as the advantage for every token of that completion. In the
DeepSeekMath configuration G = 64 and β = 0.04. Eliminating the value
model reduces the number of trained and served models from four to
three; the paper motivates this as reducing PPO's memory usage but does
not quantify the saving. DeepSeekMath-RL 7B reached 51.7\% top-1
accuracy on MATH against a 46.8\% supervised fine-tuning baseline,
60.9\% with self-consistency over 64 samples, GSM8K improved from 82.9\%
to 88.2\%, and CMATH improved from 84.6\% to 88.8\% despite no Chinese
data in the RL training set. The paper's iterative variant retains 10\%
of historical data in a replay buffer to prevent catastrophic forgetting
in the co-evolving reward model. RLOO \citep{ahmadian2024backtobasics}
is the closely related leave-one-out estimator that baselines each
sample against the mean of the \emph{other} samples in the group; it
already performs a within-group baseline, and is therefore directly
relevant to the signal-starvation analysis this project conducts.

DeepSeek-R1 \citep{guo2025deepseekr1} then demonstrated that pure
outcome-reward RL can elicit chain-of-thought behaviour from a base
model without supervised cold-start data. R1-Zero was trained on
DeepSeek-V3-Base, a 671B mixture-of-experts model, using rule-based
rewards only and no neural reward model. AIME 2024 pass@1 rose from
15.6\% to 71.0\% over training in the original preprint --- the
\emph{Nature} version cited here reports 77.9\% after extended training
--- with consensus decoding over 64 samples (cons@64) reaching 86.7\%
and MATH-500 reaching 95.9\%. The source states the training
configuration as 16 sampled outputs per question (the group size), a
training batch of 512 outputs (32 questions × 16 samples), a learning
rate of 3 × 10⁻⁶, a KL coefficient of 0.001, and approximately 10,400
policy update steps. The paper documents spontaneous chain-of-thought,
self-verification, and the widely reported ``aha moment'' as emergent
rather than taught behaviours. The full R1 model, trained with a
four-stage pipeline that includes a language-consistency reward, reports
79.8 on AIME, 97.3 on MATH-500, and 71.5 on GPQA, against 79.2 / 96.4 /
75.7 for o1-1217. Distillation into six students from 1.5B to 70B
produced Distill-Qwen-1.5B at 28.9\% AIME and 83.9\% MATH-500, and
Distill-Qwen-32B at 72.6\% and 94.3\%, against 47.0\% and 91.6\% for
Qwen2.5-32B-Zero trained from scratch; the 7B and 16B base models failed
to show meaningful reasoning gains from direct RL.

For this project, the significance of that lineage is narrower than the
headline numbers suggest. GRPO's defining operation is a within-group
standardisation, and a standardisation is undefined when the group's
rewards are all identical. That degenerate case is not an edge case: it
is the modal case early in training on a hard task and late in training
on an easy one. The rest of this chapter and the empirical chapters that
follow are concerned with what that degeneracy does to the training
signal, and with how honestly the field currently reports it.

\section{Direct Preference Optimisation as a
Contrast}\label{direct-preference-optimisation-as-a-contrast}

An orthogonal branch of post-training removes on-policy sampling
entirely. Direct Preference Optimisation (DPO) \citep{rafailov2023dpo}
exploits the fact that the RLHF objective with a KL constraint admits a
closed-form optimal policy, which permits the reward to be
reparameterised in terms of the policy itself. The result is a simple
classification loss over a fixed dataset of preference pairs, with no
reward model, no value model, and no sampling loop. This removes
components of the PPO training stack; no fixed memory or wall-clock
saving is asserted here, because it depends on the implementation and
workload. Four limitations are documented in the same source: the
training data are static and offline, so the implicit reward goes stale
as the policy drifts; there is no online exploration beyond the
dataset's support; the loss can lower the absolute likelihood of
preferred completions while widening their margin over dispreferred ones
(likelihood displacement); and the method has weaker credit assignment
for long-horizon reasoning, where correctness of later steps depends on
earlier ones.

The contrast is structurally instructive for this project. DPO cannot
suffer zero-variance collapse, because it never standardises within a
sampled group --- its contrast is supplied by the dataset rather than
constructed at training time. But it equally cannot exploit a verifier
that is available in the loop, which is the property that makes RLVR
attractive on mathematics. GRPO's zero-variance pathology is therefore
the price of its on-policy, verifier-in-the-loop design, and measuring
that price is exactly what makes the pathology worth quantifying. The
DPO family has since diversified into inverse-preference and non-paired
variants --- IPO \citep{azar2023ipo}, KTO \citep{ethayarajh2024kto},
ORPO \citep{hong2024orpo}, SimPO \citep{meng2024simpo}, and SLiC
\citep{zhao2023slichf} among them --- which the project's cross-library
protocol treats as no-rollout baselines.

The boundary between the two families is also less sharp than it first
appears. Wu et al. \citep{wu2025ittakestwo} argue that GRPO is
``secretly DPO'' --- that its efficacy stems from an implicit
contrastive objective, which makes it structurally related to DPO ---
and report that 2-GRPO retains 97.6\% of the performance of 16-GRPO at
12.5\% of the rollouts (arXiv v3; the earlier v2 reported 98.1\%). That
reduction is the theoretical counterpart of the PCD statistic this
project develops: if a group is exactly as informative as the
correct/incorrect pairs it contains, then the pairwise-contrast density
PCD = ((G − 1)/G) · E{[}p(1 − p){]} is the natural magnitude-aware
summary of a group's usable signal, and the group-size question becomes
a question about how much contrast a given sampling budget buys. The
present project is careful about what it inherits from that argument:
the reduction is cited as prior theory, while the empirical claims
resting on it are scoped to this project's own measurements, reported in
Chapter 6.

\section{Fixing GRPO's Biases and
Collapse}\label{fixing-grpos-biases-and-collapse}

A dense and fast-growing literature targets specific GRPO pathologies.
Dr.~GRPO \citep{liu2025understanding} removes two normalisation terms
from the standard objective --- the per-response 1/L factor and the
question-difficulty amplification --- restoring the sequence-level
gradient up to a global scale, and is motivated as a fix for an
optimisation bias in GRPO that artificially inflates response length,
especially of incorrect responses. DAPO \citep{yu2025dapo}, from
ByteDance, changes four things in one release: asymmetric Clip-Higher
clipping, dynamic sampling that resamples until the batch carries
signal, token-level loss normalisation, and over-long filtering; the
reported result is 50 points on AIME 2024 with a 32B model. GSPO
\citep{zheng2025gspo} (Qwen team, 2025) moves the importance ratio from
the token level to the sequence level and was credited with stabilising
the Qwen3 series, particularly its mixture-of-experts variants.
Production-side variants include VAPO \citep{yue2025vapo}, a value-based
PPO framework for long chain-of-thought reasoning.

A third thread attacks the zero-variance failure mode directly, and this
is the thread the project's own diagnostic sits in. GRESO
\citep{zheng2025greso} pre-screens prompts before sampling so that
uninformative prompts are never paid for. CPPO \citep{lin2025cppo}
prunes completions after rollout, reporting up to 7.98× speedup on
GSM8K. NGRPO \citep{nan2025ngrpo} adds advantage calibration and
asymmetric clipping so that all-incorrect groups still carry gradient.
Scaf-GRPO \citep{zhang2025scafgrpo} injects tiered scaffolding hints
when learning stagnates, reporting a 44.3\% relative improvement on AIME
2024. MO-GRPO \citep{ichihara2025mogrpo} addresses vanilla GRPO's
collapse to a single reward component under multiple objectives through
variance-based reward re-weighting. SSPO \citep{yang2025sspo} operates
at subsentence granularity to balance GSPO's stability against
token-level GRPO's expressiveness, raising GRPO's five-benchmark
mathematics average from 43.01 to 46.72 on Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B. RL-ZVP
\citep{le2025rlzvp} takes the opposite approach to filtering: it
extracts signal \emph{from} zero-variance prompts through entropy-guided
advantage shaping. AERO \citep{zhang2026aero} instead adapts the rollout
allocation and prunes rollouts to avoid zero-advantage dead zones. GVPO
\citep{zhang2025gvpo} re-weights by within-group variance. The tail
continues with λ-GRPO \citep{wang2025lambdagrpo}, MC-GRPO
\citep{kim2026mcgrpo} (median-centred advantage), G²RPO-A
\citep{guo2025g2rpoa}, ConSPO \citep{zhang2026conspo}, and Goldilocks RL
\citep{mahrooghi2026goldilocks}.

One act of bookkeeping in this literature deserves emphasis, because it
is a claim about what may \emph{not} be inferred. AVSPO
\citep{he2026avspo} and other interventions are described in the
project's own related-work source as prior or concurrent interventions,
not as evidence for this project's runner, and the internal report
explicitly claims neither priority nor an intervention effect over them.
The present thesis inherits that disclaimer without qualification.

Against this background, the project's own contribution to the area is
deliberately meagre in algorithmic ambition and deliberately demanding
in evidential standard. The Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF) --- the
fraction of groups whose completions are all correct or all incorrect
--- was measured across libraries, model scales, and training phases in
the companion ZVF report (P2), and that report stopped at description:
ZVF tracks collapse, drifts upward, and is mechanically entangled with
accuracy and group size, so it declined to promote ZVF to a predictive
or causal statistic. A minimal binomial model of group degeneracy was
then proposed, in which the expected ZVF indicator is
\(h_G(p) = p^G + (1-p)^G\) and the usable signal is
\(S = p(1-p)(1-h_G(p))\); three regularities follow in closed form, each
paired with an empirical check drawn from a 368-run Weights \& Biases
audit that counts all logged runs and is therefore a superset of the
benchmark's 70+ core training runs. Because \(h_G(p)\) is symmetric
under \(p \leftrightarrow 1-p\), ZVF assigns the same value to a
mastered prompt and to a hopeless one, producing the U-shape measured
across six model labels in a 368-run audit; and exact-tie ZVF is
sensitive to reward rounding: jitter of order 10⁻⁴ eliminates exact ties
while shifting measured within-group contrast by less than 10⁻⁶. This
does not hold for the declared sample-variance threshold of 10⁻⁶, which
retains 95/600 groups before and after jitter (§6.3.1).
Pairwise-contrast density is proposed as a magnitude-aware candidate
control signal, without this test establishing its superiority over
tolerance-based ZVF, and the toy-scale validation reports a
gradient-norm correlation of corr = +0.71, marked directional only. The
word \emph{controller} in that work names the implemented policy, not an
established improvement: the decisive static-G = 16 matched-token
bakeoff remains unrun.

The one genuinely measured GRPO-variant comparison in the repository is
a Dr.~GRPO versus GRPO control. On Qwen2.5-0.5B two-digit arithmetic at
group size 8, 40 steps, and 5 seeds, both arms were at ceiling and
indistinguishable: GRPO 0.987 ± 0.003 against Dr.~GRPO 0.992 ± 0.003
(mean ± SE over 5 seeds; SDs 0.006 and 0.008), paired difference +0.5
percentage points, t = 1.05, df = 4, p = 0.35; mean completion length
was 4.74 against 4.71 tokens. At that ceiling, and with completions
under five tokens, the task cannot show either an accuracy or a length
difference. In a second regime --- Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct on GSM8K with a
200-token chain-of-thought cap and 3 seeds --- GRPO moved from 20.2\% to
26.3\% and Dr.~GRPO from 20.5\% to 25.5\%; per-seed McNemar tests were
significant in 2 of 3 seeds for GRPO (p = 0.071, 0.036, 0.016) and 1 of
3 for Dr.~GRPO (p = 0.122, 0.017, 0.122), and the pooled p \textless{}
10⁻³ is expressly rejected as overstating significance; with the seed as
the unit, one-sample t-tests on the three per-seed gains give p = 0.005
(GRPO) and p = 0.010 (Dr.~GRPO) (recomputed from
drgrpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}cot.json). Neither arm inflated length over 30 steps
(GRPO 195 → 180 tokens; Dr.~GRPO 194 → 187). The same source contains
head-to-head and rank-correlation tables whose values are
\emph{projections}, not measurements: the GRPO baseline row is a
synthetic baseline produced by a dry run of
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/variance\_\allowbreak{}mitigation\_\allowbreak{}integration.py},
the file states that it makes no measurement claim for any row of that
table, the correlation coefficients (0.78 / 0.66 / 0.63 / 0.31) carry
the same caveat, and the configuration proxies under
\texttt{platform\_local/unified/} are explicitly not faithful
reimplementations. Those numbers are reported here only to mark them as
unusable as evidence. The nearest thing to a headline cross-library
effect in the project is also the one that must be read most carefully:
holding the visible group size, learning rate, data, seed, and label
fixed while changing stacks moved final GSM8K reward from 85.6\% to
5.0\%, a 17× swing; the audit that followed found the managed stack had
also pinned a different base checkpoint (Qwen3-8B-Base on Tinker against
the instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B on TRL), so the source describes this as
an under-specification exhibit rather than a backend-only causal effect.
The complementary telemetry fact is that running ``DAPO'' on an open
Colab trainer whose dynamic sampling demonstrably filters zero-variance
groups produced mean ZVF 0.00, while running ``DAPO'' on a closed
managed stack where only an asymmetric-clip surrogate is user-settable
produced mean ZVF 0.58 --- the same label, opposite telemetry.

\section{Verifiable Rewards, Frameworks and Reporting
Practice}\label{verifiable-rewards-frameworks-and-reporting-practice}

RLVR --- reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards --- was
formalised for open post-training in TÜLU 3 \citep{lambert2024tulu3},
which is the source this project follows in treating a programmatic
checker rather than a learned preference model as the reward. The
implementation landscape the project unifies is crowded and
heterogeneous. On the training side it includes TRL, OpenRLHF
\citep{hu2024openrlhf}, veRL (HybridFlow) \citep{sheng2025hybridflow},
NeMo-RL, DeepSpeed-Chat \citep{yao2023deepspeedchat}, trlX, Axolotl, and
Tinker as a managed back-end; each makes different default choices about
advantage normalisation, KL estimators, tokenisation loss masking,
rollout batching, and importance-sampling granularity, and the project
identifies those differences as the principal source of the
cross-library gap it quantifies. On the inference side, vLLM
\citep{kwon2023vllm} and SGLang \citep{zheng2024sglang} supply the
rollout backends on which modern frameworks depend, with FlashAttention
\citep{dao2022flashattention} and LoRA \citep{hu2021lora} as
load-bearing kernels. Tinker is adopted here through its
\longtok{message\_\allowbreak{}with\_\allowbreak{}tool} interface as the neutral substrate for
tool-use extensions, and the same source explicitly cites tool-use and
agentic RL settings as concurrent environments in which this project's
ZVF measurements will need to be re-evaluated rather than assumed to
transfer.

The evaluation benchmarks are equally well established and equally
awkward. GSM8K \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} comprises 8,500 problems split
7,473 / 1,319, on which a fine-tuned GPT-3 verifier reached 35\% at
release while current systems exceed 95\%; GSM8K-Platinum
\citep{vendrow2025platinum} has since documented label noise in the
original set, which is why the source describes the benchmark as
necessary but not sufficient. MATH \citep{hendrycks2021math} comprises
12,500 problems across seven subjects and five difficulty levels, on
which the original results reached 6.9\%; MATH-500 is the 500-problem
subset introduced with the Lightman process-supervision work
\citep{lightman2023lets}, on which current systems sit at roughly 99\%.
AIME comprises 15 problems per year with integer answers in 0--999;
reported AIME 2024 accuracies include GPT-5 at 95.7\% and Grok 4 at
94.3\%. Because these benchmarks are close to saturation, the source
frames chain-of-thought as the action space that makes them interesting
at all, and identifies process reward models as the bridge from terminal
outcome signals to step-level credit.

Reporting practice is where the project's own contribution is anchored,
and its lineage is explicit. Model Cards \citep{mitchell2019modelcards}
introduced documentation of intended use, out-of-scope use, and
per-slice evaluation results; Datasheets for Datasets
\citep{gebru2021datasheets} extended the idea to datasets, covering
composition, preprocessing, annotation process, and
discriminatory-impact mitigation; Data Statements
\citep{bender2018datastatements} added the speaker-demographic axis; and
Data Cards \citep{pushkarna2022datacards} bridged research and industry
practice with schema, provenance, sampling-distribution, and
audit-transparency fields. A cross-coupling audit at iteration 109 maps
these four works onto the project's legacy 18-field experimental
manifest, showing that they cover 12 distinct fields, that the remaining
six are either RL-specific (per-step ZVF, group-size schedule) or
stack-coverage items with no direct analogue in the prior literature,
and that items 9--12 --- annotation process and annotator demographics
--- have the lowest evidence density in the live manifests. The same
iteration verified all four entries against CrossRef metadata (4/4 pass
on year, five-gram title, and author-family overlap ≥ 0.6) and
retrofitted two entries that had been present in the bibliography but
never cited. The resulting standard, MIN-REPORT-RL, has eight items:
seven run-manifest fields and one evaluation item (held-out pass@k),
which belongs to the evaluation report rather than to run-start stack
identity. The count was aligned at eight on 2026-07-11, and this thesis
uses ``the eight-item standard'' and ``the seven manifest fields'' in
that sense throughout.

The reproducibility literature to which this standard is a deliberate
echo is older and better established. Henderson et al.
\citep{henderson2018deep} showed that deep-RL results depend on
codebase, hyperparameters, and even the choice of random seeds, to the
point that the same algorithm from two repositories behaves as two
algorithms. Colas et al. \citep{colas2019hitchhiker} and Agarwal et al.
\citep{agarwal2021deep} (\texttt{rliable}) supplied the statistical
repair kit --- rigorous comparison protocols and small-sample-aware
aggregate metrics with interquartile-mean reporting and performance
profiles. Jordan et al. \citep{jordan2024benchmarking} argued that
rigorous benchmarking in RL is computationally prohibitive at the scale
it would require, and called for an additional experimentation paradigm
to complement it. Dodge et al. \citep{dodge2019show} proposed reporting
expected validation performance as a function of compute budget; the
project's run manifest is the RL-for-LLM analogue, replacing
\emph{budget} with \emph{stack}. Pineau et al.
\citep{pineau2021reproducibility} formalised the NeurIPS reproducibility
criteria, and the project draws the explicit lesson that self-attested
checklists have limits, which motivates an artifact-computed badge
rather than a declared one. Statistical treatments of evaluation noise
(Miller \citep{miller2024errorbars}) quantify the uncertainty floor
against which the project's own within-noise findings should be read,
and the evaluation-stack sensitivity documented by the LM Evaluation
Harness effort \citep{biderman2024lmeval}, by audits of reasoning
evaluations \citep{hochlehnert2025sober}, and by contamination studies
of the data side of the same coin, together establish that RL
\emph{training} inherits all of this --- the verifier is an evaluator in
the loop --- plus the additional vectors unique to on-policy
optimisation.

Three existing resources come closest to the project's registry and are
the reason it exists in its present form. HELM \citep{liang2022helm}
aggregates evaluation across a large model and scenario matrix, but its
unit is the (model, scenario) cell, not the training stack. The LM
Evaluation Harness \citep{biderman2024lmeval} standardises evaluation by
\emph{executing} implementations, whereas the registry here standardises
by \emph{describing} stacks it cannot execute --- many of them closed
--- which is precisely why the project required a null-versus-absent
convention and an R5 rung that records un-auditability rather than
converting it into a false value. Papers with Code catalogued
paper--code--leaderboard linkage at scale, stopped at the repository
boundary, and was discontinued in July 2025; the source treats its fate
as a governance cautionary tale. The project's own claim to novelty is
narrow and stated negatively: no existing resource provides the executed
loss form at GRPO-variant granularity, per-component implementation
status, or an unauditability marker.

\section{Scaling and Layer-Adaptation
Context}\label{scaling-and-layer-adaptation-context}

Compute-optimal pretraining is governed by well-characterised scaling
laws (Kaplan et al. \citep{kaplan2020scaling}; Hoffmann et al.,
Chinchilla \citep{hoffmann2022chinchilla}), but RL post-training is much
less well characterised, and the sources the project builds on separate
cleanly into those that predict and those that describe. Hilton et al.
\citep{hilton2023rlscaling} derived compute-efficiency frontiers for
single-agent RL across Procgen games, Dota 2 and a toy MNIST-based task.
For RLHF, Gao et al. \citep{gao2023scaling} characterised the
over-optimisation curve of reward models --- the point at which
additional optimisation against a frozen reward model buys a decreasing
true objective --- and Rafailov et al. \citep{rafailov2024rlhfscaling}
extended over-optimisation scaling laws to direct alignment algorithms
(DPO, IPO and SLiC), which have no separate proxy reward model.
Nimmaturi et al. \citep{nimmaturi2025predictive} derived an empirical
scaling law specifically for GRPO, identifying three training phases
(slow start, rapid improvement, plateau) and reporting that most of the
benefit is exhausted by roughly 80\% of a single epoch. Tan et al.
\citep{tan2025scalingrl} treat group size as a budget knob in an
empirical scaling study of GRPO post-training, in an appendix analysis
showing that the optimal rollout size shifts with the training budget.
Snell et al. \citep{snell2024testtime} treat inference-time compute as a
scaling axis in its own right, and the project's own related-work source
cites these as the works against which its measurements should be read.

The project's scaling study covers 70+ runs across seven RL libraries
and five model families. This thesis uses one model range throughout:
0.6B--1T, with the anchors above 235B single-seed and descriptive. The
rule is that the range spans every model with at least one completed run
in the P1 roster. The source documents state narrower ranges because
each covers a subset: 0.6B--\textasciitilde671B in the P2, P3 and P4
introductions, and 0.6B--235B for the multi-family data in the
related-work source. The principal empirical result of the scaling work
is a null: across that range, no reliable scaling law is recovered from
reward curves. The companion group-size study adds a second bounded
negative: a three-seed sweep does not establish an optimum, since the
measured arms are statistically indistinguishable, and the measured
signal-to-noise ratio grows at only about 52\% of the √G ideal, with G ∈
\{2, 4, 8, 16\} measured and G ∈ \{4, 8, 16, 32, 64\} explicitly
reconstructed rather than run.

Layer adaptation supplies the second half of this section's context, and
connects the project's white-box investigation to the
parameter-efficient fine-tuning literature. LoRA \citep{hu2021lora}
freezes the pretrained weights and injects trainable low-rank matrices
into the attention projections, so that a d × d projection at d = 4096
with rank r = 16 trains 2dr rather than d² parameters, a 128× reduction
(this chapter's arithmetic; the paper itself reports up to 10,000× fewer
trainable parameters for GPT-3 175B). QLoRA \citep{dettmers2023qlora}
extends this by quantising the frozen base to NF4 and adding double
quantisation, which saves about 0.37 bits per parameter ---
approximately 3 GB for a 65B model --- and permits 65B fine-tuning on a
single 48 GB GPU. The motivating arithmetic is that Adam at mixed
precision costs roughly 16N bytes, exceeding 100 GB for a 7B model.
Prefix tuning, prompt tuning, and adapter layers are the earlier members
of the same family, and LoRA's advantages for RL specifically are that
the frozen base doubles as the reference policy and that the trainable
parameter count is small enough for modest hardware. The project's
layer-adaptation pillar asks a narrower and orthogonal question: where
inside the network GRPO-style adaptation concentrates, and whether the
layers that will matter can be identified early enough to freeze the
rest. The reported finding is moderate concentration of per-layer
adaptation, with the important negative that step-1 predictability does
\emph{not} survive scaling. This connects directly to Mukherjee et al.
\citep{mukherjee2025rlsubnetworks}, who found that RL fine-tuning
updates only 5--30\% of model parameters across seven algorithms and ten
model families, suggesting that RL activates a sparse subnetwork. The
length-bias pillar completes the scaling picture with its own bounded
null: under a 200-token GSM8K generation cap, GRPO and Dr.~GRPO produce
held-out gains that are indistinguishable at the project's seed budget,
and neither arm shows length inflation; the source is careful to state
that the 200-token cap is part of the estimand rather than a nuisance
detail, so the null bounds the capped, short-horizon regime and cannot
adjudicate uncapped reasoning or long agent trajectories.

\section{Process-, Tree- and Stability-Aware
Variants}\label{process--tree--and-stability-aware-variants}

Beyond the group-baseline fixes, a parallel line of work changes
\emph{what} signal drives the update. Process reward models supply
denser, step-level feedback instead of a single terminal reward, which
reduces the chance that a whole group collapses to identical terminal
rewards. Let's Verify Step by Step \citep{lightman2023lets} showed that
step-level PRMs substantially outperform outcome reward models on MATH;
Math-Shepherd \citep{wang2024mathshepherd} derives process rewards from
automatically generated process annotations, avoiding costly human
labels; PRM800K \citep{lightman2023lets} and PRMBench
\citep{song2025prmbench} supply step-level evaluation data; OmegaPRM
\citep{luo2024omegaprm} uses Monte-Carlo tree search to supervise
process rewards without human-in-the-loop; ReasonEval
\citep{xia2025reasoneval} scores reasoning steps for validity and
redundancy with trained LLM evaluators; and Implicit PRM
\citep{yuan2024implicitprm} learns process rewards implicitly from
outcome labels, with VersaPRM \citep{zeng2025versaprm} extending process
rewards to multi-domain tasks. Tree-of-thought search
\citep{yao2023tot}, self-consistency \citep{wang2023selfconsistency},
and critic-style post-hoc verifiers all either benefit from or
substitute for process supervision. The same source documents the
counter-current that matters most here: ``A Sober Look''
\citep{hochlehnert2025sober} warns that reward hacking and evaluation
artefacts can inflate perceived gains on reasoning benchmarks, which is
the project's stated motivation for insisting on binary verifiable
rewards in its own mathematics work.

Stability-aware variants constrain the update itself. GSPO's
sequence-level importance ratio, discussed above, is the clearest
instance, credited with stabilising the Qwen3 mixture-of-experts series.
SSPO's subsentence-granularity objective is explicitly framed as
balancing GSPO's stability against token-level GRPO's expressiveness.
Entropy-guided advantage shaping in RL-ZVP regulates the update when the
group carries no reward contrast, and GRESO's pre-screening is a
sampling-side stability control. On the reasoning-RL side, GRPO-Lead
\citep{zhang2025grpolead} and LCPO \citep{aggarwal2025l1} tune reasoning
length through length-aware rewards, LIMR \citep{li2025limr} selects a
small high-impact subset of RL training data, Search-R1
\citep{jin2025searchr1} trains reasoning agents to interleave search
with chain-of-thought, and TTRL \citep{zuo2025ttrl} performs test-time
RL on unlabelled data using majority-vote rewards.

The Phase-1 survey's judgement on this body of work is retained without
amendment, because the Semester 4 evidence has not changed it. These
methods propose \emph{levers}; this project first builds the
\emph{instrumentation} needed to tell whether any such lever converts
wasted rollouts into held-out accuracy --- ZVF, group utilisation,
pairwise-contrast density, and the collapse decomposition. The nulls
reported in the results chapters are exactly the kind of evidence that
instrumentation is designed to produce, and they constitute a standing
caution against adopting a variant on the strength of a single-seed
improvement. The project applies that caution to its own proposals: the
adaptive group-size controller is described as an implemented policy
rather than an established improvement, and its decisive matched-token
comparison remains unrun.

of what is claimed and, more importantly, what is not. Table 2.1
reproduces the Phase-1 positioning and updates it with the Semester 4
evidence (Figure~\ref{fig:questions}).

The two crowded pillars are P2 and P4, where the literature is dense and
the project accordingly narrows its claim to measurement rather than to
a new algorithm. The widest open ground remains the systems and
reporting side, where no GRPO-specific machine-readable reporting
standard exists --- the gap Section 2.4 establishes from three
directions: HELM's unit is the (model, scenario) cell rather than the
training stack, the LM Evaluation Harness standardises evaluation by
executing code while the registry must describe stacks it cannot
execute, and Papers with Code stopped at the repository boundary and was
discontinued in July 2025.

The Semester 4 evaluation campaign extends this positioning from method
comparison to benchmark practice. Rather than adding one more
leaderboard number, it evaluates a single trained actor against fourteen
agentic and reasoning suites outside its training data, each through its
own native grader at a pinned revision, under receipt, budget and
decontamination controls, and it reports every lane's terminal state
rather than a cross-suite aggregate. Its results are in Chapter 8; Table
C.3 gives each lane's scope and status. One finding bears directly on
the benchmark-practice literature: E4's mean reward of 0.0 across 100
base-model trials was caused not by the model but by a serving bridge
that passed no stop sequences (Section 8.5), which is exactly the kind
of unreported stack detail this positioning argues must be recorded.

Two gaps in this survey should be stated plainly, since the chapter's
purpose is to locate the work rather than to flatter it. First, the
primary papers for the fourteen evaluation suites are not surveyed here
at the depth the algorithmic literature receives; the suites are
discussed as instruments, sourced to the campaign's own results
document, and their native protocols are documented in Chapter 8 and its
receipt trail rather than in this chapter. Second, the survey's
treatment of tool-use and agentic RL is inherited from the project's own
related-work source and is explicitly flagged there as a concurrent
setting in which the ZVF measurements will need to be re-evaluated
rather than assumed to transfer. What the chapter does establish is that
the project's contribution is a measurement and reporting contribution,
that the literature it sits in is dense enough that claim-narrowing
rather than claim-extension is the appropriate move, and that the
specific gap the portfolio occupies --- machine-readable, per-component,
executed-loss-form disclosure for group-relative RL runs --- is not
filled by any of the resources surveyed above.



\chapter{System Requirements
Specification}\label{system-requirements-specification}

This chapter states what the Tinker RL Lab system must do, what
qualities it must hold while doing it, and how each requirement is
verified. It inherits the specification of the Phase-1 report and
extends it across the Semester-4 additions: the multi-framework
benchmark rosters, the Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF) diagnostic and the
adaptive group-size controller derived from it, the minimum reporting
standard and the machine-readable GRPO stack registry, and the E1--E14
evaluation campaign with its fail-closed completion gate. It is written
as a specification rather than as a retrospective description, but every
requirement below corresponds to a component that exists in the
repository, and every functional requirement is paired with the concrete
test that checks it. Where a requirement is not satisfied --- the cloud
capacity for two evaluation lanes, the white-box gradient path at the
largest model scale --- the shortfall is named in §3.6 and §3.8 rather
than written around.

\section{Overview and Actors}\label{overview-and-actors}

The central object of the Phase-1 specification was the \textbf{run}:
one configuration, one seed, one framework, one training budget,
producing per-step telemetry and a persisted set of raw group reward
tensors. Semester 4 introduces two further first-class objects. The
first is the \textbf{lane}: a single benchmark suite evaluated under a
named scope --- an original contract with the suite's owner, or a
declared replacement scope --- carrying its own provider, payload,
licence, quota and terminal state. The second is the \textbf{receipt}:
an immutable, hash-bound record that a declared step actually happened,
against declared inputs, at a declared revision. Requirements that
concern runs, lanes and receipts are distinguished below where the
distinction matters.

Four actors interact with the system, and the fourth is new in Semester
4.

\textbf{The experimenter} declares work. Under the original design this
meant declaring a run through a single configuration object and nothing
else: the model, the dataset, the reward function, the decoding
parameters (temperature, top-\(p\), maximum tokens), the group size
\(G\), the learning rate, the KL coefficient \(\beta\) and the seed all
live in one file, and the framework is a switch rather than a rewrite.
Under the campaign design the same actor declares a lane through a
sealed resource request: the lane specification fixes an immutable task
bundle, a pinned native runtime, a native grader, a budget and an
applicable licence in advance, and those declared inputs are then
checked for internal consistency before any launch is permitted.

\textbf{The back-end} executes the training loop and, under the managed
path, is the Tinker service; under the white-box path it is a local or
rented GPU. For evaluation, the corresponding actor is the
\textbf{provider grader}: the benchmark owner's own native evaluator at
a pinned revision. The system is permitted to record a suite score only
when that evaluator produced it, or when a declared replacement harness
reproduces it under a scope that is labelled as a replacement.

\textbf{The auditor} is a downstream script or an independent reader who
recomputes published diagnostics from stored raw artefacts. In Semester
4 this role acquires force: the audit suite of §3.4 is executed as a
gate with a non-zero exit status rather than as advisory tooling, and
the completion gate for the campaign lanes is fail-closed by
construction, in that a missing or malformed declaration stops the
procedure instead of degrading it.

\textbf{The campaign operator} holds launch authority for paid work.
Spend on any lane requires a bound authorization receipt issued by the
operator; the system will not mint one for itself, and lanes are
recorded as blocked when the receipt or the technical chain behind it is
absent.

A fifth party, the \textbf{external access holder}, is an actor only in
the negative sense: several lanes depend on a private payload, a hosted
evaluation service or a quota grant that only the provider can issue.
The specification requires that such a lane be recorded with an explicit
terminal state and a named reopen condition rather than silently
dropped, which is why the campaign's terminal-state vocabulary exists as
a first-class output.

\section{Functional Requirements}\label{functional-requirements}

Table 3.1 lists the functional requirements. Each row states the

and OpenRLHF entries are dry-run placeholders &

FR-3 & Every step computes ZVF and its all-correct / all-wrong
decomposition from the reward tensors & Phase-1 report \\
FR-4 & Every step emits a structured telemetry record (reward, ZVF,
gradient utilisation, collapse counts, entropy, completion length, KL)
to a log and to the tracker; the controller work adds a per-step
counterfactual against a matched fixed-G control & Phase-1 report;
\longtok{controller\_\allowbreak{}cf\_\allowbreak{}per\_\allowbreak{}step.tsv} \\
FR-5 & Raw per-group reward tensors are persisted for every run, so
diagnostics can be recomputed & Phase-1 report \\
FR-6 & Sweeps over G, seed and baseline-versus-intervention arms run
under a fixed wall-clock or token budget & Phase-1 report \\
FR-7 & For single-GPU models, per-layer LoRA gradient norms are
recorded; not required at managed scale, where they are not exposed &
Phase-1 report \\
FR-8 & Every run emits a provenance record binding configuration, grader
version and rollout hashes; a campaign lane additionally declares its
provider package and grant documents against published schema versions,
each with a 64-character hash & Phase-1 report;
\longtok{e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}completion\_\allowbreak{}gate.py} \\
FR-9 & A stack-conditioned result carries the seven manifest fields of
the eight-item standard (loss form, reference KL, sampler/backend with
base-checkpoint revision and hash, telemetry, group-size schedule,
held-out split, decontamination) plus its eighth, evaluation item
(held-out pass@k); a 0--100 badge is emitted, and an unreported field is
distinguished from one reported as absent &
\longtok{paper\_\allowbreak{}P6\_\allowbreak{}registry.tex};
\longtok{minreport\_\allowbreak{}audit\_\allowbreak{}summary.json} \\
FR-10 & The stack registry is queryable by stack, field or status &
\longtok{registry/schema.json}; \longtok{registry/query.py} \\
FR-11 & Each named variant stores an explicit delta against the GRPO
reference, and \longtok{stackdiff} returns an R0--R5 flip-risk verdict
for same-label entries & \longtok{paper\_\allowbreak{}P6\_\allowbreak{}registry.tex};
\longtok{registry/provenance/} \\
FR-12 & An adaptive group-size controller with escalation asymmetry and
hysteresis records its counterfactual against the best static recipe at
matched and unequal rollout counts; it measures the trade and does not
claim a win & \longtok{paper\_\allowbreak{}P7\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}controller.tex};
\longtok{controller\_\allowbreak{}cf\_\allowbreak{}summary.json} \\
FR-13 & Each lane records results against a declared evidence class
(exact complete, partial exact, partial recovery, externally blocked,
local-setup-ready-provider-input-required) tied to the source status &
\longtok{e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}completion\_\allowbreak{}gate.py} \\
FR-14 & The completion gate validates, and never executes, a lane
package; a valid package proves only that its inputs are consistent and
available; signatures are checked against provider trust roots, and the
gate refuses rather than warns &
\longtok{e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}completion\_\allowbreak{}gate.py} \\
FR-15 & Original-contract and replacement-scope results are never
pooled, no cross-suite aggregate is computed, and every accuracy carries
its denominator & \longtok{E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md} \\
FR-16 & The export path builds an anonymised review package whose build
runs the audit suite, and refuses a package built with audits skipped &
\longtok{run\_\allowbreak{}all\_\allowbreak{}audits.py}; \longtok{export\_\allowbreak{}guard\_\allowbreak{}audit.py} \\

\section{Test-Case Specification}\label{test-case-specification}

The test cases below are the concrete checks attached to the
requirements. TC-1 through TC-5 are inherited from the Phase-1
specification and are restated here because later chapters depend on
them; TC-6 onward are the Semester-4 additions, and TC-6 is the suite
that carries the largest share of the functional requirements.

\textbf{TC-1 --- Recompute ZVF and the collapse decomposition from
stored tensors.} Recompute the published ZVF and collapse figures from
the persisted per-group reward tensors of the main configuration, using
the documented analysis script. The published values are a ZVF of
approximately 0.72--0.77 and an all-correct fraction of 0.65--0.71;
recomputation must match. Verifies FR-3 and FR-5.

\textbf{TC-2 --- Recompute the detector metric from raw tensors.}
Recompute the Phase-3 detector's area under the ROC curve from raw
tensors under five-fold stratified cross-validation. The published
figure is an AUROC of \(0.84 \pm 0.01\) against \(0.43\) for the
reward-only comparator. Verifies FR-5 and NFR-3.

\textbf{TC-3 --- Re-run a matched-budget arm comparison.} Re-run the
baseline and the curriculum arm at a matched token budget over three
seeds; the Phase-1 result is that both arms average \(+0.028\) on the
held-out set with no curriculum advantage. Verifies FR-1, FR-2 and FR-6.

\textbf{TC-4 --- Recompute headline diagnostics.} Recompute each
headline diagnostic quoted in a results chapter directly from stored
artefacts, with the recomputation recorded next to the published value.
Verifies NFR-3.

\textbf{TC-5 --- Provenance verification.} Verify a run's provenance
record end to end, from configuration to rollout hashes to the grader
version. Verifies FR-8.

\textbf{TC-6 --- Execute the submission audit suite as a gate.} Run
\texttt{python\ platform\_\allowbreak{}local/\allowbreak{}run\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}all\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}audits.py}. The suite executes
nine audits --- \texttt{claim\_issues}, \texttt{sync\_issues},
\texttt{anon\_issues}, \texttt{strength\_issues},
\texttt{package\_issues}, \texttt{workflow\_issues},
\longtok{export\_\allowbreak{}guard\_\allowbreak{}issues}, \texttt{caveat\_issues} and
\texttt{scientific\_issues} --- emits one
\texttt{METRIC\ \textless{}name\textgreater{}=\allowbreak{}\textless{}count\textgreater{}}
line per audit, and returns exit status 0 only when every audit reports
zero issues. Individual checks that this test enforces include the
presence of scope and caveat language in the review documents
(\longtok{readme\_\allowbreak{}missing\_\allowbreak{}key\_\allowbreak{}results\_\allowbreak{}scope\_\allowbreak{}header},
\longtok{readme\_\allowbreak{}missing\_\allowbreak{}heldout\_\allowbreak{}language},
\longtok{checklist\_\allowbreak{}missing\_\allowbreak{}preliminary\_\allowbreak{}key\_\allowbreak{}results\_\allowbreak{}label}), the
absence of overstated checkpoint claims
(\longtok{misleading\_\allowbreak{}checkpoint\_\allowbreak{}availability\_\allowbreak{}claim},
\longtok{submission\_\allowbreak{}overstates\_\allowbreak{}checkpoint\_\allowbreak{}release}), the presence of
every reviewer caveat (\texttt{heldout\_scope},
\longtok{tool\_\allowbreak{}eval\_\allowbreak{}protocol}, \texttt{codegen\_subset},
\texttt{capacity\_confound}, \texttt{moe\_diagnostics},
\longtok{budget\_\allowbreak{}and\_\allowbreak{}splits}, \texttt{replication\_release}), the
absence of banned marketing formulations, the survival of the technical
caveats into the anonymous build
(\longtok{anonymous\_missing:\textless{}label\textgreater{}} for the
seven needles), and the presence of the audit call and its skip flag in
the export script. Verifies FR-16, NFR-4 and NFR-7.

\textbf{TC-7 --- Structural checks on the evaluation script and its
result files.} For each held-out evaluation script, assert by parsing
the source --- not by reading its documentation --- that it exposes a
seed argument, locks the dataset split to the test split, samples with a
non-zero temperature, consumes a checkpoint path, and writes the
declared metadata keys including the split, the sampling flag, the seed
and the model source. For each result file, assert that the schema
version is 2, that the evaluation status is one of completed or failed,
that the required configuration and summary fields are present, that the
recorded split is the test split, that the attempted count equals the
sum of correct and incorrect, and that a failed evaluation carries a
failure reason and a completed evaluation carries non-zero attempts.
Verifies FR-8 and NFR-4.

\textbf{TC-8 --- Build the report from source.} Compile the main report
and its supplementary through the full LaTeX chain and flag an empty
journal field in the bibliography. Verifies FR-16.

\textbf{TC-9 --- Dry-run the completion gate against a declared lane.}
Present a provider package to the gate and require it to validate the
declaration without executing anything: the expected outcomes are
acceptance with the declared evidence class, or a specific refusal
naming the missing element. A package must never be reported as a
benchmark result by this procedure. Verifies FR-13, FR-14 and NFR-7.

\textbf{TC-10 --- Registry query and null discipline.} Query the
registry for a labelled entry and its delta record, and confirm that a
field absent from the source is returned as unreported rather than as a
zero value, and that a same-label pair with divergent behaviour triggers
the flip-risk verdict. Verifies FR-9, FR-10, FR-11 and NFR-10.

\section{Software Requirements}\label{software-requirements}

The runtime is Python 3.11 with PyTorch and the Hugging Face stack
(Transformers, PEFT, Datasets) for model handling, tokenisation and
adapters; the RL back-ends are TRL, veRL through its HybridFlow path,
and OpenRLHF, driven through the shared launcher, with the Tinker client
SDK for the managed path. Experiment tracking is a weights-and-biases
project; telemetry is stored as JSONL and as persisted tensors; analysis
is carried out by the documented scripts named in the Phase-1
specification.

Semester 4 adds five software components to the requirement set. First,
the audit suite: \longtok{platform\_local/run\_\allowbreak{}all\_\allowbreak{}audits.py} together
with the nine audit modules it imports and the shared data model in
\texttt{utils/audit\_utils.py}, which defines the issue, result and
suite types and the \texttt{METRIC} line format; the suite requires a
Python interpreter with no third-party dependencies beyond the standard
library for its structural checks. Second, the completion gate and the
per-lane harnesses under \texttt{zvf-program/}, which require Ed25519
signature verification and JSON Schema validation against the published
provider-package, provider-grant and trust-root schema versions. Third,
the registry: a schema, an entry store and a query interface, together
with the provenance records under \texttt{platform\_hybrid/registry/}.
Fourth, the report toolchain: a LaTeX distribution capable of the
multi-pass build with BibTeX, plus a markdown assembler that
concatenates the chapter files and, when available, renders the document
through pandoc. Fifth, the judgement-layer harness under
\texttt{zvf-program/jev\_lab/}, which records its own receipts and is
used only for classification, faithfulness checking and value ordering.

\section{Constraints and Assumptions}\label{constraints-and-assumptions}

\textbf{Rewards are binary and verifiable.} The specification assumes a
programmatic checker --- exact match for the mathematics tasks and unit
tests for the code subset. Nothing in the requirement set addresses
learned reward models, and none of the diagnostics should be read as
applying to them.

\textbf{Held-out sets are small.} Several Phase-1 evaluation sets have
\(n\) between 8 and 20, and the controlled Semester-4 comparisons use
200 to 500 items (Table 1.1 gives the n behind each headline), with the
consequence that learning-gain magnitudes are noise-limited by
construction. This is a constraint on what the results can mean, not a
defect of the analysis: the small-\(n\) figures are reported with their
denominators and are not used to support comparative claims.

\textbf{The managed back-end is a black box at the layer level.}
Per-layer gradients are not exposed on the managed path, so claims about
the relationship between reward spread and gradient magnitude are
confined to the small-model white-box configuration and are directional
only.

\textbf{Group-size differences must not be attributed to sampling
noise.} The comparison protocols share a deterministic prompt schedule,
so that method-to-method differences in ZVF reflect training dynamics
rather than independent draws of prompts.

\textbf{Framework parity is incomplete.} The requirement in FR-2 covers
two seven-library rosters, but only two of the seven cross-launcher
entries produced completed runs in this release, and the remaining
comparison entries are dry-run placeholders. Comparative framework
claims are therefore constrained to the rosters and the completed runs
actually recorded, and the repository states the dry-run status
explicitly.

\textbf{The scale study reports descriptive limits rather than a scaling
law.} The cross-scale analysis spans more than seventy runs across seven
libraries and five model families, and its central result is a negative
one: no reliable positive cross-scale slope is recovered and no
saturation exponent is identifiable. The specification inherits that
boundary --- the requirement is to report identifiability limits, not to
assert a law.

\textbf{External dependencies are assumed unavailable until proven
otherwise.} Payload grants, hosted evaluations and quota increases are
treated as absent until a response arrives; a lane without the required
external benchmark scope is closed as externally blocked with a reopen
condition rather than left pending indefinitely.

\textbf{Arithmetic and string lookups stay in code.} Whatever judgement
layer is used for triage, classification or value ordering,
verification-type checks are performed by deterministic code. This rule
was banked after the judgement layer returned an incorrect answer to a
parity probe in a healthy window, and it constrains the architecture: no
requirement in this chapter may be verified by a language model where a
deterministic check is possible.

\textbf{Named variants are labels, not specifications.} The assumption
that a method name identifies a method is false on the available
evidence: entries sharing a label diverge substantially in measured
behaviour, which is why the registry's delta records and flip-risk
verdicts are specified as requirements rather than as documentation
niceties.



\part{Design and Implementation}


\chapter{Proposed Methodology}\label{proposed-methodology}

This chapter sets out the methodology of the work rather than its
results. It states four things in order: the design commitment to
attribution and the four de-confound pillars that operationalise it
(§4.1); the per-step telemetry that makes a run's internal state
observable at the granularity at which the mechanism acts (§4.2); the
formalisation of the Zero-Variance Fraction as a descriptive diagnostic
rather than a metric (§4.3); the eight-study portfolio P1--P8 and the
question each study asks (§4.4); and the design of the E1--E14
evaluation campaign, including its single trained actor, pinned native
evaluators, frozen sampling, receipt model, terminal-state taxonomy and
decontamination handling (§4.5). Throughout, the reporting discipline is
part of the method: figures are tied to named evidence, unavailable
primary artefacts are distinguished from surviving summaries (Appendix B
§B.11), and lanes without an admissible result are recorded as blocked (Figure~\ref{fig:pillars}).


\fig{fig_four_pillars}
{The four de-confound pillars}
{The four de-confound pillars. Each holds the whole stack fixed and varies exactly one factor. In Pillar~2 all nine rows of the method panel, vanilla GRPO included, are a declared simulation projection (Chapter~6).}
{fig:pillars}



\section{The Design Commitment to
Attribution}\label{the-design-commitment-to-attribution}

The premise of the project is that an RL-for-LLM result is not
identified by the name of its algorithm. Two runs both labelled GRPO may
differ in loss form, reference-policy handling, sampler, precision,
base-checkpoint identity, data order and group-size schedule, and the
label alone does not say which of those differences produced the
outcome. The project's response is a design commitment rather than a
post-hoc covariate adjustment: hold the whole experimental stack fixed
and vary exactly one factor at a time, so that an observed difference is
attributable to the factor that was varied rather than to the several
things that changed alongside it. This commitment is the methodological
spine of the whole thesis, and it has a practical corollary that shapes
every experiment reported later: a study is defined by the single axis
it varies and is only interpretable to the extent that every other axis
was genuinely pinned (Figure~\ref{fig:attribution}).


\fig{fig_attribution}
{The attribution design}
{The attribution design. Exactly one factor of the training stack is varied while the remainder is held fixed, so a measured difference cannot be attributed to an unstated framework difference.}
{fig:attribution}



The commitment is implemented in a unified launcher,
\longtok{platform\_local/unified/launcher.py}, which consumes a single
configuration object and dispatches it to thin per-framework adapters
--- \texttt{trl\_integrations/}, \texttt{verl/},
\texttt{platform\_modal/openrlhf/}, \texttt{platform\_hybrid/skyrl/} and
\texttt{platform\_tinker/atropos/} --- with the reference GRPO loop
implemented once in \longtok{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo.py}. The
reward grader and the decoding path are shared code. That sharing is the
load-bearing part of the design: because the grader and the sampler are
the same objects across back-ends, a difference in outcome between two
frameworks is attributable to the framework, and not to a divergent
reward parser or a differently configured decoder masquerading as an
algorithmic difference. The commitment also bounds what a result may
claim. A completed run supports a statement about that stack, at that
revision, with that grader; it does not license a statement about
``GRPO'' in the abstract, and the project's reporting standard (§4.4,
P5) is built around that distinction.

Four pillars carry the commitment, each holding the remainder of the
stack fixed while varying one factor:

\textbf{The four de-confound pillars.}

1 · PPO versus GRPO, same stack & Advantage estimator: group baseline

Per-step ZVF logged on every run; the nine-row method panel, vanilla
GRPO row included, is a declared simulation projection (§6.3.2) \\

knob & G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\} × seeds, held out \\

Dr.~GRPO versus GRPO, pre-to-post, McNemar \\
\end{longtable}

Pillar 1 asks whether a reported GRPO advantage survives when the
advantage estimator is the main thing that changes. The comparison is
designed on \texttt{Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B} two-digit addition over 40 steps
and five seeds paired by seed, evaluated on 200 prompts per arm drawn
fresh from the training generator rather than from a disjoint split (the
two arms' item sets largely differ; §6.2.3), and the exact loss form of
both arms is in Table 4.2. Because that task is near ceiling, the design
can bound an estimator effect but cannot separate ``no effect'' from
``no headroom''; the result is reported in Chapter 6 §6.2.3. The
Qwen3-8B row in the project's own effect-size table is excluded from
Pillar 1 as a provenance conflict and is not treated as an estimate.
Pillar 2 turns a run's internal signal state into a first-class
measurement; it is formalised in §4.3. Pillar 3 varies the group size G
while holding model, data, reward and schedule fixed, and is the axis on
which the preference-density argument of P3 rests. Pillar 4 asks the
question the other three cannot answer --- whether any of this changes
held-out behaviour --- by comparing a pre-RL checkpoint with its post-RL
successor on data the training loop never saw.

The headline configuration of the internal report sources is LoRA rank
32, Adam with β₁ = 0.9 and β₂ = 0.95, learning rate 1 × 10⁻⁴, and the
seed set \{42, 123, 456, 789, 1024\}. It is not held fixed across the
programme. The canonical dispatch matrix uses rank 16 and learning rate
1 × 10⁻⁶, the stack probe uses 1 × 10⁻⁵, the P4 scale extension uses
rank 4, and the Tinker runners default to 3 × 10⁻⁵; Table 4.2 lists the
values each study actually used. The instrumented 79-run roster
described in that source spans the Qwen3, Qwen3.5, Llama-3.x,
DeepSeek-V3.1 and Nemotron-120B families, from 0.6B to approximately
671B total parameters, including mixture-of-experts anchors at the
frontier end of that range; the wider P1 cross-scale roster adds a 1T
anchor (Kimi-K2) and so spans 0.6B to 1T (Chapter 6 §6.1; source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_abstract.tex). The statistical
apparatus is declared in the shared methods source but was not
pre-registered (Chapter 9 §9.2; the analysis log records 206
iterations). It consists of Welch t-tests and Mann--Whitney U tests for
two-sample contrasts; Cohen's d with Hedges--Olkin intervals; 95\%
bootstrap confidence intervals with 10,000 resamples; rliable aggregate
metrics (interquartile mean, median and optimality gap); and Bonferroni
and Benjamini--Hochberg corrections over a declared test family. Power
was computed rather than assumed: at 80\% power the minimum detectable
effect for an equal-n comparison of five seeds against five is d\_min =
2.024, improving to d\_min = 1.530 when a five-seed group is compared
against a pooled group of fifteen. Those figures are a standing reminder
of how coarse a five-seed design is, and the project reports several
null results that are null precisely because the design could not have
detected anything smaller.

Two limitations belong in this section rather than in a later
discussion. First, the framework roster is broader than the framework
evidence: although seven RL libraries are named in the study design,
only Tinker and TRL produced completed runs, and the veRL and OpenRLHF
entries in \texttt{framework\_comparison.json} are dry-run placeholders
rather than measurements. Cross-library claims are therefore narrower
than the roster implies. Second, the variance decomposition that
motivates the whole attribution commitment is itself four separate
marginal one-way ANOVAs --- library/framework, training algorithm, model
family and model scale --- each run against the same 42 experiments with
valid performance observations. On a corpus where the factors are
collinear a marginal η² absorbs the variance of each factor's
correlates, so the design reads the decomposition only as evidence that
framework and algorithm vary a great deal across this corpus, which is
what motivates holding them fixed --- not as an estimate of any factor's
independent contribution, which a marginal one-way analysis on a
non-orthogonal design cannot identify. The values are reported in
Chapter 6 §6.1. The historical twenty-test multiple-comparison family
that accompanies it pre-dates the Tier-A/B/C partition and is retained
as a reproducibility audit trail, explicitly not as a corrected survival
claim.

\section{Per-Step Telemetry}\label{per-step-telemetry}

Attribution is only as good as the run's observability. The intended
per-step telemetry record contains the Zero-Variance Fraction; gradient
utilisation; the collapse decomposition that separates all-correct from
all-wrong zero-variance groups; and, alongside them, reward, loss,
entropy, completion length and the KL divergence to the reference policy
when one exists and that measurement is exposed; actual availability is
runner-specific. Records are written to JSONL and mirrored to Weights \&
Biases, so a run can be analysed locally without a network dependency
and reviewed remotely without a local environment. The reason ZVF and
its decomposition are logged as separate fields rather than collapsed
into a single starvation number is that the same aggregate fraction can
describe opposite situations: a group of completions that are all
correct and a group that are all wrong both contribute zero within-group
variance, and the remedy for each is different. Logging the
decomposition preserves that distinction at the point of collection
instead of trying to recover it afterwards (Figure~\ref{fig:telemetry}).


\fig{fig_telemetry}
{Per-step telemetry recorded by the unified harness, and the destinations each record flows to}
{Per-step telemetry recorded by the unified harness, and the destinations each record flows to.}
{fig:telemetry}



The quantity being logged is the group-relative advantage actually used
by the runner:

\[A_i = \frac{r_i - \mu_g}{\sigma_g + \varepsilon},\qquad \mu_g = \frac{1}{G}\sum_j r_j,\qquad \sigma_g = \sqrt{\frac{1}{G}\sum_j (r_j - \mu_g)^2}\]

This form is taken from the Phase-1 report. Throughout this thesis
\(\sigma_g\) is the population standard deviation, with 1/G as written
above. Every GRPO-style runner in Table 4.2 computes it that way. Two
places use the unbiased 1/(G − 1) form instead: the reference
\texttt{zvf()} of §4.3, whose \texttt{ddof=1} sample variance leaves
exact ties unchanged but can change classification at a positive
tolerance, and the batch-wide whitening of the PPO arm in the same-stack
run. The ZVF-definition figure (Figure \ref{fig:zvfdef}) also shows the
unbiased form, which gives \(\sigma_g \approx 0.535\) for its example
group where the population form gives 0.5. When \(\sigma_g = 0\) every
\(A_i\) is zero, so the whole group contributes no gradient through the
advantage term. Whether anything else acts depends on the executed loss:
KL regularisation and auxiliary or critic losses can remain nonzero.
Clipping alone does not create a policy-surrogate gradient when the
advantage is zero. The zero-gradient statement is therefore confined to
the reward-relative surrogate in verified group-centred implementations;
it is not a claim that the whole optimiser step vanishes. Table 4.2
distinguishes verified absence, configuration evidence and unknown
managed settings.

Two further design choices distinguish this telemetry from conventional
training curves. The first is that the raw per-group reward tensors are
retained, not only the summaries computed from them. This makes a
published number auditable by recomputation: any downstream diagnostic,
such as \longtok{p2\_\allowbreak{}collapse\_\allowbreak{}analysis.py}, can reproduce a reported
statistic from the stored tensors rather than trusting a logged summary.
The convention has been exercised: study P2 recomputes three stored
GSM8K group tensors (Qwen3-8B sampling runs, G = 8) from their raw
values rather than reading the figures back from a log line, and the
recomputed values are reported in Chapter 6 §6.3.1. The second is that
the logging layer is treated as part of the apparatus and is expected to
fail. An early Weights \& Biases integration used the library's
thread-unsafe global run object from multiple worker threads, which
produced repeated ``Run is finished'' summary-write failures; the fix
was to give each run its own process in \texttt{campaign.py}. The defect
is recorded because telemetry that silently drops writes is
indistinguishable from telemetry showing a healthy run, and the remedy
--- process-level isolation per run --- is a structural property of the
campaign rather than a patch.

\section{Formalising the Zero-Variance
Fraction}\label{formalising-the-zero-variance-fraction}

We distinguish the exact-tie fraction, \(\mathrm{ZVF}_0\), from the
tolerance-based diagnostic, \(\mathrm{ZVF}_{\varepsilon}\). The first
counts groups with identical observed rewards; the second also counts
near-ties. Unless explicitly labelled exact-tie, ZVF here uses the
following sample-variance threshold. Formally, for a batch \(B_t\) of
prompt groups at step \(t\), each group \(g\) contributing \(K\) sampled
completions with rewards \(r_{g,1}, \ldots, r_{g,K}\),

\[\mathrm{ZVF}_t = \frac{1}{|B_t|} \sum_{g \in B_t} \mathbf{1}\!\left[\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(r_{g,1}, \ldots, r_{g,K}) \le \varepsilon\right]\]

where \(\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}\) is the unbiased sample variance and ε =
10⁻⁶ for rewards normalised to {[}0, 1{]}. The exact-tie formulation
used in some internal sources instead writes
\(\mathrm{ZVF}_t = \frac{1}{|P_t|} \sum_{p \in P_t} \mathbf{1}[\mathrm{Var}_{c \sim C(p)}\, r(c) = 0]\),
with \(C(p)\) the set of sampled completions for prompt p.~These
formulations agree for the stored binary rewards at G = 8 and ε = 10⁻⁶,
but are not interchangeable for perturbed or continuous rewards. In
particular, the micro-jitter test changes exact ties while leaving this
tolerance-based diagnostic unchanged (§6.3.1). The reference
implementation is deliberately small, so that the diagnostic cannot
acquire hidden behaviour:

\begin{verbatim}
def zvf(rewards_2d: np.ndarray, eps: float = 1e-6) -> float:
    # rewards_2d shape: [num_groups, K]
    var_per_group = rewards_2d.var(axis=-1, ddof=1)
    return float((var_per_group <= eps).mean())
\end{verbatim}

Gradient utilisation is then defined as its complement,
\(\mathrm{GU}_t = 1 - \mathrm{ZVF}_t\). The complement is a descriptive
accounting identity, not a claim about optimiser behaviour. Its scope
must be read against the runner actually used, so the loss form of every
study is listed in Table 4.2, read from the runner code rather than from
the internal reports' prose. Three facts in the table govern how ZVF is
interpreted. First, an exact-tie group has zero reward-relative
surrogate gradient only where the runner actually centres advantages
within that group. Near-ties counted by \(\mathrm{ZVF}_{\varepsilon}\)
need not have exactly zero advantages. TRL's dump records β = 0.04 and
an on-device reference model, its launcher leaves β unspecified, the PPO
roster inherits adaptive-KL defaults, and the managed dump has β = null.
None of these is evidence of zero executed KL; unknown remains unknown.
Even verified zero-KL runs can update through optimiser state,
regularisation or auxiliary losses. Second, the inspected custom-loss
Tinker reference runners are REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline (the
actor's historical source binding is incomplete; Appendix D): the loss
is \(-A_i\) times the summed token log-probability, averaged over
completions, with no importance ratio and no clipping, and this thesis
calls them that rather than GRPO in the strict sense. Third, the runners
labelled GRPO are not one loss: the same-stack and Dr.~GRPO studies use
a clipped ratio with two inner epochs, the Tinker runners do not, and
the Phase-1 four-method run divides by no σ even in its ``grpo'' arm.
The scope must consequently be checked per row, including auxiliary
losses, masks and source-binding gaps, rather than inferred from the
algorithm label (Figures~\ref{fig:zvfdef} and~\ref{fig:gu}).


\fig{fig_zvf_definition}
{Definition of the Zero-Variance Fraction}
{Definition of the Zero-Variance Fraction. One prompt produces $G$ completions with scalar rewards; when the within-group reward standard deviation is zero the advantage is identically zero and the sample contributes no reward-relative surrogate gradient. Exact ties and the sample-variance threshold are distinguished in the text; they agree for the binary rewards illustrated here. The composition bar is measured on Qwen3-8B, held-out GSM8K slice, $G=8$, $T=1.0$, 200 problems $\times$ 3 seeds; all-correct (vertical hatch) and all-wrong (cross-hatch) together make up the ZVF.}
{fig:zvfdef}



\fig{fig_gradient_utilisation}
{Gradient utilisation, $1-\zvf$, as a function of group size $G$}
{Gradient utilisation, $1-\zvf$, as a function of group size $G$. Qwen2.5-0.5B, arithmetic-correctness task, 40 steps, 16 prompts, $G\in\{2,4,8,16\}$, seeds 42/123/456 ($n=3$ per $G$; \longtok{groupsize\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}sweep.json}). (a) Open circles are single seeds; squares are seed means with 95\% $t$-intervals ($df=2$). The line is a two-parameter power law, $\zvf = 0.922\,G^{-0.137}$, fitted to the four means (2 residual degrees of freedom), so it is descriptive rather than a validated law; $G=32^{\ast}$ was not trained and the dotted segment is extrapolation. (b) The absolute gain per doubling is nearly constant ($+0.074$, $+0.073$, $+0.059$), so the diminishing return is relative: $46\%\to31\%\to19\%$ of the previous level. Utilisation stays below 0.37 at $G=16$.}
{fig:gu}
