Last-10 training reward
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Arm
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Accuracy before → after
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Change (95\% CI)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Last-10 training reward
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
GRPO, G = 8 & 0.200 → 0.245 & +0.045 {[}+0.033, +0.057{]} & 0.270 \\
GRPO, G = 2 & 0.200 → 0.250 & +0.050 {[}+0.026, +0.074{]} & 0.275 \\
PPO, learned value head & 0.200 → 0.195 & −0.005 {[}−0.033, +0.023{]} &
0.196 \\
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

Both GRPO arms improve on every seed, which reproduces the +6.2-point
GRPO gain of §6.5.4 at a similar size. The PPO arm shows no detectable
held-out gain: its training reward drifts down and its post-training
accuracy is indistinguishable from the starting point. After training,
GRPO at G = 8 exceeds PPO by +0.050 {[}+0.015, +0.085{]} (paired \(t\),
p = 0.016), and the difference is positive for all five seeds, which
puts the exact two-sided sign-flip p at its n = 5 floor of 0.0625. GRPO
at G = 8 and at G = 2 differ by −0.005 {[}−0.038, +0.028{]} (p = 0.69);
that interval is wider than the ±0.02 margin, so equivalence is not
established either (TOST p = 0.13).

The PPO result has a narrow reading. The PPO arm is the minimal one that
matches the GRPO arms' stack: a linear value head trained from zero for
30 steps at the policy's learning rate, with no critic warm-up, no
generalised advantage estimation and no tuning. Its advantage is
therefore close to batch-centred reward with a noisy baseline, so what
the contrast shows is that on this fixed stack and budget the
group-relative baseline shows a held-out gain and this minimal
learned-critic baseline shows no detectable gain. It is a
single-configuration result with five seeds, not a verdict on PPO with a
tuned critic, and the 64-prompt PPO batch also sees eight times as many
distinct prompts per step as GRPO at G = 8. The paired-t result depends
on a small-sample parametric approximation; the exact two-sided
sign-flip result does not reach 0.05. Both describe seed variation on
the same fixed 200-item evaluation slice, not uncertainty over GSM8K
items or model/task families. Fixed generation counts are not measured
equality of generated tokens or total compute. Mean training completions
are 190.16 (G8), 191.62 (G2) and 194.16 (PPO) tokens under cap 200, with
PPO last-ten length 196.19; non-ceiling accuracy does not establish a
non-binding length regime. For group size, the rerun removes the
accuracy-saturation objection and still finds no detectable effect
between G = 2 and G = 8 at this budget, without establishing that none
exists.

\section{Cross-Pillar Observations and
Limitations}\label{cross-pillar-observations-and-limitations}

Three observations span the four pillars and are worth stating together,
because the chapter's overall contribution is better captured by their
pattern than by any single number.

First, the project's strongest results are its de-confounds, and its
de-confounds mostly return nulls. The matched same-stack PPO-versus-GRPO
contrast returns a seed-paired difference of −0.002 with p = 0.374 (n =
5 seeds, arms evaluated on largely different items, at a near-ceiling
accuracy, not shown equivalent by TOST at ±0.005); the four-axis
factorization audit attributes η² = 0.023 to the algorithm axis and η² =
0.54 of last-10 training-reward variance to the group-size axis (F(3, 8)
= 3.08, p = 0.09, n = 12 runs); the controlled length-bias comparison
returns zero length-bias flags across sixteen runs. In each case the
stack was held fixed except the factor under test (and, in the PPO arm,
the number of distinct prompts per step), and in each case the expected
algorithmic advantage failed to appear. Each of these comparisons was
run on the saturated 0.5B arithmetic task, at evaluation accuracy
0.98--0.99. The group-size axis carries the largest variance share, but
at n = 3 seeds per arm it does not reach significance on either training
reward or held-out accuracy. It is also a hyperparameter rather than an
algorithm choice. The chapter's empirical conclusion is therefore
conditional on saturation: on a 0.5B arithmetic task at evaluation
accuracy near 0.99, no algorithm-label effect was detectable, which
cannot distinguish ``no effect'' from ``no headroom''; the sampling
configuration is the more plausible lever but was not shown to matter at
this seed budget. Rerun on the unsaturated Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT
configuration (§6.5.5), the picture changes for one contrast: GRPO at G
= 8 exceeds a minimal learned-critic PPO by +0.050 {[}+0.015, +0.085{]}
(paired-t p = 0.016; exact sign-flip p = 0.0625), while the minimal PPO
arm shows no detectable gain on this budget; G = 2 and G = 8 remain
indistinguishable without being shown equivalent.

Second, the diagnostics and interventions are less prescriptive than
their motivating literature suggests, and the project says so. ZVF
measures signal starvation reliably. It associates with catastrophic
collapse (ρ = 0.56, 95\% CI {[}0.19, 0.79{]}, n = 23 cells), and its
association with graded held-out accuracy is not distinguishable from
zero (ρ = 0.27, 95\% CI {[}−0.37, 0.88{]}, n = 23), because it is
unsigned and therefore cannot distinguish mastery from incapacity. The
four interventions built on it --- the difficulty curriculum, the
group-size sweet spot, the length-bias loss formulations and the step-1
layer freeze --- all return nulls at matched cost, and two of them (the
curriculum and the G = 4 win) were flagged as SUSPECT by an independent
verification pass before multi-seed re-testing reclassified them as
noise. Reporting an intervention as a null after an independent reviewer
flagged it as overclaimed is, in this project's convention, part of the
result.

Third, the single-seed regime is the dominant shared limitation. The
cross-scale anchors are single-seed and descriptive. The roster-level
verbosity-trap audit is single-seed per configuration, and its
in-training reward metric is not a held-out one. Only the P3 sweep, the
P4 controlled comparison, the five-seed Qwen3-8B held-out control and
the P4 scale extension operate at n ≥ 3, and the Qwen3-8B control is the
only multi-seed matched base-versus-RL comparison on Qwen3-8B; the
Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT pre/post runs and the small-sample pairs of Table
8.B are the other base-versus-trained comparisons. Every headline in
this chapter is therefore stated at the strength that its own evidence
base supports, which is in several cases weaker than the strength at
which it would need to be stated to generalise beyond the named stack.
The reconstructions (G = 32 and above in P3, the T = 64M grid, the
difficulty-gated saving projection) are held at hypothesis-generator
status for the same reason: they are joint-fit extrapolations, not
trained arms, and the project declines to let them carry a
recommendation.

Taken together, the four pillars establish no general algorithm ranking:
the unsaturated PPO contrast is baseline-specific and test-sensitive,
while the group-size contrasts remain unresolved at these seed budgets.
The method's most widely repeated specific failure mode --- length
inflation --- is absent in the regimes where it was tested directly and
is not established by the roster flag, which is not specific to length.
That is a narrower and more cautious set of conclusions than the P1--P4
internal reports set out to establish, and the chapter reports it as
such.



\chapter{Results II: Reporting, Registry and Controller
(P5--P7)}\label{results-ii-reporting-registry-and-controller-p5p7}

The three internal reports covered in this chapter form a chain rather
than a set of independent results. P5 makes a claim about the epistemic
status of reinforcement-learning results for language models and
proposes a checkable reporting standard to match it. P6 turns the
standard into a machine-readable artifact with a validator, a badge and
a diff tool. P7 takes the diagnostic that the standard requires authors
to publish --- the zero-variance fraction (ZVF) --- and asks whether it
is mechanistically predictable and ready to control rollout allocation.
Each report is explicit about where its claim stops, and this chapter
reports their evidence in the same register.

\section{A Minimum Reporting Standard for Stack-Conditioned Results
(P5)}\label{a-minimum-reporting-standard-for-stack-conditioned-results-p5}

P5's position is that results in this field are
\emph{stack-conditioned}, and that reporting the framework or algorithm
label without the stack is therefore insufficient to support the claims
that are routinely made from it. The evidence for that position is not a
single demonstration but a family of levers, each showing that varying
one component of the stack, with the label held fixed, moves or flips
the reported outcome or its telemetry.

The cleanest lever is a managed 12-cell head-to-head: four
group-relative labels (GRPO \citep{shao2024deepseekmath}, DAPO
\citep{yu2025dapo}, Dr.~GRPO \citep{liu2025understanding}, GSPO
\citep{zheng2025gspo}) run on one stack --- Tinker-managed, Qwen3.5-4B,
GSM8K, three seeds per arm. Mean last-10 training reward spans 0.710 to
0.744, a band of 0.034, with per-arm values of 0.744 (DAPO), 0.742
(Dr.~GRPO), 0.723 (GRPO) and 0.710 (GSPO); mean ZVF over the same arms
is 0.578, 0.500, 0.567 and 0.511. The labels move telemetry by more than
they move outcome, and neither movement is large.

The largest lever in the corpus is an outcome flip of 5.0\% to 85.6\%
final training reward under a fixed label when the backend is changed.
That figure must be read with its provenance caveat attached, because
the same inspection that surfaced it also found that the nominal backend
comparison bundled a different base checkpoint: Qwen3-8B-Base on the
managed Tinker run against the instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B on TRL. The
number is therefore evidence that item 3 of the standard must record
backend \emph{and} checkpoint identity; it is not a causal estimate of
the backend alone. A second same-label lever points the other way: the
label ``DAPO'' produced mean ZVF 0.00 on an open trainer running true
dynamic sampling and 0.58 on a closed stack running an asymmetric-clip
surrogate.

These exhibits are summarised by a variance decomposition, and its
design has to be read with it. The algorithm term comes from the
four-method same-stack N2 run (GRPO, AERO, GIFT, AREAL; one seed; 40
steps each, so 4 groups of 40 per-step observations, n = 160, df = 3
between and 156 within): η² = 0.0454 on ZVF, with a within-method
(stratified) step-bootstrap CI of {[}0.0094, 0.1476{]}, and η² = 0.0075
{[}0.0019, 0.0790{]} on mean reward (corrected; see Table E.1). The
source interval was drawn from the pooled steps, so it described a null
band rather than the estimate's uncertainty. The corrected ZVF interval
therefore does not support the reading that the algorithm explains at
most about 6\% of ZVF variance: an algorithm share of up to roughly 15\%
is compatible with these data, and the source's DECISIVE grade on this
term falls to SUGGESTIVE. Even the corrected intervals are too narrow,
because the steps are autocorrelated within a run and those 156
within-group degrees of freedom overstate the independent information
(recomputed by
\longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/p5p8/p5\_\allowbreak{}headline\_\allowbreak{}cis\_\allowbreak{}full.py}; output
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_\allowbreak{}headline\_\allowbreak{}cis\_\allowbreak{}full.tsv}).
No group-size term is reported from this decomposition: the group-size
file, \longtok{groupsize\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}sweep.tsv}, holds one row per G level (4
cells, one observation per cell, df = 0 within), on which any one-way
decomposition returns η² = 1 by construction (corrected; see Table E.1).
The 98-cell mega-campaign decomposition is an unbalanced partial grid
(98 of 120 cells over model family, task slice, G, temperature and seed,
with two seeds). Its four stack axes account for 72.9\% to 92.7\% of the
variance in the five logged metrics; the seed axis rounds to 0.000, so
no stack-to-seed ratio is quoted as an effect size (corrected; see Table
E.1).

The standard that P5 proposes has eight items --- seven run-manifest
fields and one evaluation item --- and each item earned its place by
exhibiting a documented lever of this kind. Item 1 is the loss form,
reported as a tuple of ratio level, clip low and high, token mask,
advantage normalisation and dynamic-sampling status --- the same label
with and without dynamic sampling produced opposite ZVF telemetry (0.00
against 0.58). Item 2 is the reference policy and KL handling, which
distinguishes family members that are otherwise conflated and whose
omission is unrecoverable after the fact. Item 3 is the sampler, backend
and precision, including base-checkpoint identity, motivated by the 17×
exhibit above. Item 4 is the per-step ZVF and gradient-utilisation
trajectory, because a single mean aliases opposite regimes: across a
368-run audit drawn from a 790-run corpus, mean ZVF is 0.97 and 0.95 for
two low-reward models (Llama-3.2-1B and Llama-3.2-3B), falls to 0.25 and
0.29 at mid-range rewards, and rises again to 0.87 and 0.96 for two
near-saturated models. Item 5 is the group-size schedule, since group
size is a signal-geometry knob rather than a throughput knob; measured
mean ZVF for Qwen3-32B falls from 0.33 at G=4 to 0.18 at G=8 and 0.08 at
G=16, and for Llama-3.1-8B from 0.76 to 0.65 to 0.47, while the two
boundary cases compress the effect as the theory predicts. Item 6
requires a held-out split disjoint from the reward environment. Item 7
requires a contamination check and a parser-robustness probe: adding
micro-jitter δ \textasciitilde{} U(0,10⁻⁴) collapsed the
\emph{exact-tie} fraction from 95/600 (0.1583) to 0/600 while pairwise
contrast density changed negligibly. Recalculation with the declared
sample-variance tolerance ε = 10⁻⁶ instead leaves ZVF at 95/600 before
and after jitter (§6.3.1). The example demonstrates sensitivity of exact
ties to rounding, not failure of the tolerance-based diagnostic.

Item 8 is deliberately different in kind: held-out pass@k curves at k ∈
\{1, 8, 32\} belong to the evaluation report rather than to the run
manifest. The lever here is a matched base-versus-post-RL panel --- four
adapters at G ∈ \{2, 4, 16, 32\}, one stack, the same problems --- on
which pass@1 deltas scatter from −0.5 to +1.9 points (under which
reading the G=2 arm would be called a regression) while the pass@32
frontier improves by +1.5 to +2.5 points in all four arms. Neither
reading reaches significance at a single training seed, which is itself
the argument: without pass@k curves and stated noise bands the same
panel supports any narrative the reader prefers.

P5 also ships the machinery that makes the standard enforceable rather
than advisory. An auditor scores a manifest against the eight items and
emits a 0--100 weighted badge; on an n=103 audit the weighted total was
55.0 with a badge span of {[}35.8, 56.7{]} and standard deviation 3.1,
awarding zero gold, zero silver, 99 bronze and 4 wood. Coverage,
however, is where the negative result lies. On 98 manifests, every one
of the seven manifest fields is present as a key 100\% of the time, yet
item 2 is \emph{validated} in 0 of 98 cases (0.0\%), and all twelve of
its sub-fields are present at 0 in each case; nine of the sub-fields
across items 1 and 2 --- advantage normalisation, clip high, clip low,
dynamic sampling, KL coefficient, KL estimator, ratio level, reference
snapshot and token mask --- sit at 0.0\% with zero information content.
Twenty-three sub-fields examined across the population show no field at
a full null rate, but twenty of the twenty-three carry at most two
distinct values, and the twenty entries collapse to ten distinct 23-bit
fingerprints.

Two further analyses bound what the standard can be expected to buy. The
first is a discriminative-entropy audit: four of the seven manifest
fields are vacuous on this corpus --- loss form, reference policy and
KL, sampler/backend/precision, and decontamination notes each carry too
little task-discriminative information to distinguish runs --- while the
per-step ZVF path and the group-size schedule carry the information. A
field-sufficiency regression agrees: a random forest on the disclosed
stack fields, scored by out-of-fold R² over the 98 cells (8-fold
cross-validation, 8 fold seeds), predicts ZVF at R² = 0.832 {[}0.772,
0.879{]} and mean reward at R² = 0.993 {[}0.990, 0.996{]}. The
label-only comparator is R² = 0.000 by construction, because all 98
cells share one algorithm label, so it is a floor rather than a
competing model. The second is a manifest self-sufficiency test, and it
splits sharply by metric. The manifest carries essentially all of the
ZVF signal (R² = 0.776 {[}0.730, 0.846{]} against 0.773 {[}0.735,
0.834{]} for the cells, ΔR² = −0.003 {[}−0.045, +0.034{]}) but only a
minority of the reward signal (R² = 0.280 {[}0.219, 0.443{]} against
0.743 {[}0.688, 0.820{]}, ΔR² = +0.463 {[}+0.309, +0.564{]}); all 98
cells are therefore labelled \longtok{honest\_\allowbreak{}but\_\allowbreak{}vacuous} on the
three-axis triangulation.

The most useful negative in P5 is the placebo-replacement bound. The
four-of-seven vacuity pattern admits two readings: the schema chose the
wrong items, or any stack-descriptor schema would be vacuous on a
single-stack corpus. Projecting six candidate replacement items onto the
live corpus refutes the first reading. Four of the six --- KL
coefficient, clip range low, advantage normalisation and mini-batch size
--- project to exactly one unique value on the 98-cell corpus with a
bootstrap CI excluding any positive information gain, because the corpus
never varies them; temperature schedule would add 0.995 bits and lift
the total from 11.41 to 12.41 bits, and a model-family label is
redundant with an existing column. The variance partition confirms it:
stack axes explain η² = 0.947 of ZVF variance across 25 unique buckets,
with 5.3\% seed residual and an algorithm term of zero by construction.
The schema is not the binding constraint; the corpus is. On the live
corpus the fingerprint over the seven manifest fields carries 11.4 bits
in total, four fields carry exactly zero, and Hamming distance over the
fingerprint is associated with the absolute difference in ZVF (Spearman
ρ = +0.44), PCD (ρ = +0.39) and mean reward (ρ = +0.28) across all 4,753
cell pairs, while the badge itself is constant across cells and cannot
discriminate seed-pair dispersion (ρ = +0.000, n = 48 pairs). Because
pairs that share a cell are not independent, significance comes from a
Mantel test that permutes cell labels jointly on the distance matrix
(9,999 permutations): p \textless{} 10⁻³ for all three outcomes, and the
association holds when seed replicates are averaged into 50
configurations (ρ = +0.47, +0.43, +0.33). The Hamming distance takes
only four values and is in effect a function of group size and task, so
the test shows that cells differing on those fields differ in outcome,
not that the fingerprint adds information beyond them. The source
paper's ZVF and PCD values (ρ = +0.529 and +0.541) could not be
reproduced: its own analysis script gives +0.407 and +0.336 on a
2,000-pair sample, and the full-matrix values are those above
(corrected; see Table E.1).

\section{The GRPO-Registry (P6)}\label{the-grpo-registry-p6}

P6's premise is that the deltas defining GRPO variants --- DAPO's
asymmetric clip, dynamic sampling and token-level loss; Dr.~GRPO's
dropped length and standard-deviation normalisation; GSPO's
sequence-level importance ratio \citep{zheng2025gspo} --- live in paper
appendices and framework defaults rather than in any machine-readable
form. The registry is the response, and it ships as three artifacts at
\texttt{registry/}: \texttt{schema.json}, \texttt{entries/*.json} and
\texttt{query.py}.

The schema defines two record types under a single \texttt{oneOf}, both
carrying \texttt{schema\_version}. The stack record's core is
\texttt{min\_report}, one sub-object per minimum-report item, with
base-checkpoint identity required inside the \texttt{sampler\_backend}
block precisely because the audit that motivated item 3 found a managed
backend silently pinning a different base checkpoint. Two design
decisions carry weight. The first is the distinction between an
unreported field and a field reported as absent: a null means the author
said nothing, whereas an explicit \texttt{false}, \texttt{0.0} or
\texttt{"none"} means the author made a checkable claim --- ``a stack
that says `we use no KL term' is making a checkable claim; a stack that
says nothing is not.'' The second is that item 8, the held-out pass@k
requirement, is attached under outcomes as an evaluation-report field
rather than folded into the run manifest, preserving the eight-item
standard's split between what describes a run and what describes a
result. Per-component delta status is recorded on the stack side in
\longtok{variant\_\allowbreak{}deltas\_\allowbreak{}applied}, with each entry's status drawn from
\{implemented, surrogate, absent, unknown\}. The shipped entry for the
managed DAPO run makes the point concretely: \texttt{clip\_higher} is
recorded as \texttt{surrogate} with the note that the asymmetric clip
was set through the user-facing config while the underlying managed loss
form is unverifiable, \texttt{dynamic\_sampling} is recorded as
\texttt{absent}, and \longtok{token\_\allowbreak{}level\_\allowbreak{}loss} is \texttt{unknown}
because it is managed by the runtime. An optional \texttt{ci\_method}
object (method, bootstrap count, seed, confidence level, source) is
populated on the seven derived entries with \texttt{n\_boot\ =\ 2000}
and a percentile 95\% interval, and left null on the remaining
twenty-four.

Three variant-delta records were verified against their source papers:
DAPO with five components (clip higher at ε\_low = 0.2 and ε\_high =
0.28, dynamic sampling, token-level loss, overlong reward shaping, KL
removed); Dr.~GRPO with two removals (the 1/\textbar o\_i\textbar{}
response-length normalisation in the loss and the division by the
within-group reward standard deviation in the advantage); and GSPO with
a length-normalised sequence-likelihood importance ratio and
sequence-level clipping.

The seed population is twenty stack records in five families, all of
which pass validation. Family A holds four framework config dumps
(Qwen3-8B, GSM8K, seed 42) scoring badges 74, 60, 60 and 62; family B
holds the four open Colab trainer arms at toy scale, scoring 96, 96, 96
and 96; family C is the managed head-to-head at 57, 62, 62 and 60;
family D is a same-stack four-method run at 67 each; and family E is a
five-seed risk-index batch at 43 each. The finding worth stating plainly
is why the ordering looks the way it does. The config dumps were
assembled for a reviewer-requested fairness audit and score only 57 to
74: they nail items 2, 3 and 5, expose item 1 only partially, and cover
items 6 and 7 not at all. The open Colab arms score 96 not because the
runs are better but because an open backward pass, coupled with explicit
declarations that no KL term and no decontamination check were used,
makes every field reportable. Reproducibility supplements of the kind
commonly shipped with RL papers do not automatically satisfy MIN-REPORT.

The health audit is the registry's own evidence that it works. Every
record shipped with P6 parses: 31 of 31 pass, comprising 20 stack
records and 11 variant-delta records, with zero broken delta pointers.
The delta-mix table is the sharpest single result in P6: the label
``DAPO'' is claimed by ten entries, of which four implement a delta, one
records a surrogate, three record it as absent and two record it as
unknown --- the same label covering four distinct implementations (Table
\texttt{tab:p6-delta-mix}). Per-item coverage on the twenty stack
records is 100\% for the held-out split (40/40), 95\% for
sampler/backend (76/80), 90\% for telemetry (54/60), 68\% for the
group-size schedule (41/60), 48\% for reference policy and KL (29/60),
32\% for the loss form (38/120) and 20\% for decontamination (8/40)
(Table \texttt{tab:p6-coverage}). The per-openness breakdown inverts the
conventional expectation: the open subset records sampler/backend at
91.7\% and telemetry at 83.3\% with reference KL at 58.3\%, while the
managed subset records sampler/backend and telemetry at 100\% but
reference KL at 33.3\% and the loss form at only 22.9\%.

The registry was stress-tested rather than asserted. A batch of
adversarial perturbations with B = 4000 bootstrap resamples --- 13
categories × 31 entries × 10 mutations = 4,030 generated, of which 800
were no-ops for their entry and were skipped, leaving 3,230 applied ---
moved the overall robustness statistic from 0.864 {[}0.756, 0.878{]}
before the corresponding schema bumps to 1.000 {[}1.000, 1.000{]} after
them, closing three silent-failure categories: a missing identifier
pattern check (0.645 to 1.000), MIN-REPORT leaves declared in
\texttt{properties} but not in \texttt{required} (0.000 to 1.000, fixed
by promoting key presence to a schema invariant), and free-form delta
references (0.000 to 1.000, fixed by replacing \texttt{delta\_id} with
an enum generated from the entry filenames). A separate consistency
check cross-references the variant-delta records against the MIN-REPORT
blocks: of 31 triples from 13 entries, 7 are MATCH, 0 are MISMATCH, 0
are MISSING\_REPORT, 5 are SURROGATE\_OBS and 19 are NOT\_APPLICABLE,
giving an implementation-honesty match rate of 7/7 = 1.000 with a Wilson
95\% CI of {[}0.65, 1.00{]} (a percentile bootstrap of 7/7 is degenerate
at {[}1, 1{]}).

The empirical payoff of the schema is the same-stack measurement.
Re-executing four GRPO-family methods (GRPO, AERO, GIFT, AREAL) on a
single identical stack --- managed sampler, G = 8, seed 0, 40 steps, 16
prompts per step --- with the full per-prompt reward tensor logged at
every step, produces reward deltas of magnitude ≤ 2 percentage points.
Two of the three show within-run intervals that exclude zero on the side
of a \emph{decrease} (AERO at −0.014 {[}−0.023, −0.005{]} and AREAL at
−0.020 {[}−0.032, −0.008{]}). Those intervals are paired bootstrap
intervals over the last 10 of 40 steps of a single seed-0 run per
method, so they carry no seed variance and are not replication. GIFT's
delta is +0.016 {[}−0.007, +0.040{]}. Exact sign-flip tests over the
same 10 paired steps, Holm-adjusted across the three contrasts, give p =
0.047 for AERO, 0.047 for AREAL and 0.23 for GIFT; when prompt-level
variation is included (160 paired prompt-steps), the AERO decrease is no
longer distinguishable from zero (Holm p = 0.20) (corrected; see Table
E.1). The magnitude thesis survives. Whether any difference exceeds
seed-to-seed noise was not tested, so neither the claim that all
differences are within noise nor its negation is established. The
largest same-stack effects are not on reward at all: AERO and AREAL
lengthen completions by approximately 31 tokens and tighten the length
coefficient of variation, and GIFT's loss shift of −1.67×10⁴ is a
baseline constant. A later registry extension measures the anti-herding
residual
\(\delta_{\mathrm{div}} = \mathrm{ZVF}^{\mathrm{iid}} - \mathrm{ZVF}^{\mathrm{obs}}\)
on the same tensors, reporting a stack-mean of 0.049 with a range of
{[}0.039, 0.053{]}; only GIFT shows a borderline-significant reduction
against GRPO (p = 0.066, CI {[}−0.021, +0.001{]}), which resolves a
synthesis range of {[}0.13, 0.23{]} downwards --- the anti-herding
diversity bonus is real but smaller than the qualitative synthesis had
guessed.

The reference CLI, \texttt{registry/query.py}, implements four verbs
using only the standard library: \texttt{list};
\texttt{query\ -\/-item\ reference\_kl} returning FULL, PARTIAL or
UNREPORTED; \texttt{badge}; and \texttt{stackdiff}, which maps a pair of
entries onto the R0--R5 ladder and reports the highest rung triggered
--- R0 identical manifests, R1 nuisance-only, R2 runtime differences, R3
loss-form or KL-handling differences, R4 differing variant-delta sets,
R5 unauditable because a differing item is null on at least one side.
The badge formula weights the seven items equally and is outcome-blind:
\(\mathrm{badge}(s) = \lfloor 100 \cdot \tfrac{1}{7} \sum_i c_i(s) \rfloor\).
\texttt{stackdiff} prints a warning at R4 and above that outcome
differences must not be attributed to the shared algorithm label, and
the P6 report is careful to describe the ladder as ``a risk scale, not
an effect-size scale.'' Applied to the shipped entries,
\texttt{stackdiff} flags the open-versus-closed ``DAPO'' pair as R5 from
manifests alone, and across the twenty seed entries the MIN-REPORT badge
spans 43--96. Two boundaries deserve restating. The registry reports
stated provenance and conflicts; it does not reconcile unresolved
exact-ID or model conflicts, nor does it validate nominal algorithm
comparisons. And the badge is coarse relative to the corpus --- twenty
entries collapse to ten distinct fingerprints, and twenty of
twenty-three sub-fields carry at most two values.

\section{From Diagnostic to Controller
(P7)}\label{from-diagnostic-to-controller-p7}

P7 asks a narrower question than P5 and P6. Given that the zero-variance
fraction is worth reporting, is it mechanistically predictable, and is
it ready to control rollout allocation? The P7 report's own framing is
that this does not overturn the descriptive restraint of the earlier
work --- it \emph{tests} ZVF's predictive value through falsifiable
predictions rather than assuming it --- and it flags at the outset that
the weakest link in the chain, the causal connection from within-group
contrast to gradient magnitude, rests on toy-scale directional evidence
only.

The theory is compact. Fix a prompt with latent per-rollout success
probability p and a group of G conditionally independent Bernoulli
rewards. Write \(h_G(p) = p^G + (1-p)^G\) for the probability that the
group is degenerate, and model the usable signal per prompt as
\(S(p,G) = p(1-p)(1-h_G(p))\). The first factor is the contrast a
non-degenerate group can carry; the second is the probability that any
signal survives centring. Both factors vanish at the endpoints and peak
at p = ½, so this proposed signal proxy is largest at intermediate
latent success probability and vanishes at the endpoints; it is not a
measured law of learning speed. Three consequences follow, each paired
with the measurement that could have falsified it. T1 states that the
expected ZVF indicator is exactly \(h_G(p)\). T2 states that \(h_G\) is
strictly decreasing in G for 0 \textless{} p \textless{} 1, with
exponential decay rate −ln max(p, 1−p). T3 states that with unnormalised
group-centred advantages the mean absolute advantage in a group is
exactly 2\ensuremath{\hat{p}}(1−\ensuremath{\hat{p}}).

The checks have different evidential status. Binning by the same group's
empirical success rate is an algebraic identity, not an independent
validation of latent-p predictions. For T1, binning the per-group reward
tensors of the GSM8K runs by empirical success rate gives a ZVF
indicator averaging 1.000 at \ensuremath{\hat{p}} ∈ \{0, 1\} and 0.000 at every interior
bin; at run level, measured ZVF sits \emph{above} \(h_G(\bar p)\) at
every G, which the P7 report attributes to the Jensen gap --- the
binomial model evaluates \(h_G\) at the mean \(p\) while the prompt
population can mix different latent success probabilities. The Jensen
gap explains only part of it. On the repository sweep the measured ZVF
also sits above the per-step-average null, which already absorbs the
across-step variation in p: 0.838 against 0.828 at G = 2, 0.764 against
0.709, 0.691 against 0.597 and 0.631 against 0.522 at G = 16, recomputed
from \longtok{groupsize\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}sweep.json}. This is the same baseline used
in Chapter 6 §6.3.3 and Chapter 4 §4.3. For T2, two independent grids
agree. The 368-run audit shows monotone decline for every model with an
interior success rate (Qwen3-32B 0.33 → 0.18 → 0.08; Llama-3.1-8B 0.76 →
0.65 → 0.47), with the two boundary rows behaving as the theory flags
them: Llama-3.2-1B sits near p ≈ 0 where \(h_G \approx 1\) regardless of
G (0.98 → 0.97), and Qwen3-4B-Instruct sits near saturation where the
decay rate is tiny and seed noise dominates, producing the single
non-monotone cell (0.89 → 0.84 → 0.91). The repository-internal sweep at
G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\} shows the same decline, with mean ZVF falling from
0.838 to 0.631 while held-out accuracy stays essentially flat near 0.98
throughout --- raising G buys signal, not, on this near-saturated task,
outcome. T3 cannot be checked on the closed stack at all, because it
does not expose per-group gradients; the open-backward-pass measurement,
corr(‖∇‖, \ensuremath{\hat{p}}(1−\ensuremath{\hat{p}})) = +0.71 on a 0.5B model with synthetic arithmetic, is
reported strictly as directional evidence.

Because ZVF aliases mastery with incapacity, the P7 report promotes the
pairwise contrast density PCD = ((G−1)/G)·E{[}p(1−p){]} as the signal to
control on. The micro-jitter experiment in §7.1 motivates recording the
metric definition: the exact-tie fraction falls from 0.158 to 0.000
while PCD is nearly unchanged, but \(\mathrm{ZVF}_{10^{-6}}\) remains
0.158. This experiment alone does not establish that PCD is a better
control signal than tolerance-based ZVF. The controller's design follows
from the theory: a \texttt{zvf-triage} callback watches per-step ZVF
with PCD as an aliasing guard, and an adaptive-G controller escalates
group size on ZVF spikes, changing only the sampler budget so that it
composes with any group-relative objective.

The four-arm open-trainer audit is the first place where the
intervention is measured against alternatives on the same data, budget
and seeds. Against a 120-rollout baseline, GRPO gains +0.500 held-out at
mean ZVF 0.25; Dr.~GRPO gains +0.575 at 0.27 on the same 120 rollouts;
DAPO-style dynamic sampling gains +0.550 while driving ZVF to 0.00 at
174 rollouts, a +45\% cost; and adaptive-G gains +0.575 while lowering
mean ZVF only modestly to 0.23 at 186 rollouts. Three readings are
stated. Telemetry behaves as the mechanisms dictate. Dynamic sampling
buys perfect telemetry at a 45\% rollout premium and does not exceed the
cheaper fixed recipe. Adaptive-G spends comparable overhead and ties the
best held-out result, concentrating its spend on flagged steps --- and
the tie is small-n and single-task, so the description is ``competitive
with, not superior to, the best fixed recipe.''

The controller simulation then asks a counterfactual question on the
real reward tensors: for each of the 2,560 prompt-step decisions in the
four-method run, when would each controller have fired, what group size
would it have chosen, and how much contrast would it have restored? Its
zero-rescue result is conditional on the plug-in estimator, rather than
evidence that fresh rollouts cannot recover contrast. Across all four
methods and all five thresholds of the point-estimate rule, the
controllers save \emph{zero} prompts. Of GRPO's 640 prompt-step pairs,
461 have identical observed rewards --- 427 all-correct and 34 all-wrong
--- with \ensuremath{\hat{p}} at exactly 0 or 1, and the counts are near-identical for the
other methods (AERO 461, GIFT 493, AREAL 452). Substituting \ensuremath{\hat{p}} ∈ \{0, 1\}
into \(h_G\) forces the estimated degeneration probability to one for
every G. Resampling the same eight identical observed rewards to size G
= 16 also cannot create an unobserved outcome: the resulting 0/1,867
rescue count is mechanical. Neither calculation identifies latent p as 0
or 1. These traces contain predominantly all-correct groups, but
independent fresh responses at larger G from the historical panel are
needed to estimate how often new outcomes restore contrast there. The
separate frozen-checkpoint diagnostic in §7.4 observes new responses
under a different, explicitly fixed contract; it does not reproduce this
historical panel. The operating range where the trigger fires
selectively is τ ∈ {[}0.70, 0.80{]}, 12 to 19 fires per 40 steps at a
cost ratio of 1.30 to 1.48, and the cost differences are retrospective
budget accounting under that estimator.

In the same trace the escalation asymmetry is stark: 1,723 of 1,867
escalation events (92.3\%) occur on all-correct groups and only 144 on
all-wrong groups. A symmetric ZVF trigger mostly spends on prompts whose
eight observed completions were all correct; that is not proof of
certain success on future draws. The P7 report is explicit that deleting
those fires is a counterfactual proposal only, because performance
preservation has not been measured.

Two refinements sharpen the picture and then complicate it. The Bayesian
controller replaces the point estimate with a Beta(1,1) posterior and a
mid-range probability m(k, G) = Pr{[}0.05 ≤ p ≤ 0.95 \textbar{} k, G{]},
escalating when m exceeds a posterior threshold. At τ\_post = 0.60 it
fires on the same 466.75 degenerate prompt-steps as the point-estimate
rule at τ = 0.50 --- with a 95\% bootstrap CI of {[}454.25, 485.00{]}
--- but at strictly lower cost, 8,854 rollouts against 10,240, a 14\%
saving; and all 466.75 of those mean fires are on observed all-equal
groups, so the plug-in rescue calculation again reports zero; this is
not a posterior-predictive zero-rescue guarantee. Above τ\_post = 0.65
it never fires, because the maximum m over degenerate observations is
0.630.

The posterior-predictive eight-controller comparison produces a
model-based metric reversal with bootstrap CIs attached. Its
contrast-restoration estimates depend on the assumed posterior and are
not observed outcomes from fresh rollouts; these intervals do not
validate that assumption. On the cost axis the Pareto winner is
Dualformer-Auto, an automatic difficulty-gated de-escalation rule, at a
mean cost ratio of 0.657 {[}0.599, 0.738{]} against 1.000 for fixed G =
8 --- a 34\% rollout saving, achieved because the prompts it shrinks to
G = 2 would have been degenerate at G = 8 anyway. On a different axis,
contrast restored per 1,000 extra rollouts, the ordering reverses:
\texttt{zvf-triage} at τ = 0.50 restores 90.10 units per 1,000 extra
rollouts {[}89.40, 90.77{]}, against 86.68 for τ = 0.70, 84.00 for
Dualformer-Auto and 80.00 for the Bayesian rule, and all four pairwise
differences have CIs excluding zero. The Bayesian controller ---
Pareto-dominant on the earlier axis --- is dominated here, because its
principled prior fires on the hardest-to-restore degenerate prompts. The
regime decomposition explains the mechanism rather than leaving it as a
ranking: \texttt{zvf-triage} at τ = 0.50 fires on 100\% of steps in
every regime on this panel, so it is operationally equivalent to
``escalate every step,'' and its apparent advantage is mechanical
exposure rather than a smarter rule. Its restoration decomposes 64.76\%
degenerate, 13.69\% boundary and 21.56\% mid-range; the non-degenerate
35.24\% share is what the Bayesian rule sacrifices.

Two further analyses bound what this family of shrink-only rules can
achieve. A per-prompt hindsight-optimal calculation scans G' down from a
base of 8 and finds a bimodal optimum: G* = 8 for 72.9\% of the 2,560
prompt-steps, which are observed all-equal groups assigned zero rescue
by the plug-in model, and G* = 2 for the remaining 27.1\%, which are
already non-degenerate at G = 8 and are cheapest to shrink. Mean G* is
6.376, giving a cost ratio of 0.797 {[}0.776, 0.818{]} --- a 20.3\%
saving that is pure economy, since mean contrast restored per prompt is
0.000 and mean contrast lost to shrinking is 0.130 ZVF units. This
hindsight optimum is not a ceiling on cost (corrected; see Table E.1):
Dualformer-Auto's observed 0.66 to 0.68 on the four-bin rule is
\emph{cheaper}. The two optimise different objectives. The hindsight
calculation keeps every already-degenerate prompt at G* = 8 and only
shrinks non-degenerate ones, whereas Dualformer-Auto also shrinks
prompts that would have been degenerate at G = 8. The 0.797 is therefore
the best cost ratio under a no-shrinking-of-saturated-prompts
constraint, not a lower bound on cost for any adaptive rule. A
finite-pool correction then shows that even this bound is loose: because
a finite pool never collides more than independent sampling, the exact
hypergeometric contrast-preservation probability exceeds the binomial
one everywhere, by +0.0494 {[}+0.0481, +0.0506{]} on the 693 relevant
prompt-steps, which means the binomial model over-predicts starvation
and the ``zero contrast restored'' accounting understates true preserved
contrast by roughly 34.2 ZVF units.

Cross-base replication is where the retreat from ``controller'' to
``audit'' is earned. On a second panel of five independent seeds, the
trigger fires 4.20 times per seed {[}3.00, 5.40{]} at τ = 0.70, with the
per-step rate falling from 0.48 to 0.28 as the panel's overall ZVF
drops; what replicates is the sign and the operating-point logic, not a
fixed firing magnitude. The Bayesian branch is entirely silent on this
panel, firing 0.0 times per seed, because every step's implied mid-range
probability lies in {[}0.95, 0.999{]} --- a real negative finding that
scopes the Bayesian controller to distributions containing boundary
steps. And the aggregate firing rate is stable while the \emph{location}
is not: at τ = 0.70 with a de-escalation threshold of 0.95, the
cross-seed Jaccard overlap of the fire sets is 0.133, only 13.3\% of
steps see all five seeds choose the same group size, and the mean
cross-seed Kendall-τ of per-step ZVF trajectories is −0.060. The firing
rate is seed-stable; the firing location is not, and the training-step
index is a meaningless axis for the ZVF signal across seeds. The
replayed unification confirms the direction: on this panel
Dualformer-Auto is the only controller that saves compute (+14\%
{[}0.10, 0.18{]}) while \texttt{zvf-triage} spends more than baseline
(−28\% {[}−0.36, −0.20{]}), with disjoint CIs.

Five design rules close the P7 report, and they read as constraints
discovered rather than principles asserted. Gate on PCD and alarm on
ZVF: escalate only when ZVF is high \emph{and} PCD indicates interior
difficulty, because high ZVF with near-zero PCD and high reward is
consistent with mastery --- where \(h_G(p) \approx 1 - G\varepsilon\)
near the boundary and added rollouts buy order ε --- while high ZVF with
near-zero reward is consistent with incapacity; finite observations
alone do not identify either endpoint, so the proposed choice between
data intervention and resampling still needs prospective validation.
Escalate multiplicatively in small steps, since at p = ½ each additional
rollout halves the degenerate fraction; the audited 4 → 6 → 8 schedule
at multipliers of 1.5 and 1.33 captured most of the achievable reduction
at a total cost of +55\% rollouts, whereas G = 16 costs four times the
rollouts of G = 4 for a ZVF drop from 0.76 to 0.63 and no held-out gain
on a near-saturated task. Trigger on spikes rather than levels, because
ZVF is temporally sticky with a lag-1 autocorrelation of approximately
0.94 and drifts upward over training, so a level threshold fires either
never or permanently. Never compare controllers across stacks; ship the
seven manifest fields with item 5 carrying the escalation log. And treat
ZVF = 0 as a purchase rather than a prize --- dynamic sampling shows
perfect telemetry is always available at a 45\% rollout premium, and on
this task it was not the best use of the marginal rollout.

The chapter's summary of P7 is the report's own. No result establishes
prospective superiority over static G = 16, Dr.~GRPO, or dynamic
sampling; adaptive group size is explicitly \emph{not} promoted to a
recommended policy; and all intervention evidence is single-task,
small-n, or retrospective. The report's contribution is a retrospective
controller audit together with a pre-registered prospective test plan,
and the bakeoff that would close the question --- a matched-budget,
multi-seed held-out comparison against the strong static baseline --- is
future work rather than a claim. In the same spirit, the report flags
its own August 2026 evidence boundary: unsigned ZVF alone cannot select
between retiring, distilling, retrying, resampling or warm-starting a
prompt, so any operator policy remains a prospective evaluation contract
rather than a demonstrated benefit.

\section{Historical V2 Frozen-Policy Fresh-Rollout
Diagnostic}\label{historical-v2-frozen-policy-fresh-rollout-diagnostic}

\textbf{Format-induced contrast is distinct from numeric contrast.} A
prospectively frozen diagnostic completed on 2 October 2026 found that
33 of 461 initially homogeneous eight-response groups became mixed after
24 additional responses: 7.16\%, with a prompt-level Wilson 95\%
interval of 5.14-9.88\%. Twenty of those first reward changes were
parser failures whose visible final number still agreed with the gold
answer. The remaining 13/461 (2.82\%) were assistant-audited
numeric-correctness contrasts: two wrong-to-correct recoveries and
eleven correct-to-wrong changes. Thus the raw 7.16\% measures variation
in this diagnostic's binary reward, not a useful learning-signal rate or
an accuracy gain.

The main cohort contained 512 GSM8K \textbf{train} prompts, each with 32
ordered responses from a frozen public Qwen/Qwen3-8B checkpoint in BF16
non-thinking mode. All 16,384 main responses completed. A disjoint
32-prompt smoke cohort was excluded from every main estimate. Each draw
was a fresh response to the same original prompt, with a unique request
seed, rather than a continuation of a previous answer. The primary
endpoint was predeclared: among prompts whose first eight binary rewards
all agreed, count those with both rewards in the combined 32. The strict
parser used exact rational matching; parser failures received reward
zero, while cap-hit outputs retained their exact-match reward and were
separately flagged. Neither kind of output was removed from the primary
denominator. Appendix B §B.12 gives the pinned revisions, decoder,
receipt chain and audit method.

The main corpus had 290/16,384 parser failures (1.77\%) and 18/16,384
cap hits (0.11\%), each below the predeclared 5\% quality threshold.
These global quality checks do not make a parser-induced reward change a
numeric error. Separate assistant reviewers reviewed all 33 initial
groups and first-changing responses in disjoint partitions, solving the
arithmetic and applying the frozen rubric; this was model-assisted
qualitative review, \textbf{not human annotation}. A coordinator
separately checked both recoveries and one bread-rise wording ambiguity.
That question's gold answer, 20 hours, assumes serial rises; it is
ambiguous rather than definitely wrong and was already excluded from the
numeric subset for a formatting failure. The two recoveries changed
eight initial answers of 1152 to the supported value
\(6(150+6\times30+6\times12)=2412\), and eight initial answers of 12 to
the supported coin total \(2+5+3.25+2+0.4+0.35=13\).

The predeclared clean-all-32 sensitivity was 12/439 (2.73\%). Because
eligibility requires all 32 outputs to be parseable and uncapped, it
selects on post-initial outcomes and cannot replace the primary
estimate. A \textbf{post-hoc exploratory} check found no later
parseable, uncapped numeric change in any of the 20 parser-first groups;
it leaves the frozen first-event audit unchanged and cannot predict
further draws.

This parser effect is \textbf{diagnostic-contract-specific}. The
repository's existing GSM8K test evaluator uses a more permissive
extractor, including numeric conclusions embedded in prose. These
strict-parser events are therefore not evidence of reward failures in
the historical evaluator or training runs. The 13 conservative numeric
witnesses show that eight equal observed rewards need not imply a fixed
numeric outcome on fresh draws for this checkpoint, task and decoder.
They do not reconstruct a historical checkpoint, demonstrate training or
controller benefit, identify a group-size optimum, or estimate held-out
capability. The earlier failed V1 engineering smoke remains separate and
unpooled.

\section{C1 Results with Reference and Unit
Boundaries}\label{c1-results-with-reference-and-unit-boundaries}

Benchmark reference errors, ambiguous questions and equivalent units
limit what this diagnostic can establish. Its frozen parser-defined
result is \textbf{11/64 questions (17.19\%; Wilson 95\% interval
9.88-28.21\%)}. Assistant-assisted reconciliation corroborates
\textbf{three numeric recovery witnesses on the same 64-question
denominator}. This 3/64 count (4.69\%) describes the support found under
a conservative review rule; it does not replace the frozen endpoint or
estimate a population recovery rate. The other eight parser events
include three convention-dependent interpretations, four materially
ambiguous cases and one match to an incorrect reference answer.
{[}S1-S4{]}

The study contains no training update. It does not measure useful
reinforcement-learning gradients, reasoning improvement, controller
superiority, held-out accuracy or general model capability. The prompts
are public GSM8K training questions and are fresh only to this campaign.
Historical V2 evidence remains separate and unpooled.

The three corroborated witnesses are Santana presents (C00, source rank
676), Suzanne bread (C07, rank 912), and Hank reading (C38, rank 2699).
Santana's supported answer is \((4+7)-3=8\) presents; Suzanne's is
\(3\times2\times52/12=26\) loaves; Hank's is
\(5(30+60)+2\times2(30+60)=810\) minutes. Each has eight reviewed,
genuinely wrong initial final answers and at least one independently
correct fresh final answer. {[}S3{]}

Santana also shows why final-answer correctness cannot validate
reasoning. Its earliest successful fresh response, sample index 12,
returns eight through an incorrect argument that no first-half birthday
gifts were needed. Later successful indices 15 and 23 use the valid
\(11-3\) calculation. Hank's reference final value is supported even
though its reference explanation mislabels subtotals. These distinctions
remain visible in the event ledger. All sample and selected indices in
this addendum are zero-based. {[}S3{]}

The checkpoint case (C35, rank 2593) gives the opposite result.
Checkpoints at miles one and twenty-five enclose twenty-four miles
across three gaps, so the separation is eight miles. All eight initial
answers of eight are numerically correct. Four fresh outputs match the
erroneous reference value six by dividing by four. The frozen analysis
counts the event exactly as specified; semantic review does not call it
mathematical recovery. {[}S3{]}

The citations, raft and fundraiser cases have favorable ordinary
interpretations, but retain first-versus-second review disagreements.
They are described individually rather than combined with the three
corroborated witnesses into another adjusted recovery estimate. The
remaining four cases concern aggregation of walking distance, the
cake-and-ham probability event, the pet-owner denominator and Reggie's
missing initial marble count. A gold match does not resolve those
questions. {[}S3{]}

The original second-review categories are retained: sixteen clear
wrong-reference judgments, nineteen materially ambiguous questions, two
inconsistent questions, fifteen supported references and twelve
references supported with conventional assumptions. Refinement separates
four correct final answers with flawed reference explanations from the
supported categories, leaving the following mutually exclusive view.
{[}S2, S4{]}

A narrower cross-review analysis corroborates fifteen wrong-final-value
judgments under the stated or ordinary mathematical model. The towel
case explicitly assumes equal towel weights. Separately, two
discrete-count questions have inconsistent premises and erroneous
reference relations, three correct-final references have corroborated
text defects, and two materially ambiguous questions contain explicit
reference faults. These are \textbf{22 disjoint cases with specified
reference defects, not 22 wrong labels}. The four correct-final cases in
the refined second review and the three corroborated correct-final
defects are different sets: a notation defect newly identified for
Sonia/Joss is not corroborated in both rounds. {[}S2, S4{]}

Thirteen first/second category disagreements are retained. The coin
question illustrates the reason: gross deposits total 225 cents and the
balance after withdrawal is 200 cents. The second review judges the
recorded 200 wrong for the requested deposits, while the first retains a
gross-versus-net target ambiguity. It is excluded from the narrower
fifteen. Ordinary mathematical conventions are not defects merely
because every physical assumption is unstated; genuinely different
quantities or plausible readings are described case by case. None of
these audit counts estimates whole-GSM8K error prevalence because the
cohort was selected for eight parser-zero outputs and then truncated.
{[}S2, S4{]}

Equivalent representations also matter. For Jackson ingredients (C36),
all eight initial values 0.24 are equivalent to the reference 24 percent
under the same conventional independent-selection probability
interpretation; formal odds would be 6:19. The scalar parser extracted
0.24 faithfully. For Gunner plugs (C62), 200 pairs equal 400 individual
plugs. Four initial responses preserve pair units, three remain
unit-ambiguous and one incorrectly calls 200 the number of individual
plugs. Neither case supports a claim of eight genuine mathematical
errors. The frozen scalar references and rewards remain unchanged.
{[}S5{]}

\subsection{Separate Prospective Parser
Validation}\label{separate-prospective-parser-validation}

The undeployed parser v3 candidate was frozen at 02:43:30 UTC on 3
October 2026, before the earliest target batch at 02:59:39 UTC. The
fixed target was the last 256 screening questions, eight responses each,
yielding 2,048 future outputs. This was a predefined future subset of an
ongoing study, not preregistration before the entire C1 campaign.
Historical candidate tuning is separate. C1's primary score remains
based on frozen parser v2. {[}S8, S9{]}

All 2,025 previously valid full parse objects were preserved. Four
previously rejected outputs at four distinct prompts became accepted:
one bold answer heading, two display-box blocks and one hash-prefixed
XML answer block. Two separately blinded, output-only assistant
annotations agreed on the same unambiguous scalar in all four changed
cases. No changed acceptance was ambiguous or incorrectly extracted
under those reviews. Nineteen outputs remained rejected. {[}S8, S9{]}

This supports only a narrow pass on the four observed extraction
changes. It does not establish semantic precision for the 2,025
unchanged outputs, validate the nineteen rejects, measure task
correctness, or estimate population false-positive rates. The responses
are clustered within prompts. Four examples do not exhaust their wrapper
classes or malformed-input behavior. The candidate remains undeployed
and is not used to recompute C1 recovery. {[}S9{]}

All source labels refer to Appendix G.



\chapter{The E1--E14 Evaluation
Campaign}\label{the-e1e14-evaluation-campaign}

This chapter reports the evaluation of a single Tinker-trained actor
against fourteen benchmark suites outside its recorded training-suite
list, recorded lane by lane under sealed reservations, immutable
receipts and explicit terminal states. Item-level decontamination is not
established. Lanes that did not finish and lanes that could not start
are reported too, because the methodological aim is to make the boundary
between what was measured, what was only reported, and what can still be
verified explicit. The current repository does not preserve every cited
artifact, so it cannot support an unqualified claim that every figure
has a surviving, hash-pinned evidence chain. Lane-by-lane detail that
does not change any figure is collected in Appendix D (Figure~\ref{fig:lanes}).


\fig{fig_lane_status}
{Terminal status of all fourteen E1--E14 lanes: the 2026-09-19 ledger of record, updated with the six replacement scopes scored on 2026-09-27 (double border, $\star$)}
{Terminal status of all fourteen E1--E14 lanes: the 2026-09-19 ledger of record, updated with the six replacement scopes scored on 2026-09-27 (double border, $\star$). Each cell names the suite whose scope the state refers to, and status is carried by glyph, word, border style and hatching as well as colour. Cells show the replacement scope where one exists (E1 SWE-bench Multilingual, E2 CORE-Bench, E5 Tau3, E6 WebArena, E8 LAB-Bench public, E9 MLDevBench, E10 AgentDojo benign, E13 BALROG, E14 Omni-MATH) and the original contract otherwise; the original-contract suites and their results (e.g.\ E1 SWE-bench Pro $2/731$, E2 FrontierSWE, E5 APEX-Agents, E9 MLE-bench) are shown in the campaign-design figure (Chapter~4) and are never pooled with replacement-scope numbers. *E14 closes terminal-complete: $2271/4428 = 51.29\%$ with the two unjudged rows counted as failures (official scorer: 51.31\% over 4426 judged rows).}
{fig:lanes}



Two reporting rules govern everything below, and they are worth stating
before any number appears. First, original-contract results and
replacement-scope results are never pooled and never averaged across
suites; a replacement scope is a different suite, a different interface
and often a different grader, and combining such figures would produce a
number with no referent. Second, no figure in this chapter is quoted to
a precision beyond what its source receipt records, and no comparison
against a baseline, a sibling suite, or the untrained base model is
asserted for the campaign lanes of Sections 8.2--8.6. Those lanes have
no matched base-model arm. The one paired baseline comparison in this
chapter is the small-scale trained-versus-base rerun of Section 8.7
(Table 8.B): 14 lanes, 5 to 151 identical items each, one vLLM engine
for both arms. It finds no significant difference in any lane, and this
chapter therefore claims no improvement of any kind over the base model.
Where a lane's record itself contains an unresolved discrepancy, the
discrepancy is stated.

The consolidated ledger of record for the campaign is
\longtok{outputs/E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md}, which
supersedes the working tables of 2026-09-05 and 2026-09-12; the per-lane
terminal-state ledger,
\texttt{withheld\ historical\ campaign\ chronology}, was recovered from
the original Mac checkout on 3 October 2026 with updates through 21
September, while remaining absent from the frozen repository; and
selected arithmetic of the ledger was historically re-verified by
deterministic code checks recorded in
\longtok{outputs/verification/LEDGER\_\allowbreak{}CODE\_\allowbreak{}CHECK\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.json},
which reports eleven of eleven named checks passing with zero failures
(Appendix B lists their scope; they do not cover every figure in this
chapter).

\textbf{Evidence availability (private recovery audit, 3 October 2026).}
Six original files covering all five previously missing basenames were
recovered from the Mac checkout: the E10 native receipt, E8 diagnostics,
training-identity verification, E14 terminal note and two distinct
\texttt{Pending\_Experiments.md} snapshots. All remain absent from the
frozen repository, but their exact UTF-8 bytes and SHA-256 hashes are
preserved in the withheld source archive described in Appendix B §B.11.
A separately retained source audit records bounded read-only checks of
underlying E8/E10/E14 artifacts on the Mac: 29 exact reference-path hash
matches, 1,979 E8 archive/local member checks, 525 E10 archive-member
checks and E14 stored-disposition inspection. Exported E8 grade records
and E10 selected trace records permitted independent tally checks in the
retained private evidence audit; those underlying rows are not included
in this public document package; E14 exports aggregate traversal results
and two exclusion records, not all underlying rows. This verifies stored
evidence and arithmetic within that scope, without rerunning models or
scorers, rejudging correctness, or reconstructing the missing training
logs. It supersedes the earlier missing-primary disclosure for these
files only; the frozen-checkout absence record remains valid. E14's
recovered note contains a denominator inconsistency, and the recovered
finish ledger does not substantiate the later E4 rerun; both are
disclosed below.

\section{The evaluated actor and the one-actor
design}\label{the-evaluated-actor-and-the-one-actor-design}

A single actor was evaluated across the whole campaign. The base model
is \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at revision
\longtok{995ad96eacd98c81ed38be0c5b274b04031597b0}, and the trained
parameter set is the LoRA adapter \texttt{campaign-adapter} at commit
\texttt{withheld-adapter-revision}, served in bfloat16 under the
identifier \texttt{campaign-actor-bf16}. The public package does not
contain the original execution route or operational repository access
identities. It therefore cannot reproduce the original sampler execution
from these materials alone; no current provider-retention or
account-status claim is made here.

The one-actor design is deliberate. No lane received a separate
checkpoint, per-suite prompt engineering beyond its native harness, or
tuning against its own evaluation set: the recovered training-identity
record lists only API-Bank-RLVR and SWE-Gym (§8.1.1), while the
recovered E10 receipt explicitly reports
\longtok{TRAINING\_\allowbreak{}INVENTORY\_\allowbreak{}ABSENT} and makes no held-out claim. The
E10 contract's declaration of AgentDojo training is explicitly marked as
not evidence of training consumption; neither record proves
content-level decontamination. Differences between suites therefore
reflect the interaction of one fixed policy with fourteen different
interfaces, observation spaces, tool conventions and graders, and they
are not evidence about what a specialised actor could achieve. The
campaign also cannot support any statement about seed variance.
Inference conditions were frozen per lane and recorded in that lane's
own receipt (§8.1.2).

\subsection{How the actor was trained}\label{how-the-actor-was-trained}

The adapter is attributed to a single Tinker training run (W\&B run
\texttt{withheld-id}). The recovered
\longtok{training\_\allowbreak{}identity\_\allowbreak{}verified.json} records that run ID,
aggregate dataset revision, start time, base Git commit and hashes of
\texttt{config.yaml}, \texttt{wandb-metadata.json} and
\texttt{output.log}. Its status is
\longtok{TRAINING\_\allowbreak{}CONFIG\_\allowbreak{}VERIFIED\_\allowbreak{}NOT\_\allowbreak{}CONTENT\_\allowbreak{}DECONTAMINATION}, and
it states that the dirty source diff was not uploaded. The underlying
W\&B files and local run receipt have not been recovered in this
package, so their bytes cannot yet be matched afresh to those recorded
hashes. Appendix D separates these surviving identity fields from the
still-reported configuration details in Table 8.1.

\textbf{Reported training record of the E1--E14 actor.} The
identity-verification artifact is recovered; underlying local run files
remain unavailable in this package. Current-code evidence is labelled
separately.

Four items remain unavailable or were never created --- the exact
training code, which 80 training prompts were drawn, the Tinker sampler
weights, and any held-out training-time evaluation --- and the run
departs from the planned Pavlov protocol; both are recorded in Appendix
D so that no reader fills them in.

What this means for the chapter is direct. The actor received 40 steps
of sparse-reward, critic-free policy-gradient training, with zero loss
on 35 of them. The fourteen suite results are properties of this
checkpoint, not evidence about what GRPO-style training contributes. The
campaign is reported as an exercise in evaluation governance. The only
trained-versus-base evidence is the small-sample paired comparison of
Table 8.B.

\subsection{Serving conditions per
lane}\label{serving-conditions-per-lane}

The E4 zero (§8.5) prompted identification of a serving bridge that sent
no stop sequences. The defect can cause failed agent trajectories, but
its contribution to the full 100-trial zero is not identified by the
available comparison. The same bridge,
\longtok{zvf-program/flagship/modal\_\allowbreak{}tinker\_\allowbreak{}openai\_\allowbreak{}bridge.py}, served
several other lanes, so Table 8.2 records for every lane how the actor
was served and whether that defect could apply. Its
\texttt{/v1/responses} route hard-codes \texttt{stop=None} and thinking
on. Its \texttt{/v1/chat/completions} route sends stop sequences only if
the client supplies them, and the agent clients used here (opencode,
mini-swe-agent and the APEX client) do not. The deployed bridge version
(sha256 \texttt{f59c08b9…}) matches neither committed version of the
file.

The recovered E8 diagnostic now supports its aggregate failure counts,
and the recovered E10 receipt supports its declared inference limits.
Other entries still depend on unrecovered raw responses or finish-era
sources, so recovery of these two receipts does not verify every serving
and failure count in the table. Two consequences follow. First, the
lanes served through the bridge (E2, E4, E5, E7 and the E9 streaming
arm) may be exposed to the E4 mechanism, but exposure does not
demonstrate that it caused their failures or quantify its contribution
to E4. Second, the reported format and length failures mean that E8, E11
and E14 headline figures are end-to-end harness scores; §8.2
distinguishes their sensitivity figures and missing evidence. For E11
and E14 the dominant failure is the generation budget, and for E1 it is
patch formatting: those three scores measure the harness configuration
as much as the actor (§8.2, §8.3). A note on the scope of the
stop-sequence mechanism is in Appendix D.

\section{Suites with strictly complete
scopes}\label{suites-with-strictly-complete-scopes}

The historical ledger and its check log report three strictly complete
scopes: E8, E10 and E11. E8 and E10 are replacement scopes. E11 is an
original-contract full-suite result that also meets the
strict-completeness check, because the suite evaluated is VerilogEval
itself; the ledger lists it both among its replacement scopes and among
its original-contract results, but it is one run and one result. A
fourth, E14, is recorded terminal-complete under its native protocol in
the recovered terminal note, subject to its denominator inconsistency
and the raw-output limits below. The six replacement scopes completed on
27 September postdate that check and are reported separately in §8.4 (Figures~\ref{fig:e8} and~\ref{fig:e11}).

{LAB-Bench public split, per-category accuracy, all 1{,}967 questions graded across eight categories}
{LAB-Bench public split, per-category accuracy, all 1{,}967 questions graded across eight categories. Each bar is correct/total for one category (labels give the counts). Accuracy is strongly category-dependent, so no pooled cross-category average is drawn or claimed. CloningScenarios is a historically reported zero (0/33). The original diagnostic file was recovered and byte-verified on 3 October (Appendix B); its aggregate counts agree with these bars.}
{fig:e8}



\fig{fig_e11_decomposition}

{VerilogEval decomposition: two native framings of 156 tasks, 67/156 code-completion and 62/156 spec-to-RTL, summing to $129/312 = 41.35\%$ pass@1. The dashed top bar is the sum of the two rows, not a third measurement. Graded by the suite's native harness (receipt \longtok{e11\_\allowbreak{}full\_\allowbreak{}receipt.json}); the $129/311 = 41.48\%$ value that excludes \longtok{Prob099\_\allowbreak{}m2014\_\allowbreak{}q6c} is a non-canonical sensitivity only.}
