\textbf{E8 --- LAB-Bench, public split.} The recovered
\longtok{outputs/public\_\allowbreak{}portfolio\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-05/labbench\_\allowbreak{}final\_\allowbreak{}diagnostics.json}
records all 1,967 expected items evaluated across eight categories, with
450 correct and 1,517 incorrect responses. Those counts give 450/1967 =
0.2288 (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.211, 0.248{]}, unit = question, n = 1,967).
It also records 1,259 unparsed responses
(\longtok{native\_\allowbreak{}answer\_\allowbreak{}parse\_\allowbreak{}none}) and 1,086 length-truncated
finishes. The supplementary validation found the native receipt at its
recorded hash and checked 1,979 archive members against both declared
and local hashes. Its 1,967 unique exported grade records independently
corroborate the correct/incorrect totals, category counts and 1,086
length finishes. The 1,259 parse-none count remains a field of the
byte-preserved diagnostic: the exported grade records contain no
parsed-answer field for a separate recount. The reported score is an
end-to-end harness score with substantial formatting and length
limitations. The conditional descriptive sensitivity remains 450/708 =
0.636 (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.600, 0.670{]}), not the headline or a direct
measure of domain knowledge; its response-level classification has not
been independently regraded. Per-category figures and evidence limits
are in Appendix D.

\textbf{E10 --- AgentDojo, benign utility scope.} The recovered
\longtok{outputs/public\_\allowbreak{}portfolio\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-05/agentdojo\_\allowbreak{}native\_\allowbreak{}receipt.json}
records status \longtok{COMPLETE\_\allowbreak{}NATIVE\_\allowbreak{}BENIGN\_\allowbreak{}UTILITY}, 97 completed
episodes, 88 utility passes, AgentDojo v1.2.2 and decontamination status
\longtok{TRAINING\_\allowbreak{}INVENTORY\_\allowbreak{}ABSENT}. Its score is 88/97 = 0.9072
(Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.833, 0.950{]}, unit = episode, n = 97). The receipt
binds the benchmark version but contains no upstream commit field; the
previously attributed commit
\longtok{089ed468cf3ed0322acc66b0211f26d9d90dbf60} is therefore not
verified by this receipt. Its scope is native default benign utility
only: \texttt{security\_evaluation} and \texttt{heldout\_claim} are
false, while \texttt{attack} and \texttt{defense} are null. Completion
and utility are distinct: 97 completed episodes do not mean 97 correct
episodes. The supplementary validation checks 525 archived member pins
and maps all 97 selected episodes to unique exact-hash traces (94
original and 3 explicit continuation), whose stored utility fields
independently tally to 88 passes and zero handled errors. It excludes
extra historical partial traces from that mapping. This is stored-trace
corroboration, not a rerun of AgentDojo or an independent judgement of
task correctness. The earlier 94-episode attempt and reported collector
repair retain their separate missing-source limitations in Appendix D.

\textbf{E11 --- VerilogEval, two native framings.} All 312 pinned prompt
instances were evaluated under the upstream NVlabs/verilog-eval harness,
but they represent 156 underlying designs, each appearing in
code-completion (\texttt{code-complete-iccad2023}) and
specification-to-RTL framing. The descriptive result remains 129 passes
of 312 = 0.4135, decomposed into 67/156 = 0.4295 for code-completion and
62/156 = 0.3974 for specification-to-RTL. Because paired framings share
a design, a Wilson interval treating all 312 outcomes as independent is
inappropriate. Resampling the 156 design pairs together gives a 95\%
percentile cluster-bootstrap interval of {[}0.343, 0.487{]} (200,000
replicates, seed 20261002). This describes design-composition
uncertainty under an exchangeable-design assumption for this checkpoint
and these fixed sampled generations; it is not training-seed,
decoding-seed or protocol uncertainty. The observed full
pinned-benchmark fraction itself remains descriptive. The surviving
receipt records 150 extraction failures included in the denominator, so
129/312 is an end-to-end harness score: 150 of its 183 failures are
extraction failures. Those failures are better attributed to the
generation budget than to answer format (corrected; see Table E.1). The
run used a 1,024-token cap with the chat template's default thinking
mode on, and the receipt's 312,622 response tokens give a mean of about
1,002 per sample, so nearly every response hit the cap, most of them
before closing the reasoning block. On the 50 items of the 26 September
small-scale rerun (§8.7), the receipt's verdicts give 23/50, while the
same trained adapter with thinking off and a 4,096-token cap scores
33/50 with no extraction failures (11 items pass only in the second run
and 1 only in the first; exact McNemar p ≈ 0.006). That comparison also
changes engine and temperature (managed sampler at 0.2 against vLLM at
0) and covers 50 of 312 prompts, so it shows the direction and rough
size of the budget effect rather than a corrected score. The extractor
also accepted a module found inside an unterminated reasoning block, and
per-sample response text was not kept, so some of the 162 extracted
candidates, and possibly some passes, may be drafts from the reasoning
rather than final answers. The parseable-only descriptive sensitivity is
129/162 = 0.796, hard to interpret for that reason; no interval is given
because the receipt's aggregate extraction count does not provide the
per-design extraction indicators needed for a clustered conditional
interval. One prompt,
\longtok{verilog\_eval/spec-to-rtl/Prob099\_\allowbreak{}m2014\_\allowbreak{}q6c}, fails against
its own test bench under both simulators; excluding it yields 129/311 =
0.4148, but no immutable justification for the exclusion was captured,
so the raw 129/312 is canonical and 129/311 is a noncanonical
sensitivity only. Pair counts, the bootstrap method, harness revisions,
verdict rule and amendment are in Appendix D.

\textbf{E14 --- Omni-MATH replacement scope, receipt-recorded
terminal-complete.} The surviving ledger and historical arithmetic check
record 4,426 accepted rows of 4,428 and official accuracy
0.5131043831902395, consistent with 2,271/4,426. Retaining 2,271 passes
but counting both unjudged rows as failures gives 2,271/4,428 = 51.29
per cent (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.498, 0.528{]}), 0.02 percentage points
below the judged-row rate. The recovered
\longtok{outputs/PES\_\allowbreak{}Phase2\_\allowbreak{}Review\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-12/finish/e14\_\allowbreak{}terminal\_\allowbreak{}note\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.json}
records truncated judge outputs before an Equivalence Judgement section,
two recorded skip reasons, no re-judge and terminal completion at 4,428
dispositions. The note nevertheless labels \texttt{2271/4428} as
\texttt{0.5131043831902395}, a denominator inconsistency: that decimal
equals 2,271/4,426, not 2,271/4,428 (corrected; see Table E.1). The
original note is preserved unchanged, and this thesis retains both
clearly labelled arithmetic conventions. The supplementary Mac-side
traversal found the stored disposition, judged-row and
new-judge-response files with 4,428 valid JSON rows apiece. It reports
4,426 accepted dispositions, 2,271 correct and two skipped, and exports
both exclusion records with missing Equivalence Judgement headings. It
also records the native score's explicit 4,426 denominator and the
separate all-disposition diagnostic. The full E14 rows are not exported
here, so the cloud check verifies the exported arithmetic and exclusions
rather than independently recounting all 4,428 rows or rejudging any
mathematics. The headline is strongly shaped by the 2,048-token actor
cap (corrected reading; see Table E.1). Recounting the stored
dispositions, the 2,982 responses that hit the cap are correct in 1,029
cases (34.5\%), against 1,242 of the 1,446 uncapped responses (85.9\%).
The split is confounded by difficulty, since harder problems both run
longer and fail more, so it does not identify what a larger budget would
score; it does mean 51.29\% should not be read as the actor's Omni-MATH
capability independent of its generation budget.

\section{Original-contract results}\label{original-contract-results}

Seven lanes have an original-contract figure, reported separately from
any replacement scope with its coverage caveats; two are full-suite
results. Lane detail is in Appendix D.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{E1 --- SWE-bench Pro.} 2 of 731 tasks resolved, a pass@1 of
  2/731 = 0.00274, or 0.274 per cent (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.08\%,
  0.99\%{]}, unit = task), over the complete 731-row test split of
  \texttt{ScaleAI/SWE-bench\_Pro}. The fourteen generation failures and
  four lost artifacts stay in the denominator. The 713 ``native
  evaluations'' are not 713 tests of a candidate fix (corrected reading;
  see Table E.1). Parsed with \texttt{git\ apply}, 593 of the 713
  submitted patches are rejected as corrupt, because their \texttt{@@}
  hunk headers state line counts that do not match the hunk bodies; the
  official entry script runs a plain \texttt{git\ apply} and then the
  tests, so for those 593 the tests ran against the unpatched
  repository. With \texttt{git\ apply\ -\/-recount}, 711 of 713 parse.
  Both resolved instances are among the 120 patches that parse. The
  2/731 therefore mostly measures whether the actor could write
  arithmetically consistent diff headers, not whether it could solve the
  issues, and it is not a measure of problem-solving ability.
\item
  \textbf{E2 --- FrontierSWE.} 1 of 17 tasks evaluated,
  replay-normalised score 0.8628; partial, because revision-bound
  authorisation for the remaining sixteen was never obtained.
\item
  \textbf{E4 --- BankerToolBench.} 1 of 100 tasks evaluated, recovery
  metric 0.3115 --- a verifier recovery metric on a single task, not the
  simple fraction 37/128 and not a suite score; the pass3/pass16 archive
  is missing.
\item
  \textbf{E5 --- APEX-Agents.} 7 of 480 tasks natively scored. The
  prefix mean of 0.050505 treats unscored attempts as zero and is not a
  native 480-task score; the seven scored tasks average 0.079365.
\item
  \textbf{E7 --- BinaryAudit.} One of 46 attempts recorded, verifier
  reward 0.0 after an agent error, no grade.
\item
  \textbf{E9 --- MLE-bench.} 40 of 75 competitions carry native grades,
  a coverage of 53.33 per cent, and the suite score is null; a separate
  merged-vLLM arm holds one valid grade (H\&M, 0.02132) and is not
  combined.
\item
  \textbf{E11 --- VerilogEval} is reported in §8.2; it is the second
  original-contract full-suite result, alongside E1, and not an
  additional one.
\end{itemize}

No lane here has a matched base-model run, so none licenses a claim that
the actor outperforms its untrained counterpart, a predecessor model, or
a published number.

\section{Replacement-scope lanes completed on 27 September
2026}\label{replacement-scope-lanes-completed-on-27-september-2026}

At the 19 September ledger of record, six replacement scopes were
started but incomplete: E1 (SWE-bench Multilingual, 35 of 300 graded),
E2 (CORE-Bench, 45 capsule setups, no grades), E5 (Tau3, 20 of 97
cleaned) and E13 (BALROG, 13 of 255 episodes) were launch-pending, and
E6 (WebArena, 0 of 812) and E9 (MLDevBench, 0 of 34) were
\texttt{PENDING\_QUOTA} because required execution capacity was
unavailable. All six were run to completion on 27 September (Figure~\ref{fig:replacement}).

(b) E13 BALROG reports native progression, not a success rate: per-environment means with percentile-bootstrap intervals ($B = 10{,}000$), and the BALROG overall score, the unweighted mean of the six environment means, with its normal-approximation interval. Each row is a different suite with its own metric and denominator, so no cross-lane average is drawn, and none of these values is pooled with an original-contract figure. Source: \longtok{outputs/finish\_\allowbreak{}pending\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-27/<lane>/result.json}.}

The later launch used a lead-issued protocol override rather than
independent approval. That governance limitation matters: the candidate
was both the experiment lead and the author of the override. The public
derivative omits operational authorization records and account limits;
the scientific departures from the native protocols remain explicit in
Table 8.D.

The six figures are on six different benchmarks with six different
metrics, and they are not averaged, pooled with the same lane's
original-contract result, or compared with a base model. Five lanes (E2,
E5, E6, E9, E13) now have a native score over their full replacement
scope. E6's is a lower bound: 108 tasks needing an LLM judge could not
be graded, and the true figure lies between 0.111 and 0.244. E1 has two
separately reported scores, 1/190 and 4/110 (the earlier waves, served
at a different context), that together cover all 300 tasks but are not
combined. E5's pass\^{}1 over the 72 tasks without an infrastructure
error, 8/72 = 0.111, is a sensitivity figure only, and E13's 26.1 per
cent is not compared with the 26-episode small-scale E13 rows of Tables
8.A and 8.B. Per-lane narratives are in Appendix D.

\section{The E4 rerun as a
diagnostic}\label{the-e4-rerun-as-a-diagnostic}

A fresh full BankerToolBench rerun was reported to have run the base
model with a zero-initialised LoRA snapshot through the Modal bridge
\texttt{/v1/responses} path after the lane closed
\texttt{CLOSED\_PARTIAL} on 19 September. The surviving
\longtok{outputs/e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}small\_\allowbreak{}scale\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-26/E4/result.json}
(\longtok{prior\_\allowbreak{}base\_\allowbreak{}run}) repeats 100 trials, one error and a mean
reward of 0.0. The recovered terminal ledger,
\texttt{withheld\ historical\ campaign\ chronology}, includes updates
through 21 September and explicitly says the rerun was NOT launched at
that snapshot. It therefore cannot support the later completed-run
account. The raw job receipt,
\longtok{outputs/e4\_\allowbreak{}banker\_\allowbreak{}toolbench/official\_\allowbreak{}repo\_\allowbreak{}ff6db552/jobs/btb-banking-tasks-tinker-bridge/result.json},
remains unrecovered in this package. The 100-trial result is supported
here by the surviving \longtok{prior\_\allowbreak{}base\_\allowbreak{}run} secondary record; the
earlier detailed claims of 2--14 agent steps, no deliverables and at
least 27 role-token-degenerate trajectories are not freshly verified.
The surviving bridge finding documents a route that passes no stop
sequences and can run past end-of-turn tokens into invented turns. Under
a corrected shim, the same base model scored 0.4087 on task
\texttt{btb-19b3361c} in the six-task small-scale rerun (§8.7). That
contrast demonstrates a plausible failure mechanism, but it compares one
successful corrected task with 100 bridge trials while changing tasks,
serving path and caps. It does not identify how much of the aggregate
zero the bridge caused or exclude model limitations. The 0.0 is retained
as a confounded diagnostic, not a controlled model-capability estimate.
It is not set against the original single-task outcome, and no valid
full-suite BankerToolBench capability estimate exists for this actor
family. The fuller reported audit and evidence limitations are in
Appendix D (Figure~\ref{fig:e4}).


\fig{fig_e4_diagnostic}
{The E4 rerun diagnostic}
{The E4 rerun diagnostic. Across 100 trials agents terminate at a median of roughly six steps without producing deliverables, and more than a quarter end in role-token degeneration, consistent with generation running past the end-of-turn token because the serving bridge passed no stop sequences. The causal share of the zero reward is unidentified: the corrected-path check changes task, path and caps together. Left: per-trajectory step counts (\texttt{final\_metrics.total\_steps}), stacked by whether the final agent message carries at least two concatenated role tokens (29/100; the ledger records ``27+''). Right: where the verifier chain stopped (\texttt{verifier/test-stdout.txt}, \texttt{reward.json}), all bars on one scale. Base-model rerun of 2026-09-21, all 100 trial directories under \longtok{outputs/e4\_\allowbreak{}banker\_\allowbreak{}toolbench/}. These raw trial artefacts are absent from the reviewed checkout; the graphic preserves the historical summary rather than a fresh receipt-level verification.}
{fig:e4}



\section{Externally blocked lanes and terminal
states}\label{externally-blocked-lanes-and-terminal-states}

Seven original benchmark contracts remain outside the completed
evaluation scope: SDAB, BinaryAudit, LifeSciBench, AgentHarm, AppBench,
OpenReward and FrontierMath. Their required benchmark payload or hosted
evaluation scope is not available in this public package. Six are
recorded as externally closed; OpenReward proceeds only through the
separate BALROG replacement scope. These statuses are scope limits and
not graded model failures. Private access correspondence, operational
requests and account state are omitted. The following small-scale reruns
keep original and substitute benchmarks separate (Figure~\ref{fig:taxonomy}).


\fig{fig_terminal_taxonomy}
{Terminal-state taxonomy used to close every lane}
{Terminal-state taxonomy used to close every lane. Each state has a defined meaning and a named reopen condition where one exists. The seven states are the ledger's full vocabulary; 17 lane-states cover 14 lanes because E8, E10 and E14 carry separate original and replacement rows. States classify lanes, not results: an unrecognised state is recorded as blocked rather than absorbed into a percentage, and original-contract and replacement-scope numbers are never pooled. The completion gate's \texttt{PARTIAL\_EXACT}/\texttt{PARTIAL\_RECOVERY} labels are a separate evidence-class vocabulary (Chapter~5). Lane assignments are those of 2026-09-19; the six lanes in the three pending leaves were all run and scored on 2026-09-27 (Chapter~8).}
{fig:taxonomy}



\section{Small-scale reruns of all fourteen
lanes}\label{small-scale-reruns-of-all-fourteen-lanes}

This section adds a separate, smaller set of runs, made on 26 September
2026, to give every one of the fourteen lanes a measured number under
one fixed protocol, including the lanes that never produced a score.
None of these figures is pooled with, averaged against, or substituted
for a result reported earlier in this chapter.

The protocol fixes one actor family, a fixed item-selection seed
(20260926), temperature 0 and a non-thinking chat template, and scores
each lane with its native grader where one could be run. Lanes whose
original benchmark is externally blocked were run on a public
substitute, and each substitute's gap from the original suite is
recorded in its lane file. Items that errored or timed out are counted
as failures in the denominator.

Two arms were run. The first is the base model,
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} with no adapter, sampled on Tinker (Table
8.A). The second is a paired comparison of the trained actor against the
same base model, both served by one vLLM configuration on Modal, on
identical items (Table 8.B). The pairing exists because the adapter's
effect on individual token probabilities was measured to be about the
same size as the numerical difference between the two serving engines on
the same base weights, so a trained-on-vLLM against base-on-Tinker
comparison would confound the adapter with the engine, and none is made.

These are small samples. Most lanes score between five and eighty items,
so a single item moves a lane's rate by several percentage points and
the confidence intervals are wide. In Table 8.B, b and c count items
solved only by the trained actor and only by the base model
respectively, and the CI is a paired bootstrap interval on the
difference. Four lane-specific caveats matter. For E12 the 151 rubric
items come from only six generated applications, so its McNemar p-value
overstates the evidence; the exact sign-flip test over the six per-app
differences gives p = 0.25, and the grader is an LLM judge that is the
base model itself, so the E12 rates are judge-dependent. For E4, only
\texttt{btb-19b3361c} scores above zero in either arm (trained 0.397,
base 0.557; 0.409 in Table 8.A), so no interval is valid. For E13, the
1.875 difference is the mean of per-environment differences computed
from unrounded per-item values (recomputed 1.8752), not 25.510 − 23.630.
For E9, the one competition that earned a medal in the Tinker run
(nomad2018) has a 46.8k-token prompt that exceeds the 32,768-token vLLM
limit and fails identically in both vLLM arms, so the vLLM medal rates
are not comparable with Table 8.A. The paired differences are not
combined across lanes, and none of the seventeen paired tests excludes
zero. At these sizes that is an inconclusive result, not evidence of no
effect: with 5 to 10 items, an exact McNemar test needs at least six
discordant items, all in one direction, to reach p \textless{} 0.05. No
equivalence test was run. Fuller E12 detail and the principal per-lane
caveats from each result file are in Appendix D.

\section{Summary of the campaign at the point of
writing}\label{summary-of-the-campaign-at-the-point-of-writing}

By the 19 September ledger the campaign had established two full-suite
results and one complete replacement scope with a score: E1 at 2/731 =
0.274 per cent on SWE-bench Pro, E11 at 129/312 = 41.35 per cent pass@1
(design-cluster bootstrap 95\% interval {[}34.3, 48.7{]}, n = 156
designs) across its two framings, and E8 at 450/1967 = 0.2288 ({[}0.211,
0.248{]}) on the LAB-Bench public split, alongside a complete
benign-utility evaluation of E10 at 88 of 97 episodes (0.907, {[}0.833,
0.950{]}), a terminal-complete E14 replacement scope at 2,271/4,428 =
51.29 per cent accuracy, and five original-contract partials (E2, E4,
E5, E7, E9), none of which is a suite score. Three of those scores are
dominated by the harness rather than the actor: 593 of E1's 713
submitted patches were corrupt for \texttt{git\ apply}, E11's 150
extraction failures follow from a 1,024-token cap with thinking on, and
E14's capped responses are correct at 34.5\% against 85.9\% uncapped
(§8.2, §8.3). On 27 September six further replacement scopes were
completed under a self-issued override whose deviations are recorded per
lane (Tables 8.C and 8.D). Seven original contracts remain externally
blocked, and no lane now waits on a launch or a cloud quota. The
campaign did not establish a multi-seed estimate, a held-out security
result, a BankerToolBench suite score, a graded result for the 108
WebArena tasks that need an LLM judge, or a baseline comparison for the
campaign lanes themselves; the only trained-versus-base comparison,
Table 8.B, is inconclusive in every lane. The E8 diagnostic, E10 native
receipt, training-identity verification, E14 terminal note and both
pending-state snapshots are recovered in the private package while
remaining absent from the frozen repository. Their receipt contents are
now inspectable, and bounded stored-payload checks corroborate E8/E10
counts and the E14 denominator distinction; training-source, E4
raw-evidence and semantic-reproduction limits remain as in §8.2 and
Appendix D. The available evidence chains, reproduction paths and gaps
are given in Appendix B; the next chapter draws the cross-study
synthesis and conclusions.



\part{Synthesis}


\chapter{Cross-Study Synthesis, Threats to Validity, Conclusions and
Future
Work}\label{cross-study-synthesis-threats-to-validity-conclusions-and-future-work}

\section{Cross-Study Synthesis}\label{cross-study-synthesis}

This report has presented two bodies of work built with different
instruments. The first is the P1--P8 corpus, which probes GRPO-style
post-training across libraries, model scales, group sizes and datasets
and ends in a reporting standard, a machine-readable registry and a
controller audit. The second is the E1--E14 campaign, a governance-bound
evaluation of one Tinker-trained actor against fourteen agentic and
reasoning suites outside its recorded training-suite list; the recovered
identity record does not establish content-level decontamination. They
are the same methodological commitment applied twice --- once to
\emph{what does GRPO do}, once to \emph{what does a GRPO-trained actor
score}. That commitment is attribution: every comparative claim holds
the entire training stack fixed except the one factor under test, and
where that was unachievable the confound is reported alongside the
number rather than absorbed.

The corpus's own synthesis, recorded at the close of Phase 1, is that on
this compute scale the \emph{measurement} layer is useful while most
\emph{algorithmic} levers are noise-limited. The dominant measured
phenomenon is advantage-signal starvation, with the Zero-Variance
Fraction at approximately 0.72--0.77 on the Phase-1 main configuration,
and it is easy-driven: the collapsed groups are mostly prompts the model
has already solved. That rules out the most tempting remedy, because
re-baselining the advantages merely converts the signal into a
difficulty proxy with no learning content, and the remedy that remains
--- a curriculum that removes the wasted rollouts --- returned a null.
The narrow systems observation survived better than any algorithmic one:
per-step telemetry separated the available method traces more cleanly
than reward-only features, though the classifier's integrity
generalisation was never tested.

The Semester 4 internal reports sharpen those threads rather than
reversing them. P2's diagnostics are recomputable from stored raw
tensors rather than read back from logged summaries: three stored GSM8K
group tensors reproduce ZVF at 0.130, 0.190 and 0.155, pooling to
0.1583, and the correlation between ZVF and final held-out outcome, ρ =
0.27, is not significant (95\% CI {[}−0.37, 0.88{]}, n = 23 pooled
cells). ZVF is therefore offered as a diagnostic and not a predictor.
Once mean reward is regressed out, the pooled measured residual is r =
+0.21 (95\% CI {[}−0.31, +0.62{]}, n = 17), so ZVF is not an incremental
predictor either (Chapter 6 §6.3.4). P4's matched comparison of GRPO
against Dr.~GRPO finds the two held-out gains indistinguishable, with
pooled mean completion length drifting from 194.4 to 183.5 tokens under
a 200-token cap that bounds what the comparison can say. P4's companion
error-bar audit grades the single P4-facing headline as DECISIVE: audit
H7, the iter-136 arithmetic late-training-efficiency contrast, paired n
= 5 seeds, \(d_z = +2.68\). Re-examined, it is \emph{suggestive} only
(Chapter 6 §6.5.4). Its ``one-sided parametric p = 0.031'' is the floor
of an exact one-sided Wilcoxon test at n = 5. The two-sided exact
sign-flip p is 0.0625, even though all five pairs point the same way.
The direction was one of eight hypotheses in its family, and Holm
correction across those eight raises the family's p to 0.25. The audit's
other three DECISIVE grades do not survive either. Two (H1, H2) rest on
an interval that excludes its own point estimate and on four
reconstructed budgets (§6.4.2; Table E.1), and one (H6) is an AUROC
computed on a synthetic and imputed panel (§6.3.4). Of the seven audited
headlines, then, none is decisive, one is suggestive and the rest are
null or hypothesis-generating. What survives is a consistent-direction
late-training efficiency contrast on a training metric, not a capability
claim.

P3 and P7 both treat group size as a lever and are best read as a pair,
because they disagree in emphasis. P3 finds that under saturation the
retention ratio between G = 2 and G = 16 is approximately 1.00. That
ratio is of last-10 training reward, and a seed-level TOST (n = 3,
margin ±0.02) does not establish G = 16 as equivalent to G = 2 (p =
0.056; Chapter 6 §6.4.1). P3 also finds that the effective
signal-to-noise ratio reaches only about 52\% of the √G ideal, and that
a 505-task conditional utility audit selects G = 4 over G = 5 by 0.00177
with a 95\% bootstrap interval of {[}0.00082, 0.00270{]} --- positive,
small, and explicitly cohort- and objective-conditional. P7 models
usable signal per prompt as \(S = p(1-p)(1-h_G(p))\) with
\(h_G(p) = p^G + (1-p)^G\), derives three falsifiable consequences, and
checks them: a 368-run Weights \& Biases audit across six model labels
(Appendix A, row P7-C1) reproduces the predicted U-shape in accuracy,
with ZVF 0.95--0.97 at the low-reward extreme and 0.87--0.96 at the
high-reward extreme and 0.25--0.29 at mid-range rewards of 0.35--0.50; a
model × G grid confirms the monotone effect at interior accuracy,
Qwen3-32B moving 0.33 → 0.18 → 0.08 across G = 4/8/16; and a toy-scale
open-backward-pass experiment finds corr(‖∇‖, p(1−p)) = +0.71 on a 0.5B
model, which the P7 report labels directional only. Where P3's sweep
could not find an optimum, P7 supplies a mechanism under which a shallow
one should exist. That is a reconciliation, not a resolution: the
mechanism is checked in telemetry, and its causal link to the gradient
remains toy-scale.

The same report is clear about the control it does not have. A
micro-jitter test collapses the exact-tie fraction from 0.158 to 0.000
while barely changing PCD, but the declared variance-threshold ZVF
remains 0.158 (§6.3.1). It therefore does not establish superiority of
PCD over tolerance-based ZVF. In a four-arm open-trainer audit, adaptive
group size matches the best fixed-recipe held-out gain, +0.575, at 186
rollouts, while DAPO-style dynamic sampling drives ZVF to 0.00 at +45\%
rollout cost --- an accuracy-versus-compute trade, not a controller win.
In the larger 2,560-observation controller trace, 1,723 of 1,867
escalation events, 92.3\%, occur on all-correct groups and only 144 on
all-wrong, so a symmetric ZVF trigger spends most of its budget on
observed all-correct groups. Eight successes do not prove latent p = 1,
and the plug-in simulation's zero rescue is imposed by its estimator.
Deleting those fires remains counterfactual because performance
preservation was not measured. Adaptive group size is therefore not
promoted to a recommended policy.

A separate frozen-checkpoint diagnostic now observes fresh responses
directly (§7.4; Appendix B §B.12): 33/461 initially homogeneous groups
became reward-mixed, 7.16\% {[}5.14\%, 9.88\%{]}. Twenty first changes
are strict-parser format failures with the same visible gold number;
only 13/461 (2.82\%) are assistant-audited numeric contrasts, comprising
two recoveries and eleven new failures. The format effect belongs to
this diagnostic contract, not the repository's more permissive
historical evaluator. The numeric witnesses reject certainty from eight
equal observations on this new checkpoint/task/decoder, but supply no
historical-checkpoint reproduction, training or controller benefit,
group-size optimum, or held-out capability estimate from the GSM8K train
split.

P5 and P6 supply the corpus's methodological claim, and they contain its
sharpest internal tension. P5's headline exhibits are a nominally
matched-configuration backend audit whose final training rewards span
5.0\% to 85.6\% --- a 17× same-label swing the P5 report declines to
assign to the backend, because the managed stack also silently pinned a
different base checkpoint (Qwen3-8B-Base on Tinker against the
instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B on TRL), and that undisclosed bundling is
itself part of the exhibit --- and a pair of runs carrying the same
``DAPO'' label in which an open trainer with true dynamic sampling
produced mean ZVF 0.00 while a closed stack running an asymmetric-clip
surrogate produced mean ZVF 0.58. Alongside them sits a third exhibit: a
12-cell head-to-head of four labelled algorithms on one fixed stack,
landing within a 0.034 band with mean last-10 reward 0.710--0.744, a
spread the P5 report calls comparable to seed-level variation. P5's
framing is that a label-only comparison can hide stack-level variation,
and that the converse also holds: when the stack is genuinely fixed, the
labels stop separating. P6 reads the same exhibit in the reverse
direction, using it to motivate a schema while explicitly declining to
re-claim it as novel empirical science; on the twenty seed entries the
MIN-REPORT auditor badge spans 43--96, and \emph{stackdiff} flags the
open-versus-closed ``DAPO'' pair as an R5 label-flip risk from manifests
alone.

Two gaps should be named. The first is that P7's controller audit and
P5's stack-attribution programme have not been joined: the controller is
evaluated on open trainers where the update rule is auditable, while the
standard is motivated by a closed-stack divergence that cannot be
audited. The second is P8, the credit-card fraud side-probe, absorbed
here as a measurement-discipline case study and nothing more. Its two
AUCs are not a like-for-like ranking: XGBoost's 0.7955 and the
Qwen3.5-4B SFT arm's 0.48268 on a positive-enriched 500-row held-out
evaluation sit on different held-out splits by construction.

The E1--E14 campaign is a different instrument and should be read as
one. Its figures have unequal source accessibility. The six original
files covering five missing basenames listed in Appendix B §B.11 are now
preserved in a withheld source archive, while remaining absent from the
frozen checkout. Supplementary read-only payload checks corroborate
stored E8/E10 counts and E14 disposition/denominator reporting; they do
not rerun a model or scorer, independently judge correctness, establish
content decontamination or recover the missing training logs. A single
Tinker-trained actor --- base \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at commit
\texttt{995ad96e…}, with adapter \texttt{campaign-adapter} at commit
\texttt{withheld-adapter-revision…}, in bfloat16 --- was evaluated
against fourteen suites outside its recorded training-suite list, under
per-lane native evaluation contracts. The recovered AgentDojo receipt
binds version v1.2.2 but no upstream commit; the broader revision claims
retain their source-specific limits (§8.2). Three scopes closed strictly
complete: two replacement scopes, LAB-Bench public at 1,967/1,967 across
eight categories and AgentDojo benign utility with 97/97 episodes
completed and utility 88/97 = 0.907 (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.833, 0.950{]}),
and one original-contract suite, VerilogEval, at 312/312 with 129/312 =
41.35\% pass@1 canonical (design-cluster bootstrap 95\% CI {[}34.3\%,
48.7\%{]}, resampling 156 paired designs (§8.2)). Omni-MATH closed
terminal-complete under its native protocol, with all 4,428 dispositions
recorded, 4,426 accepted, and an accuracy of 2,271/4,428 = 51.29\% with
the two unjudged rows counted as failures (the stored native-score
fields give 51.31\% over the 4,426 judged rows). The recovered terminal
note incorrectly associates that decimal with denominator 4,428; the
thesis retains the corrected arithmetic and preserves the source
unchanged (Table E.1; sources: recovered E14 terminal note and
\texttt{stored-payload\ source\ audit}, Appendix B §B.11). Two suites
carry full-coverage scores under their original contracts: SWE-bench Pro
at 2/731 = 0.274\% pass@1 (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.08\%, 0.99\%{]}) and
VerilogEval, the same E11 result counted once. Both, and Omni-MATH, are
dominated by the harness configuration: 593 of 713 SWE-bench Pro patches
were corrupt for \texttt{git\ apply}, and the VerilogEval and Omni-MATH
scores are bound by their generation budgets (§8.2, §8.3). Six further
replacement scopes were run in full on 27 September 2026 (Table 8.C):
CORE-Bench hard at 27/45 = 0.600, Tau3 at 8/97 = 0.082, WebArena at
90/812 = 0.111 (a lower bound, with 108 judge-dependent tasks ungraded),
ML-Dev-Bench at 10/34 = 0.294, BALROG at 26.1\% progression over 255
episodes, and SWE-bench Multilingual at 1/190 on the tasks not attempted
before, kept separate from the earlier 4/110. Lane scope and status are
tabulated once, in Table C.3. The rest are partial or externally
blocked, each with a terminal state and a named gate.

What the campaign establishes is descriptive, and its scope matters. For
\emph{this} actor, at \emph{this} revision, under \emph{each suite's
own} evaluator and denominator, these are the scores. It is not a
post-training delta: no per-suite base-model comparator appears in the
campaign ledger at the campaign's own scopes, so no campaign number,
high or low, is evidence about what the GRPO step contributed. The one
paired comparison of trained actor and base model is the 26 September
small-scale rerun (Table 8.B): 14 lanes, 5 to 151 identical items each,
one serving engine. It finds no significant difference in any lane,
which at those sizes is inconclusive rather than evidence of no effect.
The surviving E4 \longtok{prior\_\allowbreak{}base\_\allowbreak{}run} secondary record reports a
BankerToolBench base-model rerun with mean reward 0.0 across 100 trials.
The recovered finish ledger explicitly says the rerun was not yet
launched at its 21 September snapshot, so it is not evidence for the
later completion. The bridge finding documents omitted stop sequences
and reports invented turns; the detailed historical claims of
termination after 2--14 steps without \texttt{deliverables/} output
remain unverified against the still-unrecovered raw job receipt and
trajectories. One corrected-path task achieved positive reward,
supporting a concrete harness defect, but task, serving path and caps
changed together; the fraction of the 100-trial zero attributable to
that defect is unidentified. The result describes that
model-plus-harness configuration (Section 8.5; sources:
\longtok{outputs/e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}small\_\allowbreak{}scale\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-26/E4/result.json},
\longtok{prior\_\allowbreak{}base\_\allowbreak{}run};
\longtok{outputs/e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}small\_\allowbreak{}scale\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-26/E4/BRIDGE\_\allowbreak{}STOP\_\allowbreak{}TOKEN\_\allowbreak{}FINDING.md}).

What governance bought is visible in the ledger itself: eleven of eleven
named deterministic checks passing on 2026-09-19 (scope in Appendix B),
and the recovered finish ledger preserving a historical snapshot
recording E7 as ``1/28 errored'' visibly beside the superseding table's
``1/46 attempted'', without inventing a reconciliation.

\section{Rigour and Threats to
Validity}\label{rigour-and-threats-to-validity}

\textbf{What rigour was exercised.} Diagnostics are recomputable from
stored raw tensors, and headline structural claims were re-verified by
eleven named deterministic code checks on 2026-09-19, all passing,
covering exact divisions, receipt presence, sealed-artifact hash
equality and lost-source absence probes. Held-out \emph{n} is reported
alongside every gain, and failed runs are counted rather than hidden:
failed attempts remain part of the stated limitations.

Table 9.1 lists the threats discussed below, grouped as internal,
construct and external, with the mitigation applied to each. Most
mitigations narrow what is claimed rather than repair the evidence.

\textbf{Threats to validity and the mitigation applied to each.}

\textbf{Statistical power: single-seed Tinker experiments.} Most Tinker
configurations ran once, because cost precluded repetition, and those
single-seed runs carry no variance estimates or significance tests. The
exceptions are stated where they occur. They are the three-seed-per-arm
managed head-to-head (Chapter 7 §7.1), the five-seed Qwen3-8B held-out
control (p = 0.26, Chapter 6 §6.5.4) and the Qwen3-8B scale extension.
Henderson et al.~(2018) recommend at least ten seeds, and no result
family reported here reaches that bar. The seed counts are: one seed for
most Tinker runs and all P1 anchors; three for the P3 sweep, the P4
GSM8K-CoT and scale-extension arms and the managed head-to-head; five
for the same-stack PPO/GRPO contrast, the P4 arithmetic arms and the
Qwen3-8B pre/post control; and eight for the variant audit (P11). The
limitations document says the ten-seed bar was met for the TRL baseline
and the GSM8K evaluation and then that the evaluation was cut to five
seeds; the run records in Appendix A show five seeds for both the TRL
Modal campaign (Table A.3) and the GSM8K pre/post evaluation. The 30B
MoE scaling analysis uses three seeds; the bootstrap confidence
intervals assume independent observations, so any positive correlation
from shared initialisation or data ordering would make the reported
intervals too narrow, and the true intervals wider; and secondary
analyses carry uncorrected p-values. Quantitative conclusions therefore
come from the open-source side, where every hyperparameter is auditable,
while Tinker figures are descriptive.

\textbf{Horizons of 30--50 steps.} The Tinker runs are early-training
snapshots; long-horizon effects such as reward hacking, catastrophic
forgetting and late-stage policy collapse are unlikely to manifest, and
the source conclusion is direct: ``Asymptotic claims are unsupported''.
This bounds results rather than vitiating them: P1's failure to recover
a scaling law across roughly 2.4 orders of magnitude is a bounded
negative, and P4's drift from 194.4 to 183.5 mean completion tokens is
bounded by the 200-token cap.

\textbf{Closed Tinker internals.} The Tinker SDK exposes a custom loss
path, a limited optimiser surface and standard LoRA hyperparameters, but
not the exact server-side GRPO loss formulation, the reward
normalisation or baseline-subtraction scheme, minibatch or
gradient-accumulation strategy, hardware configuration, or system-level
telemetry. This applies unevenly: the inspected custom-loss Tinker
reference loops define their loss in client code (REINFORCE with a
group-mean baseline, Table 4.2), while the actor's missing original
execution source limits verification of its historical loss path
(Appendix D), whereas the runners that use Tinker's built-in
\texttt{importance\_sampling} loss leave its server-side details
unrecorded. The consequence is best quoted: Tinker results measure ``the
\textbf{platform's} implementation of GRPO, not an abstract algorithmic
specification''. The illustrative case in the limitations document
compares a pooled last-10 training reward of 99.9\% for Tinker GRPO with
73.4\% for TRL GRPO on GSM8K. Those figures are training rewards, not
held-out accuracy, and they pool Tinker runs on 4B--235B models against
TRL on Qwen2.5-0.5B, so model size and implementation are confounded;
the source itself attributes part of the gap to the model difference.
The only same-model pair, Qwen3-8B, used the Base checkpoint on Tinker
and the Instruct checkpoint on TRL (Table A.2), so it does not remove
the confound either. The threat propagates into the campaign, whose
actor was trained on Tinker. The SDK is also a moving target: Phase 1
pinned \texttt{tinker==0.16.1}, while the September 2026 bridge required
\texttt{tinker==0.30.0} after the older SDK produced a
no-longer-supported error.

\textbf{Per-suite native denominators are not comparable.} Every E1--E14
result is expressed on its own suite's denominator, and those
denominators differ in size, in what they count and in what they
exclude. SWE-bench Pro is 2/731, whose denominator retains 14 generation
failures and 4 artifact losses alongside 713 native evaluations, 593 of
which ran on the unpatched base because the submitted patch was corrupt
for \texttt{git\ apply} (§8.3); APEX-Agents is 7/480; E9 MLE-bench is
40/75 competitions natively graded, stated as 53.33\% coverage with a
null suite score, because coverage and score are different quantities;
E1's replacement SWE-bench Multilingual is 1/190 on one contract and
4/110 on another, never summed to a 300-task figure; E6 WebArena is
90/812 with 108 tasks ungraded, so its denominator counts items that
were never graded; E13 BALROG is a mean of six per-environment means
rather than a rate over episodes; and E14's Omni-MATH records 4,428
dispositions of which 4,426 were accepted, with the headline diagnostic
computed over all 4,428 (the stored native score's 51.31\% uses the
4,426 judged rows; the recovered terminal note's contrary denominator
label is corrected in Table E.1). A 41.35\% on one denominator and a
0.274\% on another do not license a comparison, an average or a ranking,
and none is offered; the rule holds within lanes too, and the ledger
states it flatly: ``N/A means unverified; no pooled results''.

\textbf{No cross-suite aggregate.} No mean, median or composite index
over suites appears anywhere in this report or in the campaign materials
it draws on: an aggregate over native denominators would be a number
with no referent, mixing suites that measure different capabilities at
different difficulties under different scoring rules. The cost is that
the campaign has no single headline; the three strictly complete scopes,
the terminal-complete Omni-MATH scope, the six replacement scopes
completed on 27 September and the two original-contract scores are the
most the record supports.

\textbf{The E10 benign-utility scope says nothing about adversarial
robustness.} The E10 lane's completed evidence is a benign-utility
evaluation of 97/97 completed tasks with utility 88/97 = 0.907 under
AgentDojo's benign scope; its original contract, private AgentHarm
tasks, is externally blocked and carries a closure record with a named
reopen condition. The completeness result therefore carries no
adversarial-robustness measurement, and this report makes no such claim
for the trained actor. The lane has a dual-use dimension, though the
limitations analysis finds the marginal uplift of the released artefacts
small, because the base model's capability is overwhelmingly
pre-existing: the pre-RL Qwen3-8B-Instruct checkpoint already scores
82.0\% on held-out GSM8K, and full GRPO adds only +1.3 points to 83.3\%,
not significant at p = 0.26. The 82.0\% is the same Instruct checkpoint
before RL, not a separate base model.

\textbf{The retained E11 result came through the original sampler.} The
129/312 full-run receipt attributes its samples to the managed Tinker
sampler rather than a locally served copy. Private execution and
repository access identities are omitted, so this public package cannot
replay the original route. A different serving stack would be a protocol
change with unmeasured same-weights, different-sampler divergence. The
separate four-task step-40 pilot has different source and
decontamination limitations and is not substituted for the full run.

\textbf{Known evidence loss in the finish-era recovery source.} The
record is explicit: the historical source-loss incident recorded in
\texttt{withheld\ historical\ source-loss\ chronology} removed the
execution sources of the finish-era recovery and build code --- the E1
wave-10 controller, the E9 restored dependencies and the E6 validator
--- while sealed requests, validation receipts and test logs survive
with matching hashes, and the replacement lanes' native runners survive
under \texttt{zvf-program/flagship/}. The lost-source policy was applied
rather than waived: a faithful re-implementation was written into a
tracked path under \texttt{zvf-program/e1\_wave10/}, where it verifies
10/10 targeted regressions offline (\longtok{test\_\allowbreak{}process\_\allowbreak{}group.py}
4/4, \longtok{test\_\allowbreak{}cleanup\_\allowbreak{}regressions.py} 6/6). Two consequences
follow: a sealed request without its execution source is not a rerun, so
the E9 MLDevBench runtime recipe was never reconstructed (the 27
September run built a fresh environment on Google Cloud instead); and
the policy response --- execution sources henceforth live under tracked
paths --- cannot restore what was already lost. A related item: the Jev
(TypeSafe) judgment harness lost reliability from roughly 13:10Z on
2026-09-19, both blocker-classification batteries returning degenerate
distributions and a controlled yes-probe falling from about 1.0 to 0.3;
the receipts are retained as incident evidence and the batteries must be
re-run once the health check passes, so the ledger's
first-uncleared-blocker column stands on document evidence rather than
judgment output.

\textbf{Scope and confound inventory.} Several Phase-1 threats remain
live. Many early runs logged train-set rewards only, so time-series
comparisons spanning old and new runs must account for the
instrumentation change. Initial phases used LoRA at ranks 8--64
exclusively; historical baseline comparisons use default hyperparameters
from the Tinker cookbook and the TRL documentation; and no full
learning-rate × rank × batch × group-size grid was swept. Tinker, Modal,
Colab and the PES A100 node use different accelerators, memory
hierarchies and training stacks, so observed differences in reward
dynamics are not purely algorithmic, and the work was not
pre-registered. The model-scale range is 0.6B--1T, with the anchors
above 235B single-seed and descriptive (Chapter 6 §6.1); the limitations
record's ``reliable'' range of 0.6B--235B is the multi-run part of that
roster, so dense-70B and trillion-parameter MoE extrapolations are
unreliable; benchmark coverage is narrow and biased toward verifiable
rewards such as exact match, unit tests and schema validity, with no
human preference data, so findings should not be extended to
reward-model-based RLHF; and the work was carried out at one institution
in Bengaluru, so benchmark choices and evaluation design reflect that
context.

The resource accounting is itself a threat. The report estimates roughly
60 A100 GPU-hours of in-kind PES University compute and projects
environmental use of about 776 kWh and about 296 kg CO₂-eq; the dominant
uncertainty is the Tinker GPU-hour count, inferred because the platform
exposes no hardware telemetry, giving a pessimistic Tinker contribution
of about 540 kg against an optimistic one of about 130 kg. The
mitigation offered is reproducibility by artefact.

\section{Conclusions}\label{conclusions}

This report set out to build a multi-framework training harness for
GRPO-style post-training of large language models and to study that
post-training under a single methodological rule: hold the stack fixed,
change one factor, report what happens. The harness dispatches one
configuration to several back-ends, but only Tinker and TRL produced
completed runs, on different base checkpoints, so it is not a completed
multi-framework benchmark. The four research questions of §1.4 have the
following answers.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{RQ1 (ZVF).} Yes, it is recomputable: the three stored Qwen3-8B
  tensors reproduce 0.130, 0.190 and 0.155 exactly. Its information
  about outcome is limited: its reported association with catastrophic
  collapse is stronger than with final held-out accuracy, and the latter
  is not distinguishable from zero (§6.3; Table 1.1). Its magnitude is
  regime-dependent, from 0.16 to 0.84 across the measured runs.
\item
  \textbf{RQ2 (algorithm labels on a fixed stack).} Unanswered at this
  budget. PPO versus GRPO, GRPO versus Dr.~GRPO and G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\}
  all sit at evaluation accuracy 0.98--0.99 on 0.5B two-digit
  arithmetic, and nulls at ceiling are not evidence of equivalence: they
  cannot separate no effect from no headroom. The unsaturated
  GRPO/Dr.~GRPO comparisons (Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT; the eight-seed
  variant audit of §1.2) are inconclusive and establish no equivalence
  either (§6.2.3, §6.4, §6.5; Table 1.1). The same-stack rerun on the
  unsaturated Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT configuration answers part of it:
  GRPO at G = 8 exceeds a minimal learned-critic PPO by +0.050
  {[}+0.015, +0.085{]} (five paired seeds, paired-t p = 0.016; exact
  sign-flip p = 0.0625), a baseline-specific, suggestive gap against an
  untuned critic whose gain is not detectable at this budget, and G = 8
  minus G = 2 is −0.005 {[}−0.038, +0.028{]}, neither different nor
  equivalent at a ±0.02 margin (§6.5.5).
\item
  \textbf{RQ3 (manifest-level flip risk).} Yes, for the one pair tested:
  \texttt{stackdiff} flags the open-versus-closed ``DAPO'' pair as R5
  from manifests alone (§7.2). Whether it flags risk that matters on
  outcome was not tested beyond that pair.
\item
  \textbf{RQ4 (governance-first evaluation).} It yields receipt-bound
  per-suite scores and an explicit terminal state for every lane (Table
  C.3). It does not yield a post-training delta: the actor had 40
  training steps, and the paired Table 8.B comparison is inconclusive in
  every lane.
\end{itemize}

On the analytical side the contribution is a measurement layer and a set
of bounded negatives. The Zero-Variance Fraction is a reproducible
diagnostic of advantage-signal starvation, recomputable from stored
tensors, regime-dependent in magnitude (0.72--0.77 on the Phase-1 main
configuration, 0.16 to 0.84 across the measured runs; Table 1.1), with
an association with final held-out outcome that is not distinguishable
from zero (ρ = 0.27, 95\% CI {[}−0.37, 0.88{]}, n = 23). It is
descriptive, not predictive. Its theory has been made falsifiable, and
two of its three consequences reproduced: the U-shape in accuracy across
reward levels and the monotone group-size effect; the gradient link
remains toy-scale and directional. Group size therefore has a candidate
mechanism, but the measured three-seed sweep found its arms
statistically indistinguishable and establishes no optimum (Chapter 6
§6.4). Length bias is not established: the roster-level flag that marks
four of eleven GRPO runs --- peak before 65\% of training, terminal
reward below 90\% of peak --- has post hoc thresholds, is not specific
to length, and fires on most runs in the wider trace pool, so it is not
evidence of length bias; in the controlled comparison the same rule
fires on both GRPO and Dr.~GRPO (five of six GSM8K-CoT runs) while no
run shows length growing, completion length drifts shorter, and the two
are indistinguishable on held-out gain (Chapter 6 §6.5). No claim is
made that Dr.~GRPO is uniquely free of the pattern. The levers we could
test --- a naive curriculum, a matched-token-budget intervention, an
integer group-size sweep --- did not produce reliable held-out gains.
The earlier same-stack de-confounds (PPO versus GRPO, GRPO versus
Dr.~GRPO, and group size) were all measured on a saturated 0.5B
arithmetic task with evaluation accuracy near 0.99, so their nulls say
that no difference was detectable there, not that the methods are
equivalent; the 2 October unsaturated Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT rerun now
supplies PPO/GRPO and G = 2 versus G = 8 contrasts (§6.5.5). Its +0.050
GRPO G8-minus-PPO estimate is suggestive and baseline-specific (paired-t
p = 0.016; exact sign-flip p = 0.0625), while the group-size contrast
remains inconclusive. PPO was minimal and untuned, prompt exposure
differed, and the 200-token cap remained potentially binding. The
strongest original contribution remains P5, the reporting standard,
whose status the record states precisely: a targeted search of the GRPO,
model-reporting and provenance literature through July 2026 did not
identify an equivalent GRPO-specific machine-readable run datasheet with
rollout provenance, and this is ``an evidence-bounded novelty claim, not
proof of non-existence''. The earlier second bet --- predictive layer
freezing from step-1 behaviour --- is weakened rather than withdrawn: it
held at 1.5B and collapsed to chance at 3B across four seeds, at a mean
of 0.083 ± 0.048.

The E1--E14 campaign is an evaluation-governance contribution, not
evidence about GRPO: the actor received 40 steps of critic-free
policy-gradient training (§8.1.1), so its scores describe a checkpoint
rather than a training method. It delivered what a governance-first
campaign can deliver: complete, receipt-bound results on three suites
(two replacement scopes and one original contract), with Omni-MATH
terminal-complete; full-coverage original-contract scores on two (E1 and
E11); and an explicit, auditable terminal state for every remaining
lane, with a named gate and a named reopen condition instead of an
indefinite pending. Its discipline is negative, which is why it is
credible: original-contract and replacement-scope numbers are never
pooled, no cross-suite aggregate is computed, and no claim of
improvement over the base model is made for this campaign (the paired
trained-versus-base comparison, Table 8.B, is inconclusive in every
lane). The controlled Chapter 6 gains are separate, stack-specific
findings and are not inferred from these campaign scores. The strongest
complete positive result, 129/312 = 41.35\% pass@1 on VerilogEval at
full 312/312 coverage, is an end-to-end harness score (150 of its 183
failures are extraction failures, driven by a 1,024-token cap with
thinking on; on a 50-item subset the same adapter scores 33/50 with
thinking off at 4,096 tokens against 23/50 in this run) and is reported
as a measurement of one actor under one generation budget on one suite,
with operational source bindings retained outside this public
derivative.

The report's overall conclusion is about method rather than algorithms.
On this compute scale, and with these instruments, the measurements are
informative while the algorithmic levers are noise-limited; the stack is
a material part of the result, so a label without its stack is not a
reproducible experimental specification; and the most durable artefacts
produced here are the ones that make other people's results checkable
--- the ZVF/PCD diagnostic layer, the eight-item minimum reportable
stack (seven manifest fields and one evaluation item), the registry and
its CLI, and a campaign ledger with named receipts and gates, with
recovered primary receipts preserved separately from the frozen checkout
and remaining raw-evidence and provenance limits stated explicitly
(Appendix B §B.11).

\subsection{C1 adds numerical witnesses and exposes reference
limits}\label{c1-adds-numerical-witnesses-and-exposes-reference-limits}

The C1 extension makes the evidence boundary more precise. Its frozen
score is 11/64 on a deterministic first-64 subset, with a question-level
Wilson 95\% working-model interval of 9.88-28.21\%. Assistant-assisted
reconciliation corroborates three numerical recovery witnesses on that
unchanged denominator. Three further events retain convention-dependent
interpretations, four are materially ambiguous and one matches an
incorrect checkpoint reference after eight correct initial answers. The
frozen score is preserved, not retrospectively repaired. Selection on
parser zeros can enrich the reference faults and representation
mismatches that prevent a direct mathematical-recovery interpretation
(Appendix G, sources S1-S5).

These three witnesses show that eight observed wrong numerical answers
do not force future numerical answers to remain wrong for this frozen
checkpoint, task and decoder. They do not validate the reasoning in a
successful response: Santana's earliest successful number was reached by
an incorrect argument. They do not measure a gradient, training update,
held-out capability or controller benefit. Reference auditing and
unit-aware evaluation therefore belong in the measurement design before
a future intervention is interpreted. The full 64-case audit retains
thirteen category disagreements and provides no whole-benchmark
error-prevalence estimate. Historical V2 remains separate and unpooled.

The separately frozen parser-v3 candidate passes only its narrow
four-change extraction check in 2,048 prospectively selected future
outputs. Preservation of 2,025 old-valid objects and agreement on four
new scalar extractions do not establish task correctness or broad parser
precision. The candidate remains undeployed; the C1 endpoint stays on
parser v2 (Appendix G, sources S8-S9).

\section{Future Work}\label{future-work}

The work leaves three kinds of open question: evaluation lanes whose
measurement is incomplete, analytical claims that the present evidence
could not settle, and methodological tools that have been built but not
yet tested at scale.

\textbf{Closing the remaining evaluation gaps.} The six replacement
lanes that were launch-pending or quota-blocked on 21 September were run
to completion on 27 September 2026 with the reported lane outcomes
(Chapter 8 §8.4). Two measurement gaps remain in them. The 108 WebArena
tasks that need an LLM judge are ungraded because the judge was
unavailable, and the native judge model has since been retired, so a
regrade would record a judge substitution. SWE-bench Multilingual has
been attempted on all 300 tasks, but under two serving contracts, so a
single 300-task figure would need the first 110 tasks rerun under the 27
September contract (32,768-token context and the re-implemented source
collector). Six original-contract scopes are recorded
\texttt{CLOSED\_EXTERNAL} with written closure records: E3 (SDAB private
bundle), E7 (BinaryAudit private payload), E8-original (LifeSciBench
package), E10-original (AgentHarm private tasks), E12 (AppBench
deployment) and E14-original (FrontierMath hosted evaluation). A seventh
original contract, E13's OpenReward held-out games, is also blocked on
external access but has no closure record, because the lane continues
through its BALROG replacement scope (Table C.3). A new evaluation would
require the unavailable benchmark scope. A full BankerToolBench score
for E4 would be a new-runtime experiment; the recovered pending-state
ledger recorded a grader-only lower bound of approximately an
undisclosed operational estimate before launch. The later 100-trial
zero-mean rerun is reported separately by
\longtok{outputs/e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}small\_\allowbreak{}scale\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-26/E4/result.json}
(\longtok{prior\_\allowbreak{}base\_\allowbreak{}run}) and remains a confounded diagnostic with its
raw job receipt unrecovered, not a suite capability estimate or a result
substantiated by that pending-state ledger (§8.5).

\textbf{Turning the campaign into a post-training measurement.} The
campaign reports scores for one actor, not the contribution of the
training step. The paired same-engine comparison of §8.7 (Table 8.B) is
the first base-model arm; scaling it from 5--151 items per lane to the
full native scopes, with more than one training seed and a longer
training run than the actor's 40 steps, would turn each lane from a
descriptive score into a post-training delta. The original execution
route is not reproducible from this public package. A future controlled
rerun on an explicitly documented serving stack would measure the
currently unmeasured sampler divergence.

\textbf{Settling the analytical questions.} Five Phase-1 directions
remain open, each answering a threat identified above:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  adequately powered evaluation, moving from the Phase-1 probes'
  held-out \emph{n} of 8--20 (the multi-seed controlled comparisons
  already use 200--500 items, Chapter 1 §1.5) to the full GSM8K test
  split or a large stratified subset at five or more seeds;
\item
  a token-budget-optimal curriculum with staleness bounds, tested
  multi-seed;
\item
  testing whether the mid-to-late band in the per-layer gradient profile
  supports a FLOP-saving freeze schedule, even though step-1
  predictability did not survive scaling;
\item
  hardening the Phase-1 method-trace classifier on grouped run-level and
  temporal splits rather than row-wise cross-validation;
\item
  consolidating the diagnostic findings and the reporting standard for
  publication.
\end{itemize}

Three more follow from the Chapter 6 results. The first is a
length-confounded regime, with the completion cap lifted and longer
horizons, in which a Dr.~GRPO length advantage would be expected to
appear if it exists. The second is the head-to-head protocol between ZVF
and the pairwise-contrast density, to test whether PCD meets its target
association. The third extends the unsaturated same-stack rerun of
§6.5.5: a PPO arm with a warmed-up, tuned critic and generalised
advantage estimation, so that the GRPO--PPO gap can be separated from
critic quality, and enough seeds or evaluation items to resolve the G =
2 versus G = 8 contrast at a ±0.02 margin.

\textbf{Testing the methodological tools.} Three extensions come from
the Semester-4 work:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a pre-registered controller bakeoff against static G = 16 and naive
  boundary heuristics, the test P7 declines to claim in advance of;
\item
  a cross-framework divergence study that runs an identical task,
  reward, decoding and seed configuration across TRL, veRL, OpenRLHF and
  Tinker, turning P5's attribution argument from an audit of existing
  runs into a designed experiment;
\item
  dogfooding the registry schema on the campaign's own runs, so that
  every future benchmark result ships with a verifiable provenance
  record.
\end{itemize}

Two commitments carry forward, because the threats above are structural
rather than incidental. The first is the never-pool rule:
original-contract and replacement-scope numbers stay separate, and
per-suite native denominators are never averaged. The second is that
failures are reported with their mechanism, as the E11 sampler path, the
E4 zero, the lost execution sources and the seed-count discrepancy are
here.

\chapter{Historical Run Registry and Coverage
Reconciliation}\label{historical-run-registry-and-coverage-reconciliation}

This appendix records the historical run inventories used by the report
and the later reconciliation of those inventories. It is not an
exhaustive current account history. Provider logging identities,
independent scientific executions, checkpoint revisions, and
claim-to-run links are different units and must not be added as though
they were interchangeable. Run identifier, library and platform, base
model, algorithm, group size, dataset, seed, steps, and terminal status
are retained where a primary source records them; unknown fields remain
unknown. A claim without supporting primary evidence must be qualified
or withdrawn rather than inferred from an inventory count.

Historical inventories differ in scope and counting units.
Provider-ledger entries, opening-study records, claim links and
independent scientific trials are not interchangeable. This public
derivative omits all account-wide totals, project breakdowns and exact
provider identities. Only scientific study configurations, denominators,
outcomes and limitations are retained; it makes no complete-coverage
claim.

infrastructure: a common harness, a fixed evaluation configuration, and
integrations for several independent RL post-training libraries. These
runs are the shared baseline measurements, and they are the ones for
which the registry is \emph{most} complete, because each was produced
under a declared configuration.

mean of the final ten reported training rewards. Source:

The two \texttt{dryrun} rows are a documented threat to validity, not a
result: the generator falls back to a deterministic seeded trace from
\texttt{verl.VERLTrainer} / \texttt{OpenRLHFTrainer} when the framework
is absent from the sandbox, and the JSON carries the note ``Seeded
deterministic fallback \ldots{} (verl not installed in sandbox)''. Any
comparison reading them as measurements of verl or OpenRLHF is invalid.
The Tinker row also initialises from the \emph{base} checkpoint while
the open-framework rows use the instruction-tuned one, so even the two
real rows are not a clean framework contrast.

rows: model \texttt{Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B}, dataset arithmetic/math, 125
training steps, NVIDIA L4, one run per seed. Seeds are 42, 123, 456,
789, 1024. Group size is not recorded in the artefact and is therefore
shown as ``---''.

\section{Semester-4 P1--P8 study runs}\label{semester-4-p1p8-study-runs}

The P1--P8 series is the individual Semester-4 contribution, and its
runs are indexed by \emph{claim} rather than by project. The audit
ledger at \longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/claim\_\allowbreak{}to\_\allowbreak{}run/}
maps each published claim to its supporting run identifiers, provider
references retained in the non-public source records, whether the model
named in the paper matches the model actually trained, the seed and step
counts, the held-out metric, and an evidence tier. The retained claim
map has eighteen scientific claim rows. Private link and
provider-inventory counts are omitted; the table retains the scientific
evidence tiers and study outcomes.

\textbf{P1--P8 claim-to-run ledger.} Tier vocabulary: A = ≥5 seeds and
≥100 steps; B = 3--4 seeds and 50--99 steps; C = completed/descriptive;
R = deterministic resource or schema claim; X = unlinked, contradicted,
or provenance-conflicted.

Three facts here are load-bearing. First, \textbf{no empirical headline
row in the P1--P8 ledger reaches Tier A or B} under the
statistical-rigor thresholds; the rectified P1--P8 tally is C = 12, R =
2, X = 4. P11 adds an eight-seed matched block but does not meet the
stated Tier A step threshold, as clarified below. Second, the four Tier
X rows are X for specific, checkable reasons: \textbf{P3-C2} rests on G
= 32 \emph{sampling} cells with zero optimizer steps, so no matched G =
4/G = 32 training replication exists; \textbf{P4-C1}'s abstract names
Qwen3-8B while every mapped run is \texttt{Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct};
\textbf{P5-C1} compares \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base} against
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B}, so the base checkpoint changes together with the
backend and the pair must not be cited as a backend-only causal effect;
and \textbf{P8-C3} is a proposed architecture with no execution, hence
zero linked runs. Third, some cross-provider model-identity conflicts
remain unresolved. Their private identity reconciliation is outside this
public package; the affected scientific model-attribution claims remain
contested.

Two column caveats travel with the table. The dataset cell for P7-C1 is
\texttt{—} because the ledger's only dataset-adjacent field for those
368 runs is a prompt/tokenizer family (\texttt{llama3},
\texttt{qwen3\_instruct}, \longtok{gpt\_\allowbreak{}oss\_\allowbreak{}low\_\allowbreak{}reasoning},
\longtok{nemotron3\_\allowbreak{}disable\_\allowbreak{}thinking}), not a dataset name. And the P3
sweep here is \textbf{not} the Semester-3 sweep of Table A.4: it is a
different project (\texttt{study\ logging\ group}), a different model
(Qwen3.5-4B), and it reports deltas of 0.0000 rather than held-out
accuracies near 0.99. The two share a group-size grid and nothing else.
Chapters 4 and 6 cite the Table A.4 sweep (Qwen2.5-0.5B) whenever they
give the 0.838 → 0.631 ZVF decline or the 0.98--0.99 held-out figures.
They do not cite P3-C1.

The extension ledger added in August 2026 carries five rows besides P11:
P10-C1 is Tier R (an algebraic identity, not an empirical result),
P10-C2 and P12-C2 are Tier X (placeholder figures and unlinked
prospective claims, zero runs), and P12-C1 is Tier R as a lemma and Tier
X as an empirical headline.

\textbf{P11} is an eight-seed, matched-stack block of 40 arm--seed units
at
\longtok{local:zvf-program/audit/results/full/\{grpo,dapo,gspo,drgrpo,aero\}-seed-\{11,23,37,53,71,89,107,131\}.json},
five arms × eight seeds on one stack (Qwen3-8B, GSM8K, 30 steps, Colab
A100, held-out n = 500). Its headline contrast, DAPO versus GRPO, is Δ =
+0.001, percentile-bootstrap CI {[}−0.0045, +0.00675{]} (paired-t 95\%
CI {[}−0.0063, +0.0083{]}, n = 8 seeds; the bootstrap undercovers at
this n), MDE₈₀ = 0.01012, all raw p \textgreater{} 0.21, with
Benjamini--Hochberg rejecting nothing. The mechanism is visible in the
diagnostic: DAPO's mean ZVF is 0.000 against GRPO's 0.693, bought with
3.61× the rollouts (1,734 versus 480) and 1.44× the wall clock. This
eight-paired-seed contrast supports a null. Every retained P11 final
manifest specifies \texttt{max\_steps=30}; the 40 units are 30-step
runs. Accordingly this block does \textbf{not} satisfy the stated Tier A
requirement of at least 100 steps per run and remains Tier C under the
vocabulary above. Pooling steps across arms or seeds does not satisfy
that per-run requirement. This corrects the evidence tier, not the
recorded rewards, seed counts, interval, or null result.

\subsection{Opening and null-result
runs}\label{opening-and-null-result-runs}

Chapter 6 reports four null families --- a mixed-variance curriculum, a
matched token budget, hard-prompt filtering, and step-1 layer selection
(§6.4.3) --- and each rests on source-specific scientific result records
rather than an account-wide inventory. The study The rows are counted
from the per-run \texttt{name} (or mode) fields of each artefact's JSON
--- \texttt{results.json} for the curriculum, token-budget, hard-prompt
and loss-formulation blocks, \texttt{result.json} and its siblings for
P1 --- not from an inventory API, which is why this cohort is reported
as its own total. Three neighbouring blocks add nothing to it: the P3
group-size artefacts are already indexed ---
\texttt{groupsize\_zvf/results.json} reproduces the per-group deltas of
Table A.5's P3-C1 row exactly, and the second sweep file is discussed
below --- and the P2, P7 and P8 ``openings''
(\longtok{p2\_openings/collapse\_\allowbreak{}baseline\_\allowbreak{}analysis.json},
\texttt{p7\_controller/sim.json}, \texttt{p8\_openings/*.json}) are
zero-compute re-analyses that launch no runs.

\textbf{Semester-4 opening and null-result runs (46 runs).} Model is
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.5-4B} and dataset GSM8K unless a row says otherwise.
All rows are Tinker-managed except the P1 layer-freeze block, which ran
on Colab for per-layer gradient access. ``Curriculum'' means training
only on non-collapsed (mixed-variance) groups. Group sizes marked
\emph{default} are the launch defaults of the scripts in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/openings/}; only the campaign
artefact records a group size per run. Δ is the change in held-out
accuracy.

Three provenance notes belong with this table, because two of its blocks
carry superseded siblings and one carries a refuted claim. First,
\texttt{campaign/FINDINGS.md} describes a nine-run, three-seed version
of the campaign on a twelve-prompt held-out set with arm means of +0.028
and −0.028; \texttt{campaign/results.json} holds fifteen runs over six
seeds on a thirty-prompt held-out set (0.8667 = 26/30), and the table
follows the per-run file. The FINDINGS warning that
\longtok{zero\_\allowbreak{}loss\_\allowbreak{}frac} was invalid (an empty-metrics-dict bug,
``since fixed'') belongs to the nine-run batch, not to that file.
Second, the P1 layer-freeze block has the same shape: the two-seed 3B
pass (overlap 0.1111 ± 0.0000) is superseded by the four-seed pass
(0.0833 ± 0.0481), and both sit near chance, so the toy-scale overlap of
1.0 must not be read as a transferable layer-selection signal. Third,
the two single-seed training blocks flagged SUSPECT by independent
verification (kimi, 2026-07-06) are superseded by the multi-seed blocks
above: the curriculum opening's +0.05 (campaign curriculum and baseline
arms separate by 0.006 over six seeds, at 3.3--7.2× oversample cost),
and a single-seed G = 4 gain of +0.125 in
\longtok{p3\_groupsize/sweep\_results.json} --- the campaign's G4 seed-0
baseline gains 0.0000, not 0.125, and that sweep file retains only two
of its twelve entries, so the W\&B group is its record.

\section{E1--E14 evaluation campaign
lanes}\label{e1e14-evaluation-campaign-lanes}

The fourteen evaluation lanes are not training runs; they are
evaluations of a single frozen actor, and they carry the registry's
strictest receipt discipline. The actor is base
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} (commit \texttt{995ad96e…}) with adapter
\texttt{campaign-adapter} (commit \texttt{withheld-adapter-revision…}),
bfloat16, trained on Tinker, run seed 809 taken from the adapter name.
The adapter was trained for 40 steps at G = 4 and learning rate 1 × 10⁻⁵
by REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline (no KL, no clipping), as
recorded in the local W\&B run directory for run {[}identifier
withheld{]} and the run receipt
\texttt{withheld\ actor-training\ receipt} (Chapter 8 §8.1.1). Those are
properties of the single actor rather than of any lane, and every lane
carries the same run seed (809), so algorithm, group size, seed and step
columns are omitted from Table A.8. Terminal states are from the ledger
of record, \texttt{withheld\ historical\ campaign\ chronology}; all
scores are from \longtok{outputs/E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md},
except the six rows marked ``finish 2026-09-27'', which are from
\longtok{outputs/finish\_\allowbreak{}pending\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-27/\textless{}lane\textgreater{}/result.json}.

retained rather than resolved into one, because the disagreement is
itself evidence: the historical snapshot records E7 as ``1/28 errored,
no grade'' against the terminal-state table's ``1/46 attempted'' ---
both real observations at different points in the lane's life. Likewise
E11's canonical figure is 129/312 = 41.35\%; a 129/311 sensitivity
figure survives in non-canonical receipts and is not used as the
headline.

\section{Completeness limitations}\label{completeness-limitations}

The historical registry is an index of its retained snapshot and is
incomplete as a scientific dataset. Many records lack model, dataset,
algorithm, group size, steps or a usable outcome. Missing fields remain
unknown; names and provider state are not used to infer them. The public
version does not include the account-wide field-coverage table or
private per-identity export.
